Infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equations for statistical inference on sparse graphs
Tomas Dominguez, Jean-Christophe Mourrat

Abstract
We study the well-posedness of an infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation posed on the set of non-negative measures and with a monotonic non-linearity. Our results will be used in a companion work to propose a conjecture and prove partial results concerning the asymptotic mutual information in the assortative stochastic block model in the sparse regime. The equation we consider is naturally stated in terms of the Gateaux derivative of the solution, unlike previous works in which the derivative is usually of transport type. We introduce an approximating family of finite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equations, and use the monotonicity of the non-linearity to show that no boundary condition needs to be prescribed to establish well-posedness. The solution to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation is then defined as the limit of these approximating solutions. In the special setting of a convex non-linearity, we also provide a Hopf-Lax variational representation of the solution.



1
Introduction and main results

A recent approach to describe the asymptotic free energy of a mean-field disordered system is as the solution to a Hamilton-Jacobi equation.
Spin-glass models have typically led to infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equations of transport type
[
27
,
29
,
30
]
while statistical inference problems have given rise to finite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equations defined on closed convex cones
[
7
,
8
,
10
,
11
,
26
,
28
]
. A general well-posedness theory for the former was established in
[
12
]
while one for the latter was developed in
[
13
]
. In
[
19
]
we will propose to describe the asymptotic mutual information in the sparse stochastic block model in terms of a Hamilton-Jacobi equation posed over a space of probability measures, but featuring derivatives of “affine” rather than transport type. The purpose of this paper is to develop a well-posedness theory for such an infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation; we expect that this type of equation will appear in other mean-field problems with sparse interactions. While our setting is different, the techniques we use here draw heavily upon the arguments introduced in
[
12
]
and
[
13
]
.

Let us describe the class of infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equations that we consider. We denote by
\mathcal{M}_{s}
the space of signed measures on
[-1,1]
,

\mathcal{M}_{s}=\big\{\mu\mid\mu\text{ is a signed measure on }[-1,1]\big\},

(1.1)

and by
\mathcal{M}_{+}
the cone of non-negative measures on this interval,

\mathcal{M}_{+}=\big\{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}\mid\mu\text{ is a non-negative measure}\big\}.

(1.2)

We follow the convention that a signed measure can only take finite values, and in particular, every
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
must have finite total mass.
We fix a continuously differentiable function
g:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
, and for each measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, define the function
G_{\mu}:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
by

G_{\mu}(x)=\int_{-1}^{1}g(xy)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y).

(1.3)

We introduce the cone of functions

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}=\big\{G_{\mu}\mid\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\big\}

(1.4)

as well as the non-linearity
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}:\mathcal{C}_{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}
defined on this cone by

\mathsf{C}_{\infty}(G_{\mu})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\mu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}g(xy)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

(1.5)

This non-linearity is well-defined by the Fubini-Tonelli theorem. Indeed, if
G_{\mu}=G_{\nu}
for some measures
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, then

\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\mu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\nu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}g(xy)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(x),

(1.6)

while

\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\nu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(x)=\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\mu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(x)=\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}g(xy)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(x),

(1.7)

and the symmetry of the map
(x,y)\mapsto g(xy)
implies that these two expressions coincide.
Given a function
f:[0,\infty)\times\mathcal{M}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
and measures
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, we denote by
D_{\mu}f(t,\mu;\nu)
the Gateaux derivative of the function
f(t,\cdot)
at the measure
\mu
in the direction
\nu
,

D_{\mu}f(t,\mu;\nu)=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{f(t,\mu+\epsilon\nu)-f(t,\mu)}{\epsilon}.

(1.8)

We will say that the Gateaux derivative of
f(t,\cdot)
at the measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
admits a density if there exists a bounded measurable function
x\mapsto D_{\mu}f(t,\mu,x)
defined on the interval
[-1,1]
with

D_{\mu}f(t,\mu;\nu)=\int_{-1}^{1}D_{\mu}f(t,\mu,x)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(x)

(1.9)

for every measure
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
. We will often abuse notation and identify the density
D_{\mu}f(t,\mu,\cdot)
with the Gateaux derivative
D_{\mu}f(t,\mu)
. The purpose of this paper is to establish the well-posedness of the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}f(t,\mu)&=\mathsf{C}_{\infty}\big(D_{\mu}f(t,\mu)\big)&\text{on }&\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathcal{M}_{+},\\
f(0,\mu)&=\psi(\mu)&\text{on }&\mathcal{M}_{+}.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.10)

under appropriate assumptions on the kernel
g
and the initial condition
\psi:\mathcal{M}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
. In particular, these assumptions will imply that, in a suitably weak sense, the Gateaux derivative of the solution
D_{\mu}f(t,\mu)
belongs to the cone
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
for all
t\geq 0
and
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
. Before stating these assumptions and the results they lead to precisely, let us describe the general strategy we will follow.

To obtain the well-posedness of the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.10
), we will project it from the infinite-dimensional space of measures
\smash{\mathcal{M}_{+}}
to a family of finite-dimensional spaces of measures
\smash{\mathcal{M}_{+}^{(K)}}
with dimension monotone in some integer parameter
K\geq 1
. The well-posedness of each of these projected equations will be obtained using techniques similar to those in
[
13
]
, and the limit as
K
tends to infinity of these projected solutions will be shown to exist using techniques similar to those in
[
12
,
27
,
29
]
; we take the limit thus obtained as the definition of the solution to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.10
).

In previous works, derivatives of transport type were the primary focus of investigation, and it was thus natural to discretize the space of measures by restricting to measures of the form
\smash{K^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\delta_{x_{k}}}
, only allowing the
x_{k}
’s to vary but keeping the weight of each atom fixed. Due to the nature of the derivatives appearing in (
1.10
), we choose instead here to define our finite-dimensional approximating space as the cone of non-negative measures supported on dyadic rationals in the interval
[-1,1]
. That is, we allow the weights to vary, provided that they remain non-negative, but keep the positions of the atoms fixed.
Given an integer
K\geq 1
, we write

\mathcal{D}_{K}=\Big\{k=\frac{i}{2^{K}}\mid-2^{K}\leq i<2^{K}\Big\}

(1.11)

for the set of dyadic rationals on
[-1,1]
at scale
K
. It will be convenient to index vectors using the set of dyadic rationals, writing

\smash{x=(x_{k})_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
.
We denote the set of discrete measures supported on the dyadic rationals at scale
K
in the interval
[-1,1]
by

\mathcal{M}^{(K)}_{+}=\Big\{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\mid\mu=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}x_{k}\delta_{k}\text{ for some }x=(x_{k})_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\Big\}.

(1.12)

A natural way to project a general measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
onto
\mathcal{M}_{+}^{(K)}
is via the mapping

x^{(K)}(\mu)=\big(\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert\mu\big[k,k+2^{-K}\big)\big)_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}.

(1.13)

For
\smash{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}^{(K)}}
, the image of
\mu
is simply the sequence of weights of the measure
\mu
at each point in
\mathcal{D}_{K}
, up to multiplication by
\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert
. The inverse of this mapping assigns to each
\smash{x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
the measure

\mu^{(K)}_{x}=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}x_{k}\delta_{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}^{(K)}.

(1.14)

We can use these projections to devise finite-dimensional approximations to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.10
). These will be posed on the cone
\smash{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
. Indeed, any real-valued function
\smash{f:[0,\infty)\times\mathcal{M}_{+}^{(K)}\to\mathbb{R}}
may be identified with the function

f^{(K)}(t,x)=f\big(t,\mu_{x}^{(K)}\big)

(1.15)

defined on
\smash{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
. Moreover, the Gateaux derivative at the measure
\smash{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}^{(K)}}
may be identified with the gradient
\smash{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert\nabla f^{(K)}(t,x^{(K)}(\mu))}
by duality. Indeed, for any direction
\smash{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}^{(K)}}
,

D_{\mu}f(t,\mu;\nu)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\epsilon}\Big|_{\epsilon=0}f^{(K)}(t,x^{(K)}(\mu)+\epsilon x^{(K)}(\nu))=\nabla f^{(K)}(t,x^{(K)}(\mu))\cdot x^{(K)}(\nu).

(1.16)

The additional factor of
\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert
appears because
\smash{x^{(K)}(\nu)}
has
\ell^{1}
-norm
\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert
whenever
\nu
is a probability measure. The corresponding initial condition becomes the function
\smash{\psi^{(K)}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\to\mathbb{R}}
defined by

\psi^{(K)}(x)=\psi\big(\mu_{x}^{(K)}\big).

(1.17)

The cone (
1.4
) and the non-linearity (
1.5
) may be projected in a similar manner. We introduce the symmetric matrix

G^{(K)}=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert^{2}}\big(g(kk^{\prime})\big)_{k,k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}\times\mathcal{D}_{K}},

(1.18)

and observe that for every
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}^{(K)}
and
k\in\mathcal{D}_{K}
,

G_{\mu}(k)=\sum_{k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}g(kk^{\prime})\mu(k^{\prime})=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}\sum_{k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}g(kk^{\prime})x^{(K)}(\mu)_{k^{\prime}}=\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert\big(G^{(K)}x^{(K)}(\mu)\big)_{k}.

(1.19)

This motivates the definition of the projected cone,

\mathcal{C}_{K}=\Big\{G^{(K)}x^{(K)}(\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\mid\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}^{(K)}\Big\}=\Big\{G^{(K)}x\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\mid x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\Big\},

(1.20)

and the projected non-linearity
\mathsf{C}_{K}:\mathcal{C}_{K}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\mathsf{C}_{K}\big(G^{(K)}x\big)=\frac{1}{2}G^{(K)}x\cdot x=\frac{1}{2\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert^{2}}\sum_{k,k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}g(kk^{\prime})x_{k}x_{k^{\prime}}=\mathsf{C}_{\infty}\big(G_{\mu_{x}^{(K)}}\big).

(1.21)

This projected non-linearity can be shown to be well-defined using the same argument that showed the non-linearity (
1.5
) was well-defined. With this notation, our finite-dimensional approximation of the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.10
) reads

\partial_{t}f^{(K)}(t,x)=\mathsf{C}_{K}\big(\nabla f^{(K)}(t,x)\big)\quad\text{on}\quad\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}

(1.22)

subject to the initial condition
f^{(K)}(0,x)=\psi^{(K)}(x)
on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}
. To study this equation we will introduce an appropriate extension
\smash{\mathsf{H}_{K}:\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\to\mathbb{R}}
of the non-linearity
\smash{\mathsf{C}_{K}}
, and instead consider the Hamilton-Jacobi equation

\partial_{t}f^{(K)}(t,x)=\mathsf{H}_{K}\big(\nabla f^{(K)}(t,x)\big)\quad\text{on}\quad\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}

(1.23)

subject to the initial condition
f^{(K)}(0,x)=\psi^{(K)}(x)
on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}
. Notice that we have used the cone
\smash{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
as opposed to the more intuitive cone
\smash{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
. As detailed below, the monotonicity of the projected non-linearity (
1.21
) and the ideas regarding boundary conditions of Hamilton-Jacobi equations with suitable non-linearities developed in
[
13
,
18
,
31
]
make these two choices equivalent. In particular, it will not be necessary to endow the projected Hamilton-Jacobi equation with a boundary condition. Remembering that this Hamilton-Jacobi equation appears in the context of statistical inference makes this insight rather reassuring. Indeed, the statistical inference model does not suggest an obvious choice of boundary condition—given that we are ultimately interested in the identification of the value of the solution at a point in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\{0\}
, we would at least not want to use a Dirichlet boundary condition there! In earlier works, the imposition of a Neumann-type boundary condition was observed to be a workable option
[
27
,
28
,
29
]
. In
[
13
]
, it was shown that this somewhat artificial choice is not necessary, and no boundary condition needs to be specified, because the non-linearity “points in the right direction”.

We now state the precise assumptions that will allow us to obtain the well-posedness of the projected Hamilton-Jacobi equations and establish the convergence of their solutions. In the same spirit as
[
12
,
13
,
27
,
29
]
, we will need the initial conditions
\smash{\psi^{(K)}}
and
\psi
to satisfy a certain number of Lipschitz continuity assumptions. Given an integer
d\geq 1
, we introduce the normalized-
\smash{\ell^{1}}
and normalized-
\smash{\ell^{1,*}}
norms, defined for every
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\lvert x_{k}\rvert\quad\text{and}\quad{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}=\max_{k\leq d}d\lvert y_{k}\rvert.

(1.24)

The underlying dimension
d\geq 1
will be kept implicit but will always be clear from the context. The normalized-
\smash{\ell^{1}}
norm is meant to measure elements of
\smash{\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{k}}}
with a scaling that is consistent with our identification of this space with the space of measures
\smash{\mathcal{M}^{(K)}_{+}}
. The normalized-
\smash{\ell^{1,*}}
norm serves to measure elements of the dual space, and is defined so that the Hölder-type inequality
x\cdot y\leq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\,{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}
is valid.

The key continuity assumption on the projected initial condition
\smash{\psi^{(K)}}
that will make it possible to establish the well-posedness of the projected Hamilton-Jacobi equations will be Lipschitz continuity with respect to the normalized-
\smash{\ell^{1}}
norm. Another way to encode this property is to require the initial condition
\psi:\mathcal{M}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
to be Lipschitz continuous with respect to the total variation distance on
\mathcal{M}_{+}
,

\mathrm{TV}(\mu,\nu)=\sup\big\{\lvert\mu(A)-\nu(A)\rvert\mid A\text{ is a measurable subset of }[-1,1]\big\}.

(1.25)

The normalized-
\ell^{1,*}
norm will play its part when discussing the Lipschitz continuity of the projected non-linearity (
1.21
). To determine the convergence of the projected solutions, it will be important to assume that the initial condition
\psi:\mathcal{M}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
is Lipschitz continuous with respect to the Wasserstein distance on the set of probability measures
\Pr[-1,1]
,

W(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})=\sup\bigg\{\Big\lvert\int_{-1}^{1}h(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(x)-\int_{-1}^{1}h(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}(x)\Big\rvert\mid\lVert h\rVert_{\text{Lip}}\leq 1\bigg\}.

(1.26)

Here
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip}}
denotes the Lipschitz semi-norm

\lVert h\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip}}=\sup_{x\neq x^{\prime}\in[-1,1]}\frac{\lvert h(x)-h(x^{\prime})\rvert}{\lvert x-x^{\prime}\rvert}

(1.27)

defined on the space of functions
h:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
. The final assumption on the initial condition will ensure that, in a sense to be made precise, the solution to the projected Hamilton-Jacobi equation has a bounded gradient close to the projected cone
\mathcal{C}_{K}
defined in (
1.20
). It would of course be more convenient to assume that the gradient really belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{K}
, rather than only being close to it, but unlike in earlier works, this stronger property does not hold in the context of the main application we have in mind in
[
19
]
. To impose the boundedness of the gradient, fix
a>0
, and for each integer
K\geq 1
introduce the closed convex set

\mathcal{K}_{a,K}=\Big\{G^{(K)}x\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\mid x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\text{ and }{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq a\Big\}\subset\mathcal{C}_{K}.

(1.28)

Given a closed convex set
\smash{\mathcal{K}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, write

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}=\mathcal{K}+B_{d^{-1/2}}(0)

(1.29)

for the neighborhood of radius
\smash{d^{-1/2}}
around
\mathcal{K}
in the normalized-
\ell^{1,*}
norm. Here

B_{r}(x)=\big\{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x^{\prime}-x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq r\big\}

(1.30)

denotes the closed ball of radius
r>0
centered around
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
relative to the normalized-
\ell^{1,*}
norm. We will say that a Lipschitz continuous function
\smash{h:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}}
has its gradient in
\mathcal{K}
if

\nabla h\in L^{\infty}\big(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d};\mathcal{K}\big).

(1.31)

Recall that a Lipschitz continuous function is differentiable almost everywhere by Rademacher’s theorem (see Theorem 6 in Chapter 5.8 of
[
20
]
), so the spatial gradient
\nabla h
is well-defined as an element of
L^{\infty}
, and the condition (
1.31
) requires that this object take values in
\mathcal{K}
almost everywhere. A non-differential criterion for the gradient of a Lipschitz continuous function to lie in a closed convex set is given in
Proposition

B.2
, and will be used frequently throughout the paper. As will be shown below, assuming that the initial condition has its gradient in
\smash{\mathcal{K}_{a,K}^{\prime}}
suffices to ensure that the gradient of the solution remains in this set at all times. Notice that this is insufficient to be able to evaluate the non-linearity
\smash{\mathsf{C}_{K}}
at the gradient of the solution; however, under suitable Lipschitz continuity properties of the extension
\mathsf{H}_{K}
, it ensures that the projected Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.23
) should be an adequate replacement for the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.22
). In particular, it justifies defining the solution to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi (
1.10
) as the limit of the solutions to the projected Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.23
).
Besides some smoothness, the only constraint we will impose on the kernel
g:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
is that it be strictly positive. Among other things, this assumption ensures that a non-negative measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
cannot have a large total mass unless the function
G_{\mu}
takes large values. In summary, the assumptions on the kernel
g:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
and the initial condition
\psi:\mathcal{M}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
required for the validity of our main results are the following.

H1

The kernel
g:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
is continuously differentiable and bounded away from zero by some positive constant
m>0
,

g(x)\geq m.

(1.32)

H2

The initial condition
\psi:\mathcal{M}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
is Lipschitz continuous with respect to the total variation distance (
1.25
),

\lvert\psi(\mu)-\psi(\nu)\rvert\leq\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}\mathrm{TV}(\mu,\nu)

(1.33)

for all measures
\nu,\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
.

H3

There exists
a>0
such that the initial condition
\psi:\mathcal{M}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
has the property that each of the projected initial conditions (
1.17
) has its gradient in the set
\smash{\mathcal{K}_{a,K}^{\prime}}
,

\nabla\psi^{(K)}\in L^{\infty}\big(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d};\mathcal{K}_{a,K}^{\prime}\big).

(1.34)

H4

The initial condition
\psi:\Pr[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
is Lipschitz continuous with respect to the Wasserstein distance (
1.26
),

\lvert\psi(\mathbb{P})-\psi(\mathbb{Q})\rvert\leq\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},W}W(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})

(1.35)

for all probability measures
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}\in\Pr[-1,1]
.

Observe that the hypothesis (
H2
) on the initial condition implies that the projected initial conditions (
1.17
) are Lipschitz continuous with respect to the normalized-
\smash{\ell^{1}}
norm,

\big\lvert\psi^{(K)}(x)-\psi^{(K)}(x^{\prime})\big\rvert\leq\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}\mathrm{TV}\big(\mu^{(K)}_{x},\mu^{(K)}_{x^{\prime}}\big)\leq\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}.

(1.36)

With these assumptions at hand, it is natural to wonder why we cannot simply invoke the main result in
[
13
]
to obtain the well-posedness of the projected Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.23
). The setting proposed in
[
13
]
is that of a Hamilton-Jacobi equation posed on a cone
\mathcal{C}
and with a non-linearity that is defined over the cone
\mathcal{C}
as well; the key assumption to establish well-posedness is that the non-linearity and the initial condition have their gradients in the cone
\mathcal{C}
. In our context, the non-linearity is initially only well-defined on the cone
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
, or
\mathcal{C}_{K}
for the projected equations, and we must make sure that the gradient of the solution remains in this space. This suggests that we try to use the results in
[
13
]
with
\smash{\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}
, or
\smash{\mathcal{C}_{K}}
for the projected equations. However, our problem, say for the projected equations, is naturally posed over
\smash{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
rather than
\mathcal{C}_{K}
, and moreover, the gradient of the non-linearity that appears in our setting is not in
\mathcal{C}_{K}
, although it is in
\smash{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
.
To make matters more complicated, the gradient of the finite-dimensional initial condition, and therefore also of the solution, does not quite belong to
\mathcal{C}_{K}
, although it is in the closed convex set
\smash{\mathcal{K}_{a,K}^{\prime}}
. Despite all this, we will show that the somewhat richer geometry of our problem can be dealt with using arguments that are similar to those in
[
13
]
.

We now describe the structure of these arguments in more detail, and state our main results. We will first show that for any
R>0
, it is possible to define a non-linearity
\smash{\mathsf{H}_{K,R}:\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\to\mathbb{R}}
which agrees with the projected non-linearity
\mathsf{C}_{K}
on a large enough ball
\smash{\mathcal{C}_{K}\cap B_{R}(0)}
, and is uniformly Lipschitz continuous. We will then obtain the well-posedness of the projected Hamilton-Jacobi equation

\partial_{t}f^{(K)}(t,x)=\mathsf{H}_{K,R}\big(\nabla f^{(K)}(t,x)\big)\quad\text{on}\quad\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}

(1.37)

subject to the initial condition
\smash{f^{(K)}(0,x)=\psi^{(K)}(x)}
on
\smash{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
.
Finally, we will show that the solutions to these projected Hamilton-Jacobi equations admit a limit as
K
tends to infinity. We will verify that this limit does not depend on the choice of the extension
\mathsf{H}_{K,R}
, provided that
R
is chosen sufficiently large, and define it to be the solution to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.10
).

To state our main well-posedness results, we introduce additional notation.
Given functions
h:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\smash{u:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}}
, we define the semi-norms

{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|h|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}=\sup_{x\neq x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\frac{\lvert h(x)-h(x^{\prime})\rvert}{{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}}\quad\text{and}\quad[u]_{0}=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}t>0\\
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\end{subarray}}\frac{\lvert u(t,x)-u(0,x)\rvert}{t},

(1.38)

and introduce the space of functions with Lipschitz initial condition that grow at most linearly in time,

\mathfrak{L}=\big\{u:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\mid u(0,\cdot)\text{ is Lipschitz continuous and }[u]_{0}<\infty\big\},

(1.39)

as well as its subset of uniformly Lipschitz continuous functions,

\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{unif}}=\Big\{u\in\mathfrak{L}\mid\sup_{t\geq 0}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|u(t,\cdot)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}<\infty\Big\}.

(1.40)

The main well-posedness results for the projected Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.37
) and the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.10
) now read as follows.

Theorem 1.1
.

Under assumptions (
1.32
)-(
1.34
), the projected Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.37
) with
\smash{R>0}
admits a unique viscosity solution
\smash{f^{(K)}_{R}\in\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{unif}}}
subject to the initial condition
\smash{\psi^{(K)}}
. Moreover,
\smash{f^{(K)}_{R}}
has its gradient in the set
\smash{\mathcal{K}_{a,K}^{\prime}}
and satisfies the Lipschitz bound

\sup_{t>0}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|f_{R}^{(K)}(t,\cdot)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi^{(K)}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}\leq\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}.

(1.41)

Theorem 1.2
.

Suppose (
1.32
)-(
H4
), and given an integer
K\geq 1
and a real number
\smash{R>\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}}
, denote by
\smash{f_{R}^{(K)}\in\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{unif}}}
the unique viscosity solution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.37
) constructed in
Theorem

1.1
. For every
t\geq 0
and every measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, the limit

f(t,\mu)=\lim_{K\to\infty}f_{R}^{(K)}(t,x^{(K)}(\mu))

(1.42)

exists, is finite and is independent of
R
. The value of this limit is defined to be the solution to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.10
).

Solutions to (
1.10
) satisfy a comparison principle, since a comparison principle also holds for solutions to the projected Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.37
), by Corollary
A.2
.

As is apparent, and similarly to
[
27
,
29
]
, we content ourselves here with identifying the solution to (
1.10
) as the limit of our finite-dimensional approximations. This will suffice for our purposes, and we leave open the question of providing a more intrinsic characterization of the solution to (
1.10
), as was achieved in
[
12
]
in a related context.

In addition to these well-posedness results, we also obtain a Hopf-Lax variational representation for the solution to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.10
) in the case when the non-linearity
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
is convex. Hopf-Lax formulas for related problems have been explored in
[
9
,
10
,
12
,
13
]
. In
[
19
]
, this variational representation will allow us to verify that, in the disassortative regime, our conjectured asymptotic mutual information for the sparse stochastic block model coincides with the value of the asymptotic mutual information established in
[
14
]
. The convexity condition on
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
boils down to the requirement that the mapping
(x,y)\mapsto g(xy)
be non-negative definite, and can be phrased as follows.

H5

The kernel
g:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies the property

\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}g(xy)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\geq 0

(1.43)

for every signed measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}
.

Theorem 1.3
.

If (
1.32
)-(
H5
) hold, then the unique solution
\smash{f:[0,\infty)\times\mathcal{M}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}}
to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.10
) constructed in
Theorem

1.2
admits the Hopf-Lax variational representation

f(t,\mu)=\sup_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}}\bigg\{\psi(\mu+t\nu)-\frac{t}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\nu}(y)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(y)\bigg\}

(1.44)

for every
t>0
and
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
. Moreover, the supremum in (
1.44
) is achieved at some
\nu^{*}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, and whenever the initial condition
\psi
admits a Gateaux derivative at the measure
\mu+t\nu^{*}
with a density
x\mapsto D_{\mu}\psi(\mu+t\nu^{*},x)
belonging to the cone
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
, we have

G_{\nu^{*}}=D_{\mu}\psi(\mu+t\nu^{*},\cdot).

(1.45)

For the purposes of our companion work
[
19
]
, it will also be important to identify solutions to equations of the form (
1.10
) with a kernel
g
that does not satisfy the positivity assumption (
1.32
). The idea will be to introduce a new kernel which satisfies (
1.32
) by translating
g
, and to deduce the well-posedness of the equation with kernel
g
from the well-posedness of the equation with the translated kernel. For this strategy to work, we will replace the assumption (
1.34
) on the initial condition by a stronger assumption which we now describe.

For every
a\in\mathbb{R}
introduce the set of measures with mass
a
,

\mathcal{M}_{a,+}=\big\{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\mid\mu[-1,1]=a\big\},

(1.46)

as well as the set of functions

\mathcal{C}_{a,\infty}=\big\{G_{\mu}\mid\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{a,+}\big\}.

(1.47)

The assumption (
1.34
) on the initial condition will essentially be replaced by the assumption that its Gateaux derivative lies in the set
\smash{\mathcal{C}_{a,\infty}}
for some
a\in\mathbb{R}
. As before, it will be convenient to state this as an assumption on the projected initial conditions (
1.17
). For every integer
K\geq 1
introduce the set of projected measures with mass
a
,

\mathcal{M}_{a,+}^{(K)}=\bigg\{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}^{(K)}\mid\mu[1,1]=a\bigg\},

(1.48)

and write

\mathcal{K}_{=a,K}=\Big\{G^{(K)}x\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\mid x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\text{ and }{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}=a\Big\}

(1.49)

for its associated set of functions. We replace (
1.34
) by the following stronger assumption.

H3’

There exists
a>0
such that the initial condition
\smash{\psi:\mathcal{M}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}}
has the property that each of the projected initial conditions (
1.17
) has its gradient in the set
\mathcal{K}_{=a,K}^{\prime}
,

\nabla\psi^{(K)}\in L^{\infty}\big(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d};\mathcal{K}_{=a,K}^{\prime}\big).

(1.50)

Formal calculations now suggest a way to modify the solution to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.10
) if the kernel
g
is not assumed to satisfy (
1.32
) but is translated by a large enough constant so that it becomes positive. Given a continuously differentiable kernel
g:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
, fix
b\in\mathbb{R}
such that the modified kernel

\widetilde{g}_{b}(z)=g(z)+b

(1.51)

is strictly positive. For every
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
define the modified function
\smash{\widetilde{G}_{b,\mu}}:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
,

\widetilde{G}_{b,\mu}(x)=\int_{-1}^{1}\widetilde{g}_{b}(xy)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y),

(1.52)

the modified cone of functions,

\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b,\infty}=\big\{\widetilde{G}_{b,\mu}\mid\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\big\},

(1.53)

and the modified non-linearity
\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{b,\infty}:\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b,\infty}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{b,\infty}\big(\widetilde{G}_{b,\mu}\big)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\widetilde{G}_{b,\mu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}\widetilde{g}_{b}(xy)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

(1.54)

Notice that the additional constant
b
in
\widetilde{g}_{b}
induces a shift in the expression above that depends only on the total mass of the measure
\mu
. This suggests that, under assumption (
H3’
), if
\widetilde{f}_{b}
is a solution to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\widetilde{f}(t,\mu)&=\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{b,\infty}\big(D_{\mu}\widetilde{f}(t,\mu)\big)&\text{on }&\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathcal{M}_{+},\\
\widetilde{f}(0,\mu)&=\widetilde{\psi}_{b}(\mu)&\text{on }&\mathcal{M}_{+},\end{aligned}\right.

(1.55)

for the initial condition
\widetilde{\psi}_{b}:\mathcal{M}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\widetilde{\psi}_{b}(\mu)=\psi(\mu)+ab\int_{-1}^{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu,

(1.56)

then the function

f_{b}(t,\mu)=\widetilde{f}_{b}(t,\mu)-ab\int_{-1}^{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu-\frac{a^{2}bt}{2}

(1.57)

should be a solution to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.10
). We omit the dependence of
\smash{\widetilde{f}_{b}}
,
\smash{f_{b}}
and
\smash{\widetilde{\psi}_{b}}
on
a
since this constant is given to us and fixed by (
H3’
). The following result renders this construction precise and ensures that it is independent of the choice of
b
.

Theorem 1.4
.

Fix a continuously differentiable kernel
g:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
and assume that (
H2
), (
H3’
), and (
H4
) hold. Let
b\in\mathbb{R}
be such that the function
\smash{\widetilde{g}_{b}}
defined in (
1.51
) is positive on
[-1,1]
, let
\smash{\widetilde{\psi}_{b}}
be defined by (
1.56
), and let
\smash{\widetilde{f}_{b}}
be the solution to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.55
) constructed in
Theorem

1.2
. The function
\smash{f_{b}}
given by (
1.57
) does not depend on the choice of
b\in\mathbb{R}
, and it is defined to be the solution to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.10
).

Combining this well-posedness result with the Hopf-Lax representation formula in
Theorem

1.3
shows that under the additional assumption (
H5
), the function (
1.57
) admits a variational representation.

Theorem 1.5
.

Fix a continuously differentiable kernel
g:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying (
H2
), (
H3’
) and (
H4
). Suppose that there exists
b\in\mathbb{R}
such that the translated kernel
\widetilde{g}_{b}
in (
1.51
) is strictly positive on
[-1,1]
and satisfies (
H5
). Suppose moreover that for every
\smash{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}}
, the initial condition
\psi
admits a Gateaux derivative with density
\smash{x\mapsto D_{\mu}\psi(\mu,x)}
belonging to the set
\smash{\mathcal{C}_{a,\infty}}
. Then, the unique solution
\smash{f:[0,\infty)\times\mathcal{M}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}}
to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.10
) constructed in
Theorem

1.4
admits the Hopf-Lax variational representation

f(t,\mu)=\sup_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{a,+}}\bigg\{\psi(\mu+t\nu)-\frac{t}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\nu}(y)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(y)\bigg\}

(1.58)

for every
t>0
and
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
. Moreover, the supremum in (
1.58
) is achieved at some
\nu^{*}\in\mathcal{M}_{a,+}
with

G_{\nu^{*}}=D_{\mu}\psi(\mu+t\nu^{*},\cdot).

(1.59)

We briefly review related works on Hamilton-Jacobi equations in infinite dimensions. The study of equations posed on infinite-dimensional Banach spaces was initiated in
[
15
,
16
,
17
]
. The assumptions imposed on the Banach space preclude the possibility to apply the results presented there to the space of bounded measures. The existence of solutions is obtained via a connection with differential games. An example is also given in which solutions to natural finite-dimensional approximations fail to converge to the solution of the infinite-dimensional equation. We do not expect this phenomenon to occur for the problem we consider in this paper, and in any case, our definition of the solution as the limit of finite-dimensional approximations is the one we make use of in our companion work
[
19
]
. Moreover, for the equations of transport type appearing in the context of mean-field spin glasses, it was shown in
[
12
]
that finite-dimensional approximations do converge to the intrinsic viscosity solution of the infinite-dimensional equation.

Equations that are posed over a space of probability measures, or more general metric spaces, have been considered in a number of works including
[
1
,
4
,
5
,
6
,
21
,
22
,
23
,
24
]
. These works revolve around equations involving derivatives of transport type for probability measures over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Since transportation of mass over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
can be carried without limit, questions of boundary conditions do not arise there, unlike in the more recent works
[
12
,
27
,
29
]
already cited above in which probability measures over
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
or the space of non-negative definite matrices are considered. We are not aware of previous works considering equations that involve derivatives of “affine” type, as we do here. In this context, the natural “movements” are different from those appearing for the transport geometry, and our additional constraint that we must deal with non-negative measures is the source of the necessity to address boundary issues.

We close this section with a brief outline of the paper. In
Section

2
, we introduce a non-decreasing and uniformly Lipschitz continuous non-linearity
\smash{\mathsf{H}_{K,R}}
which agrees with the projected non-linearity (
1.21
) on the intersection between the projected cone (
1.20
) and a large enough ball, and we define the appropriate notion of solution to the projected Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.37
). The definition of the function
\smash{\mathsf{H}_{K,R}}
is inspired by Proposition 6.8 in
[
27
]
and Lemma 2.5 in
[
13
]
. We then leverage the well-posedness results established in
Appendix

A
to prove
Theorem

1.1
. In Section
3
, with the well-posedness of the projected Hamilton-Jacobi equations (
1.37
) at hand, we modify the arguments in Section 3.2 of
[
29
]
and Section 3.3 of
[
12
]
to obtain the convergence of solutions as described in
Theorem

1.2
. In
Section

4
we proceed as in Section 6 of
[
13
]
to obtain an approximate Hopf-Lax variational representation for the solution to the projected Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.37
). By taking an appropriate limit in this variational formula,
Theorem

1.3
is established in
Section

5
.
Section

6
is devoted to the proof of
Theorem

1.4
and
Theorem

1.5
. So as to not disrupt the flow of the paper, the main technical arguments required to establish the well-posedness of the projected Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.37
) have been postponed to
Appendix

A
. All the results in this appendix appear in
[
13
]
in some form but have been reproduced here for the reader’s convenience. It is also worth pointing out that we treat a slightly different setting to the one in
[
13
]
. Indeed, our initial condition has its gradient close to a convex set and not in it, and our non-linearity is monotonic with respect to a different convex set, as discussed below (
1.36
).
Appendix

B
reviews a fundamental duality theorem from convex analysis, establishes a non-differential criterion for a Lipschitz function to have its gradient in a closed convex set and provides a refresher on the basic properties of semi-continuous functions, which play a role in
Appendix

A
when running Perron’s argument for the existence of solutions.

Acknowledgements
.

We would like to warmly thank Hong-Bin Chen and Jiaming Xia for sharing a preliminary version of
[
13
]
with us. Their work considerably simplified our task, allowing us in particular to discard the much more complicated approach we had originally envisaged.

2
Construction of finite-dimensional approximations

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.1
, and in particular establish the well-posedness of the projected Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.37
). Throughout the paper, we say that a function
\smash{h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}}
is non-decreasing if for every
\smash{x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
with
\smash{x^{\prime}-x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
, we have
h(x^{\prime})-h(x)\geq 0
. More generally, given a closed convex cone
\smash{\mathcal{C}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, and denoting its dual cone by
\mathcal{C}^{*}
(see (
B.1
) for the definition), we say that a function
\smash{h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}}
is
\smash{\mathcal{C}^{*}}
-non-decreasing if for every
\smash{x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
with
x^{\prime}-x\in\mathcal{C}^{*}
, we have
h(x^{\prime})-h(x)\geq 0
. In the case when
h
is Lipschitz continuous, this is equivalent to the requirement that
h
have its gradient in
\mathcal{C}
(see
Proposition

B.2
). To alleviate notation and strive for generality, fix an integer dimension
d\geq 1
and a symmetric matrix
G\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
for which there exist positive constants
m,M>0
with

\frac{m}{d^{2}}\leq G_{kk^{\prime}}\leq\frac{M}{d^{2}}

(2.1)

for all
1\leq k,k^{\prime}\leq d
. Consider the cone

\mathcal{C}=\big\{Gx\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\big\},

(2.2)

the closed convex set

\mathcal{K}_{a}=\big\{Gx\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\text{ and }{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq a\big\},

(2.3)

and the non-linearity
\mathsf{C}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\mathsf{C}(Gx)=\frac{1}{2}Gx\cdot x.

(2.4)

This mapping is well-defined, for the same reason as that explained below (
1.5
). Recall the definition of the enlarged set (
1.29
) and of the normalized-
\smash{\ell^{1}}
and normalized-
\smash{\ell^{1,*}}
norms in (
1.24
). The first important result of this section will be the definition of a uniformly Lipschitz continuous and non-decreasing non-linearity
\smash{\mathsf{H}_{R}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}}
which agrees with
\mathsf{C}
on the intersection of the cone
\mathcal{C}
and a large enough ball. We will then obtain the well-posedness of the Hamilton-Jacobi equation associated with this non-linearity,

\partial_{t}f(t,x)=\mathsf{H}_{R}\big(\nabla f(t,x)\big)\quad\text{on}\quad\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d},

(2.5)

subject to a Lipschitz continuous initial condition
\psi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
with

\lvert\psi(x)-\psi(y)\rvert\leq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\nabla\psi\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d};\mathcal{K}_{a}^{\prime}).

(2.6)

By (
1.34
) and (
1.36
), the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
2.5
) corresponds to the projected Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.37
) for the choices
d=\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert
,
G=G^{(K)}
and
\psi=\psi^{(K)}
.
Theorem

1.1
will therefore be an immediate consequence of the main well-posedness result of this section.

To establish the well-posedness of the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
2.5
) using the results in
Appendix

A
, it will be important that the extended non-linearity
\smash{\mathsf{H}_{R}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}}
be Lipschitz continuous and non-decreasing. Let us start by verifying that these properties are satisfied locally on the cone by the original non-linearity (
2.4
). It will be convenient to note that

{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq\frac{1}{m}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|Gx|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}

(2.7)

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
.

Lemma 2.1
.

The non-linearity (
2.4
) is locally Lipschitz continuous with respect to the normalized-
\smash{\ell^{1,*}}
norm,

\lvert\mathsf{C}(y)-\mathsf{C}(y^{\prime})\rvert\leq\frac{1}{m}\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\big){|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y-y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}

(2.8)

for all
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

Fix
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
with
y=Gx
and
y^{\prime}=Gx^{\prime}
for some
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
. The symmetry of
G
and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality imply that

\lvert\mathsf{C}(y)-\mathsf{C}(y^{\prime})\rvert\leq\lvert G(x-x^{\prime})\cdot x\rvert+\lvert G(x-x^{\prime})\cdot x^{\prime}\rvert\leq\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\big){|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y-y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}.

It follows by (
2.7
) that

\lvert\mathsf{C}(y)-\mathsf{C}(y^{\prime})\rvert\leq\frac{1}{m}\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\big){|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y-y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.2
.

The non-linearity (
2.4
) is non-decreasing.

Proof.

Fix
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
with
y\leq y^{\prime}
(by this we mean that
y^{\prime}-y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
), and let
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
be such that
y=Gx
and
y^{\prime}=Gx^{\prime}
. Observe that

2\mathsf{C}(y)=Gx\cdot x=y\cdot x\leq y^{\prime}\cdot x=Gx^{\prime}\cdot x=Gx\cdot x^{\prime}=x^{\prime}\cdot y\leq x^{\prime}\cdot y^{\prime}=Gx^{\prime}\cdot x^{\prime}=2\mathsf{C}(y^{\prime}).

This completes the proof.
∎

Extending the non-linearity (
2.4
) to
\smash{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
while preserving these two key properties requires some care. For each
R>0
, we will define a non-decreasing function
\mathsf{H}_{R}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
which is uniformly Lipschitz continuous with respect to the normalized-
\smash{\ell^{1,*}}
norm and agrees with the non-linearity (
2.4
) on the intersection of the cone (
2.2
) and the ball
\smash{B_{R}=B_{R}(0)}
defined in (
1.30
). The definition of this extension is inspired by Proposition 6.8 in
[
27
]
and Lemma 2.5 in
[
13
]
.

Proposition 2.3
.

For every
R>0
, there exists a non-decreasing non-linearity
\mathsf{H}_{R}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
which agrees with
\mathsf{C}
on
\mathcal{C}\cap B_{R}
and satisfies the Lipschitz continuity property

\lvert\mathsf{H}_{R}(y)-\mathsf{H}_{R}(y^{\prime})\rvert\leq\frac{8RM}{m^{2}}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y-y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}

(2.9)

for all
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proof.

The proof proceeds in two steps: first we regularize
\mathsf{C}
by defining a non-decreasing and uniformly Lipschitz continuous function which agrees with
\mathsf{C}
on
\smash{\mathcal{C}\cap B_{R}}
, and then we extend this regularization to
\smash{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
.

Step 1: regularizing
\mathsf{C}
.

By
Lemma

2.1
, the non-linearity (
2.4
) satisfies the Lipschitz bound

\lvert\mathsf{C}(y)-\mathsf{C}(y^{\prime})\rvert\leq\frac{4R}{m}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y-y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}

for all
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2R}
. With this in mind, let
L=\frac{4R}{m}
, and define the regularized non-linearity
\smash{\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbb{R}}
by

\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(y)=\begin{cases}\max\Big(\mathsf{C}(y),\mathsf{C}(0)+2L\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}-R\big)\Big)&\text{if }y\in\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2R},\\
\mathsf{C}(0)+2L\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}-R\big)&\text{if }y\in\mathcal{C}\setminus B_{2R}.\end{cases}

To see that
\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}
agrees with
\mathsf{C}
on
\mathcal{C}\cap B_{R}
, observe that for any
y\in\mathcal{C}\cap B_{R}
,

\mathsf{C}(0)+2L\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}-R\big)\leq\mathsf{C}(0)=0\leq\mathsf{C}(y),

where the last inequality uses the non-negativity of the components of
G
. It will also be convenient to note that by Lipschitz continuity of
\mathsf{C}
on
\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2R}
,

\mathsf{C}(0)+2L\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}-R\big)=\mathsf{C}(0)+2LR=\mathsf{C}(0)+L{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\geq\mathsf{C}(y)

for any
\smash{y\in\mathcal{C}\cap\partial B_{2R}}
. This shows that
\smash{\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}}
is continuous.
To establish the non-decreasingness of
\smash{\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}}
, fix
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
with
y\leq y^{\prime}
. If
\smash{y,y^{\prime}\in B_{2R}}
, then the non-decreasingness of
\mathsf{C}
in
Lemma

2.2
implies that
\mathsf{C}(y)\leq\mathsf{C}(y^{\prime})
. Combining this with the fact that
\smash{{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}}
reveals that
\smash{\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(y)\leq\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(y^{\prime})}
. On the other hand, if
\smash{y\in B_{2R}}
and
\smash{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}\setminus B_{2R}}
, then

\mathsf{C}(y)\leq\mathsf{C}(0)+L{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq\mathsf{C}(0)+L{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}+L\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}-2R\big)=\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(y^{\prime})

and

\mathsf{C}(0)+2L\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}-R\big)\leq\mathsf{C}(0)+2L\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}-R\big)=\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(y^{\prime}).

Once again
\smash{\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(y)\leq\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(y^{\prime})}
. Finally, if
\smash{y\in\mathcal{C}\setminus B_{2R}}
, then
\smash{2R\leq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}}
so
\smash{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}\setminus B_{2R}}
and clearly
\smash{\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(y)\leq\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(y^{\prime})}
. This establishes the non-decreasingness of the regularized non-linearity
\smash{\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}}
. We now show that this non-linearity is uniformly Lipschitz continuous. The reverse triangle inequality implies that the map
\smash{y\mapsto\mathsf{C}(0)+2L\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}-R\big)}
is Lipschitz continuous with Lipschitz constant at most
2L
. Recall that the maximum of two Lipschitz continuous maps with Lipschitz constants at most
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
, respectively, is Lipschitz continuous with Lipschitz constant at most
\max(L_{1},L_{2})
. This means that
\smash{\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}}
is Lipschitz continuous with Lipschitz constant at most
2L
when it is restricted to
\smash{\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2R}}
or
\smash{\mathcal{C}\setminus B_{2R}}
. For
\smash{y,y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}}
with
\smash{y\in B_{2R}}
and
\smash{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}\setminus B_{2R}}
, we distinguish two cases. On the one hand, if
\smash{\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(y)=\mathsf{C}(0)+2L\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}-R\big)}
, the reverse triangle inequality shows that

\big\lvert\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(y)-\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(y^{\prime})\big\rvert\leq 2L\big\lvert{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}-{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\big\rvert\leq 2L{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y-y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}.

On the other hand, if
\smash{\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(y)=\mathsf{C}(y)}
, then the reverse triangle inequality reveals that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(y)-\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(y^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{C}(0)+L{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}-\mathsf{C}(0)-2L\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}-R\big)\leq L{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y-y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}+L\big(2R-{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\big)

\displaystyle\leq L{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y-y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}

while the lower bound
\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(y)=\mathsf{C}(y)\geq\mathsf{C}(0)+2L\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}-R\big)
yields

\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(y^{\prime})-\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(y)=2L\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}-{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\big)\leq 2L{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y-y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}.

This shows that
\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}
is a non-decreasing function which agrees with
\mathsf{C}
on
\mathcal{C}\cap B_{R}
and satisfies the Lipschitz continuity property

\big\lvert\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(y)-\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(y^{\prime})\big\rvert\leq\frac{8R}{m}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y-y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}

(2.10)

for all
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
.

Step 2: extending to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

To extend the regularization of the non-linearity (
2.4
) to
\smash{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, define the function
\mathsf{H}_{R}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\mathsf{H}_{R}(y)=\inf\Big\{\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(w)\mid w\in\mathcal{C}\text{ with }w\geq y\Big\}.

(2.11)

Let
\iota=(1,\ldots,1)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and observe that the vector
v=\frac{G\iota}{m}
belongs to
\mathcal{C}
and satisfies the bounds

\frac{1}{d}\leq v_{k}\leq\frac{M}{dm}

(2.12)

for
1\leq k\leq d
. In particular, the infimum in (
2.11
) is never taken over the empty set. Moreover, the non-decreasingness of
\smash{\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}}
and the fact that this function agrees with
\mathsf{C}
on
\mathcal{C}\cap B_{R}
imply that
\smash{\mathsf{H}_{R}}
also agrees with
\mathsf{C}
on
\mathcal{C}\cap B_{R}
. To see that
\smash{\mathsf{H}_{R}}
is non-decreasing, fix
\smash{y,y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
with
y\geq y^{\prime}
, and let
w\in\mathcal{C}
be such that
w\geq y^{\prime}
. Since
w\geq y
, the definition of
\smash{\mathsf{H}_{R}}
gives
\smash{\mathsf{H}_{R}(y)\leq\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(w)}
, and taking the infimum over all such
w
shows that
\smash{\mathsf{H}_{R}(y)\leq\mathsf{H}_{R}(y^{\prime})}
. To establish the Lipschitz continuity of
\smash{\mathsf{H}_{R}}
, fix
\smash{y,y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
and let
z={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y-y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}v\in\mathcal{C}
. Recalling (
2.12
) reveals that for any
1\leq k\leq d
,

y_{k}-y_{k}^{\prime}\leq\lVert y-y^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}=\frac{1}{d}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y-y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq v_{k}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y-y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}=z_{k}.

This means that
z\geq y-y^{\prime}
. In particular, if
w\in\mathcal{C}
is such that
w\geq y^{\prime}
, then
w+z\in\mathcal{C}
with
w+z\geq y
. It follows by (
2.10
), (
2.11
) and (
2.12
) that

\mathsf{H}_{R}(y)-\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(w)\leq\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(w+z)-\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{R}(w)\leq\frac{8R}{m}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|z|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq\frac{8RM}{m^{2}}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y-y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}.

Taking the infimum over all such
w
and reversing the roles of
y
and
y^{\prime}
completes the proof.
∎

With this extended non-linearity at hand, we can now establish the well-posedness of the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
2.5
). The appropriate notion of solution for (
2.5
) will be that of a viscosity solution. With
Appendix

A
in mind, let us fix a non-linearity
\smash{\mathsf{H}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}}
and a domain
\smash{\mathcal{D}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
, and define the notion of a viscosity solution for the more general Hamilton-Jacobi equation

\partial_{t}f(t,x)=\mathsf{H}\big(\nabla f(t,x)\big)\quad\text{on}\quad\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathcal{D}.\\

(2.13)

A brief review of the definition and elementary properties of semi-continuous functions is provided in
Appendix

B
.

Definition 2.4
.

An upper semi-continuous function
\smash{u:[0,\infty)\times\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{R}}
is said to be a viscosity subsolution to (
2.13
) if, given any
\smash{\phi\in C^{\infty}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathcal{D}\big)}
with the property that
u-\phi
has a local maximum at
\smash{(t^{*},x^{*})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathcal{D}}
,

\big(\partial_{t}\phi-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\phi)\big)(t^{*},x^{*})\leq 0.

(2.14)

Definition 2.5
.

A lower semi-continuous function
\smash{v:[0,\infty)\times\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{R}}
is said to be a viscosity supersolution to (
2.13
) if, given any
\smash{\phi\in C^{\infty}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathcal{D}\big)}
with the property that
v-\phi
has a local minimum at
\smash{(t^{*},x^{*})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathcal{D}}
,

\big(\partial_{t}\phi-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\phi)\big)(t^{*},x^{*})\geq 0.

(2.15)

Definition 2.6
.

A continuous function
\smash{f\in C\big([0,\infty)\times\mathcal{D}\big)}
is said to be a viscosity solution to (
2.13
) if it is both a viscosity subsolution and a viscosity supersolution to (
2.13
).

The existence and uniqueness results for Hamilton-Jacobi equations on positive half-spaces developed in
Appendix

A
now give the well-posedness of the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
2.5
). It will be convenient to remember that any closed and convex set
\smash{\mathcal{K}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}}
may be represented as the intersection of the closed and affine half-spaces which contain it,

\mathcal{K}=\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid x\cdot v\geq c\text{ for all }(v,c)\in\mathcal{A}\big\},

(2.16)

where

\mathcal{A}=\big\{(v,c)\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\mid x\cdot v\geq c\text{ for all }x\in\mathcal{K}\text{ and }\lVert v\rVert=1\big\}

(2.17)

for any norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert
. A proof of this classical result may be found in Corollary 4.2.4 of
[
25
]
.

Proposition 2.7
.

For every
R>0
, the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
2.5
) admits a unique viscosity solution
f_{R}\in\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{unif}}
subject to the initial condition
\psi
. Moreover,
f_{R}
has its gradient in the set
\smash{\mathcal{K}_{a}^{\prime}}
and satisfies the Lipschitz bound

\sup_{t>0}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|f_{R}(t,\cdot)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}.

(2.18)

Proof.

To alleviate notation, we fix
R>0
and omit all dependencies on
R>0
. We invoke
Corollary

A.12
to find a viscosity solution
f\in\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{unif}}
to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
2.5
) subject to the initial condition
\psi
with

\sup_{t>0}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|f(t,\cdot)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}.

(2.19)

We will now show that
f
has its gradient in the closed convex set
\smash{\mathcal{K}_{a}^{\prime}}
. Denote by
\mathcal{A}
the set (
2.17
) associated with
\smash{\mathcal{K}_{a}^{\prime}}
and the Euclidean norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{2}
. For each
(v,c)\in\mathcal{A}
introduce the closed convex cone

\mathcal{H}_{v}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid x\cdot v\geq 0\big\}

as well as the function
g_{v,c}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by
g_{v,c}(t,x)=f(t,x)-cx\cdot v
. It is readily verified that
g_{v,c}
satisfies the Hamilton-Jacobi equation

\partial_{t}g(t,x)=\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{R}\big(\nabla g(t,x)\big)\quad\text{ on }\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}

subject to the initial condition
\smash{g_{v,c}(0,x)=\psi(x)-cx\cdot v}
for the non-linearity
\smash{\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{R}(y)=\mathsf{H}_{R}(y+cv\iota)}
, where
\smash{\iota=(1)_{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
. Moreover, this initial condition is
\smash{\mathcal{H}_{v}^{*}}
-non-decreasing (the definition of being
\smash{\mathcal{H}_{v}^{*}}
-non-decreasing was introduced at the beginning of this section). Indeed, we have
\smash{\mathcal{H}_{v}^{*}=\mathbb{R}v}
by the biduality result in
Proposition

B.1
. Moreover, for any
\smash{x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
with
x^{\prime}-x=tv
for some
t\in\mathbb{R}
, the fact that
(x^{\prime}-x)\cdot z\geq tc
for all
\smash{z\in\mathcal{K}_{a}^{\prime}}
and the characterization of
\psi
having its gradient in the set
\smash{\mathcal{K}_{a}^{\prime}}
given in
Proposition

B.2
imply that

g_{v,c}(x^{\prime})-g_{v,c}(x)=\psi(x^{\prime})-\psi(x)-c(x^{\prime}-x)\cdot v\geq tc-tcv\cdot v=0.

It follows by
Proposition

A.13
that
g_{v,c}
is
\mathcal{H}_{v}^{*}
-non-decreasing. We now fix
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
with the property that for all
z\in\mathcal{K}_{a}^{\prime}
, we have
(x^{\prime}-x)\cdot z\geq c
. If
\smash{v=\frac{x^{\prime}-x}{\lVert x^{\prime}-x\rVert_{2}}}
and
\smash{c^{\prime}=\frac{c}{\lVert x^{\prime}-x\rVert_{2}}}
, then
(v,c^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}
so the function
\smash{g_{v,c^{\prime}}}
is
\smash{\mathcal{H}_{v}^{*}}
-non-decreasing. This implies that

f(t,x^{\prime})-f(t,x)=g_{v,c^{\prime}}\big(t,x+\lVert x^{\prime}-x\rVert_{2}v\big)-g_{v,c^{\prime}}(t,x)+c^{\prime}\lVert x^{\prime}-x\rVert_{2}v\cdot v\geq c

which means that
f
has its gradient in
\smash{\mathcal{K}_{a}^{\prime}}
by
Proposition

B.2
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Writing
\mathsf{H}_{K,R}
for the extension of the non-linearity
\mathsf{C}_{K}
constructed in
Proposition

2.3
, the desired result is now an immediate consequence of
Proposition

2.7
and the Lipschitz bound (
1.36
).
∎

3
Well-posedness of the infinite-dimensional equation

In this section, we establish the convergence of the solutions to the projected Hamilton-Jacobi equations (
1.37
) as stated in
Theorem

1.2
. In the notation of
Theorem

1.1
, given an integer
K\geq 1
and some
R>0
, write
\smash{f_{R}^{(K)}\in\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{unif}}}
for the unique solution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
2.5
) subject to the initial condition
\smash{\psi^{(K)}}
. Recall that
\smash{f_{R}^{(K)}}
has its gradient in the set
\smash{\mathcal{K}_{a,K}^{\prime}}
and satisfies the Lipschitz bound

\sup_{t\geq 0}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|f^{(K)}_{R}(t,\cdot)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi^{(K)}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}\leq\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}.

(3.1)

To prove the existence of the limit

f_{R}(t,\mu)=\lim_{K\to\infty}f^{(K)}_{R}\big(t,x^{(K)}(\mu)\big)

(3.2)

we will appropriately adapt the arguments in Section 3.2 of
[
29
]
and Section 3.3 of
[
12
]
. Given two integers
K^{\prime}>K
, it will be convenient to introduce the projection map
\smash{P^{(K,K^{\prime})}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
defined by

P^{(K,K^{\prime})}x=x^{(K)}\big(\mu^{(K^{\prime})}_{x}\big)

(3.3)

as well as the lifting map
L^{(K,K^{\prime})}:\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}}
given by

L^{(K,K^{\prime})}x=\big(\widetilde{x}_{k}\big)_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{K}},

(3.4)

where
\smash{\widetilde{x}_{k}=(x_{k},\ldots,x_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{2^{K^{\prime}-K}}}
. A key observation in proving the existence of the limit (
3.2
) is that

P^{(K,K^{\prime})}L^{(K,K^{\prime})}x=x.

(3.5)

The following technical lemmas will also play their part. The first two translate non-differential properties of a non-differentiable function into differential properties of a smooth function at any point where the difference of these functions is locally maximal. The third analyzes the transformation of the pairs
(v,c)
in the representation (
2.16
) of a closed convex set by the projection map (
3.3
), and the fourth shows that these pairs can be used to quantify the distance from a point to the closed convex set they define.

Lemma 3.1
.

Fix a Lipschitz function
\smash{u\in C\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\big)}
with
\smash{L=\sup_{t>0}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|u(t,\cdot)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}<\infty}
.
If
\smash{\phi\in C^{\infty}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}\big)}
is a smooth function with the property that
u-\phi
has a local maximum at the point
\smash{(t^{*},x^{*})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}}
, then
\smash{{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq L}
. An identical statement holds at a local minimum.

Proof.

Since
u-\phi
has a local maximum at
(t^{*},x^{*})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}
, for every
\epsilon>0
small enough and
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
,

\phi(t^{*},x^{*}+\epsilon x)-\phi(t^{*},x^{*})\geq u(t^{*},x^{*}+\epsilon x)-u(t^{*},x^{*})\geq-\epsilon L{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}.

Dividing by
\epsilon
and letting
\epsilon
tend to zero reveals that

\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})\cdot x\geq-L{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}.

Choosing
\smash{x_{k}=-d\sgn\big(\partial_{x_{k}}\phi(t^{*},x^{*})\big)e_{k}}
for each
1\leq k\leq d
completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.2
.

Fix a closed convex set
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a Lipschitz function
\smash{u\in C\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\big)}
with
\smash{\nabla u\in L^{\infty}((0,\infty)\times}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d};\mathcal{K}^{\prime})
. Any smooth function
\smash{\phi\in C^{\infty}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}\big)}
with the property that
u-\phi
has a local maximum at
\smash{(t^{*},x^{*})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}}
is such that
\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})\in\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
. An identical statement holds at a local minimum.

Proof.

Recall the representation (
2.16
) of the closed convex set
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
as the intersection of the closed and affine half-spaces which contain it, and fix
(v,c)\in\mathcal{A}
. Since
u-\phi
has a local maximum at
(t^{*},x^{*})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}
, for every
\epsilon>0
small enough,

\phi(t^{*},x^{*}+\epsilon v)-\phi(t^{*},x^{*})\geq u(t^{*},x^{*}+\epsilon v)-u(t^{*},x^{*})\geq\epsilon c.

The second inequality uses the characterization of
\nabla u\in\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
given in
Proposition

B.2
and the fact that
x\cdot\epsilon v\geq\epsilon c
for all
\smash{x\in\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}
. Dividing by
\epsilon
and letting
\epsilon
tend to zero reveals that

\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})\cdot v\geq c

for all
(v,c)\in\mathcal{A}
. It follows that
\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})\in\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.3
.

Fix two integers
K^{\prime}>K
large enough and a pair of vectors
\smash{(v,c)\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}}\times\mathbb{R}}
with
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|v|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}=1
such that, for every
\smash{x\in\mathcal{K}_{a,K^{\prime}}^{\prime}}
, we have
v\cdot x\geq c
. Then, for every
\smash{y\in\mathcal{K}_{a,K}^{\prime}}
,

P^{(K,K^{\prime})}v\cdot y\geq c-\frac{2}{2^{K/2}}.

(3.6)

Proof.

Fix
\smash{y\in\mathcal{K}_{a,K^{\prime}}}
, and find vectors
\smash{u^{(K)}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
and
\smash{w^{(K)}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
with

y=G^{(K)}u^{(K)}+w^{(K)},\quad{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|u^{(K)}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq a\quad\text{and}\quad{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|w^{(K)}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq\frac{1}{2^{K/2}}.

Consider the vector

u_{k^{\prime}}^{(K^{\prime})}=\frac{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}\rvert}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}u^{(K)}_{k^{\prime}}\mathbbm{1}\{k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{K}\}

in
\smash{\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}}}
, and for each
k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}
, write
\underline{k}^{\prime}
for the unique dyadic
\underline{k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{K}
with
k^{\prime}\in[\underline{k}^{\prime},\underline{k}^{\prime}+2^{-K})
. Observe that

P^{(K,K^{\prime})}v\cdot y=P^{(K,K^{\prime})}v\cdot G^{(K)}u^{(K)}+P^{(K,K^{\prime})}v\cdot w^{(K)}=v\cdot\big(G^{(K^{\prime})}u^{(K^{\prime})}+\alpha^{(K^{\prime})}\big)+P^{(K,K^{\prime})}v\cdot w^{(K)}

for the vector
\alpha^{(K^{\prime})}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}}
defined by

\alpha^{(K^{\prime})}_{k^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}\sum_{k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}}\big(g(\underline{k}k^{\prime})-g(kk^{\prime})\big)u^{(K^{\prime})}_{k^{\prime}}.

Since
G^{(K^{\prime})}u^{(K^{\prime})}\in\mathcal{K}_{a,K^{\prime}}^{\prime}
, the defining property of
v
, the fact that
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|v|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}=1
and Hölder’s inequality give the lower bound

P^{(K,K^{\prime})}v\cdot y\geq c-{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\alpha^{(K^{\prime})}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}-{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|w^{(K)}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\geq c-{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\alpha^{(K^{\prime})}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}-\frac{1}{2^{K/2}},

(3.7)

where we have used that
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|P^{(K,K^{\prime})}v|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|v|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}
and
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|w^{(K)}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq 2^{-K/2}
. The mean value theorem reveals that

{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\alpha^{(K^{\prime})}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq\frac{\lVert g^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}}{2^{K}}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|u^{(K)}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq\frac{a\lVert g^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}}{2^{K}}.

Substituting this into (
3.7
) and taking
K
large enough completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.4
.

Fix a closed convex set
\smash{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, and recall its representation (
2.16
) with
\smash{\lVert\cdot\rVert={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\cdot|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}}
as the intersection of the closed and affine half-spaces which contain it. If
\smash{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
and
\epsilon>0
are such that
x\cdot v\geq c-\epsilon
for all
(v,c)\in\mathcal{A}
, then there exist
\smash{y\in\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}
and
\smash{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
with
x=y+z
and
\smash{{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|z|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq\epsilon}
.

Proof.

Let
\smash{y\in\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}
denote a projection of
\smash{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
onto the set
\smash{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}
with respect to the normalized-
\smash{\ell^{1,*}}
norm. More precisely, let
\smash{y\in\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}
be any minimizer of the map
\smash{y^{\prime}\mapsto{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y^{\prime}-x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}}
over points
\smash{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}
. The existence of such a projection is guaranteed by the fact that
\smash{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}
is closed. If
y=x
, then the desired conclusion is immediate, so from now on we assume that
y\neq x
. Introduce the set

\mathcal{I}=\big\{k\leq d\mid d\lvert x_{k}-y_{k}\rvert={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\big\}

of indices at which
\smash{{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}}
is achieved, and define the vector
\smash{v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
by

v_{k}=\frac{d}{\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert}\sgn(y_{k}-x_{k})\mathbbm{1}\{k\in\mathcal{I}\}.

We now show that
(v,c)\in\mathcal{A}
for
c=v\cdot y
. By construction
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|v|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}=1
, so suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists
\smash{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}
with
\smash{(y^{\prime}-y)\cdot v=y^{\prime}\cdot v-c<0}
. This means that

(y-y^{\prime})\cdot v=\frac{d}{\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}(y_{k}-y^{\prime}_{k})\sgn(y_{k}-x_{k})>0.

In particular, a coordinate
k^{*}\in\mathcal{I}
at which the quantity
\smash{(y_{k}-y^{\prime}_{k})\sgn(y_{k}-x_{k})}
is maximized over
k\in\mathcal{I}
must satisfy

(y_{k^{*}}-y^{\prime}_{k^{*}})\sgn(y_{k^{*}}-x_{k^{*}})>0.

At this point, fix
t\in(0,1)
small enough so that
\sgn(y_{k}-x_{k}+t(y_{k}^{\prime}-y_{k}))=\sgn(y_{k}-x_{k})
for every
k\in\mathcal{I}
. For such a value of
t>0
,

{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y-x+t(y^{\prime}-y)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}=d(y_{k^{*}}-x_{k^{*}}+t(y^{\prime}_{k^{*}}-y_{k^{*}}))\sgn(y_{k^{*}}-x_{k^{*}})<{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}.

Since the point
y^{\prime\prime}=y+t(y^{\prime}-y)
is a convex combination of
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
, it must lie in the convex set
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
. This contradicts the fact that
y
minimizes the map
\smash{y^{\prime\prime}\mapsto{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y^{\prime\prime}-x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}}
over points
\smash{y^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}
, and shows that
(v,c)\in\mathcal{A}
. It follows that

\epsilon\geq c-x\cdot v=v\cdot(y-x)={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}.

Setting
z=x-y
completes the proof.
∎

We are now in a position to prove
Theorem

1.2
.

Proof of
Theorem

1.2
.

To alleviate notation, until otherwise stated, we fix
\smash{R>\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}}
and keep all dependencies on
R
implicit. The existence of the limit (
3.2
) will be established by showing that the sequence
\smash{(f^{(K)}(t,x^{(K)}(\mu)))_{K}}

is Cauchy. With this in mind, fix
K^{\prime}>K
and introduce the function

f^{(K,K^{\prime})}(t,x)=f^{(K)}\big(t,P^{(K,K^{\prime})}x\big)

(3.8)

defined on

\smash{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}}}
. Since
x^{(K)}(\mu)=P^{(K,K^{\prime})}x^{(K^{\prime})}(\mu)
, the Cauchy condition may be expressed in terms of this function as

\big\lvert f^{(K^{\prime})}\big(t,x^{(K^{\prime})}(\mu)\big)-f^{(K)}\big(t,x^{(K)}(\mu)\big)\big\rvert=\big\lvert f^{(K^{\prime})}\big(t,x^{(K^{\prime})}(\mu)\big)-f^{(K,K^{\prime})}\big(t,x^{(K^{\prime})}(\mu)\big)\big\rvert.

(3.9)

To control the right-hand side of this expression, we will first show that
f^{(K,K^{\prime})}
is an approximate viscosity solution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
2.5
) satisfied by
f^{(K^{\prime})}
, and then we will leverage the comparison principle in
Corollary

A.12
.

Step 1:
f^{(K,K^{\prime})}
is an approximate viscosity solution.

Consider a function
\phi_{K^{\prime}}\in C^{\infty}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}}\big)
with the property that
\smash{f^{(K,K^{\prime})}-\phi_{K^{\prime}}}
achieves a local maximum at
\smash{(t^{*},x^{*})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}}}
. To be more precise, suppose that

\sup_{B_{K^{\prime}}(r)}\big(f^{(K,K^{\prime})}-\phi_{K^{\prime}}\big)=\big(f^{(K,K^{\prime})}-\phi_{K^{\prime}}\big)(t^{*},x^{*}),

where

B_{K^{\prime}}(r)=\Big\{(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}}\mid\lvert t-t^{*}\rvert+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{*}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq r\Big\}

is the ball of radius
r>0
centered at
(t^{*},x^{*})
. Decreasing
r>0
if necessary, assume without loss of generality that

B_{K^{\prime}}(r)\subset(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}}.

Assume also that
\smash{\phi_{K^{\prime}}\in C^{\infty}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}}\big)}
; this can be ensured by replacing
\smash{\phi_{K^{\prime}}}
with
\smash{\eta\phi_{K^{\prime}}}
for some
\smash{\eta\in C^{\infty}\big(\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}}\big)}
which is identically one on
\smash{B_{K^{\prime}}(r)}
and vanishes outside
\smash{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}}}
. With these simplifications at hand, introduce the smooth function

\phi_{K}(t,y)=\phi_{K^{\prime}}\big(t,x^{*}+L^{(K,K^{\prime})}y-L^{(K,K^{\prime})}P^{(K,K^{\prime})}x^{*}\big)

defined on
\smash{(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
.
We will now show that the function
\phi_{K}
admits a local maximum at
\smash{(t^{*},P^{(K,K^{\prime})}x^{*})}
. It will be convenient to notice that for any
\smash{y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}

P^{(K,K^{\prime})}\big(x^{*}+L^{(K,K^{\prime})}y-L^{(K,K^{\prime})}P^{(K,K^{\prime})}x^{*}\big)=P^{(K,K^{\prime})}x^{*}+y-P^{(K,K^{\prime})}x^{*}=y\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}

(3.10)

by (
3.5
).
To simplify notation, let
\smash{y^{*}=P^{(K,K^{\prime})}x^{*}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
and introduce the ball

B_{K}(r)=\big\{(t,y)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\mid\lvert t-t^{*}\rvert+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y-y^{*}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq r\big\}\subset(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}

of radius
r>0
centered at
(t^{*},y^{*})
. Given
(t,y)\in B_{K}(r)
, let
z_{y}=x^{*}+L^{(K,K^{\prime})}y-L^{(K,K^{\prime})}P^{(K,K^{\prime})}x^{*}
in such a way that by (
3.10
),

f^{(K)}(t,y)-\phi_{K}(s,y)=f^{(K,K^{\prime})}(t,z_{y})-\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t,z_{y}).

Observe that

\lvert t-t^{*}\rvert+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|z_{y}-x^{*}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}=\lvert t-t^{*}\rvert+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|L^{(K,K^{\prime})}y-L^{(K,K^{\prime})}y^{*}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}=\lvert t-t^{*}\rvert+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y-y^{*}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq r

so
(t,z_{y})\in B_{K^{\prime}}(r)
. It follows that

\sup_{B_{K}(r)}\big(f^{(K)}-\phi_{K}\big)\leq\sup_{B_{K^{\prime}}(r)}\big(f^{(K,K^{\prime})}-\phi_{K^{\prime}}\big)=\big(f^{(K,K^{\prime})}-\phi_{K^{\prime}}\big)(t^{*},x^{*})=\big(f^{(K)}-\phi_{K}\big)(t^{*},y^{*})

which means that
\phi_{K}
admits a local maximum at
\smash{(t^{*},y^{*})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
. Since
\smash{f^{(K)}}
is a viscosity subsolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
2.5
) with
\nabla f^{(K)}\in\mathcal{K}_{a,K}^{\prime}
,
Lemma

3.2
implies that

\nabla\phi_{K}(t^{*},y^{*})\in\mathcal{K}_{a,K}^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad\big(\partial_{t}\phi_{K}-\mathsf{H}_{K}\big(\nabla\phi_{K}\big)\big)(t^{*},y^{*})\leq 0.

To write this expression in terms of the original test function
\phi_{K^{\prime}}
, notice that

\partial_{t}\phi_{K}(t^{*},y^{*})=\partial_{t}\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})\quad\text{ and }\quad\nabla\phi_{K}(t^{*},y^{*})=\frac{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}\rvert}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}P^{(K,K^{\prime})}\nabla\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*}).

This means that

\frac{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}\rvert}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}P^{(K,K^{\prime})}\nabla\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})\in\mathcal{K}_{a,K}^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{t}\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})-\mathsf{H}_{K}\bigg(\frac{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}\rvert}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}P^{(K,K^{\prime})}\nabla\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})\bigg)\leq 0.

The first of these conditions gives vectors
\smash{u^{(K)}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
and
\smash{w^{(K)}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
with

\frac{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}\rvert}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}P^{(K,K^{\prime})}\nabla\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})=G^{(K)}u^{(K)}+w^{(K)},\quad{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|u^{(K)}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq a\quad\text{and}\quad{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|w^{(K)}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq\frac{1}{2^{K/2}}.

(3.11)

Observe that

{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|G^{(K)}u^{(K)}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq\frac{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}\rvert}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|P^{(K,K^{\prime})}\nabla\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}+\frac{1}{2^{K/2}}\leq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\nabla\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}+\frac{1}{2^{K/2}}.

Since
f^{(K,K^{\prime})}-\phi_{K^{\prime}}
achieves a local maximum at
(t^{*},x^{*})
,
Lemma

3.1
and (
3.1
) imply that

{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\nabla\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}.

Recalling that
