\smash{R>\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}}
and taking
K
large enough ensures that
G^{(K)}u^{(K)}\in\mathcal{C}_{K}\cap B_{R}
. It follows by the Lipschitz continuity of
\mathsf{H}_{K}
established in
Proposition

2.3
that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})-\mathsf{C}_{K}\big(G^{(K)}u^{(K)}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\partial_{t}\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})-\mathsf{H}_{K}\bigg(\frac{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}\rvert}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}P^{(K,K^{\prime})}\nabla\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})\bigg)+\frac{8RM}{2^{K/2}m^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8RM}{2^{K/2}m^{2}}.

At this point, introduce the vector
u^{(K^{\prime})}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}}
defined by

u^{(K^{\prime})}_{k^{\prime}}=\frac{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}\rvert}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}u_{k^{\prime}}^{(K)}\mathbbm{1}\{k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{K}\}

in such a way that

\mathsf{C}_{K^{\prime}}\big(G^{(K^{\prime})}u^{(K^{\prime})}\big)=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert^{2}}\sum_{k,k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}g(kk^{\prime})u^{(K)}_{k}u^{(K)}_{k^{\prime}}=\mathsf{C}_{K}(G^{(K)}u^{(K)}\big),

and therefore,

\partial_{t}\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})-\mathsf{C}_{K^{\prime}}\big(G^{(K^{\prime})}u^{(K^{\prime})}\big)\leq\frac{8RM}{2^{K/2}m^{2}}.

(3.12)

We now show that, up to an error vanishing with
K
, the term
\smash{G^{(K^{\prime})}u^{(K^{\prime})}}
in this expression may be replaced by
\smash{\nabla\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})}
. This is where
Lemma

3.3
will play its part. Recall the representation (
2.16
) with
\smash{\lVert\cdot\rVert={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\cdot|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}}
of
\smash{\mathcal{K}_{a,K^{\prime}}^{\prime}}
as the intersection of the closed and affine half-spaces which contain it, and fix
\smash{(v,c)\in\mathcal{A}}
with
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|v|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}=1
. The characterization of
\smash{\nabla f^{(K)}\in\mathcal{K}_{a,K}^{\prime}}
given in
Proposition

B.2
and
Lemma

3.3
imply that for every
\epsilon>0
small enough,

\displaystyle\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*}+\epsilon v)-\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})

\displaystyle\geq f^{(K)}\big(t^{*},P^{(K,K^{\prime})}x^{*}+\epsilon P^{(K,K^{\prime})}v\big)-f^{(K)}\big(t^{*},P^{(K,K^{\prime})}x^{*}\big)

\displaystyle\geq\epsilon\bigg(c-\frac{2}{2^{K/2}}\bigg)

Dividing by
\epsilon
and letting
\epsilon
tend to zero reveals that
\smash{\nabla\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})\cdot v\geq c-\frac{2}{2^{K/2}}}
. Invoking
Lemma

3.4
gives
\smash{\alpha^{(K^{\prime})}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}}}
and
\smash{\beta^{(K^{\prime})}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}}}
with

\nabla\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})=G^{(K^{\prime})}\alpha^{(K^{\prime})}+\beta^{(K^{\prime})},\quad{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\alpha^{(K^{\prime})}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq a\quad\text{and}\quad{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\beta^{(K^{\prime})}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq\frac{2}{2^{K/2}}.

(3.13)

At this point, fix
k\in\mathcal{D}_{K}
and
k^{\prime}\in[k,k+2^{-K})
. The mean value theorem implies that

\displaystyle\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}\rvert\big\lvert\big(G^{(K^{\prime})}u^{(K^{\prime})}\big)_{k^{\prime}}-\partial_{x_{k^{\prime}}}\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})\big\rvert

\displaystyle=\bigg\lvert\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}\sum_{k^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}g(k^{\prime}k^{\prime\prime})u^{(K)}_{k^{\prime\prime}}-\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}\rvert\partial_{x_{k^{\prime}}}\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})\bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\big\lvert\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert\big(G^{(K)}u^{(K)}\big)_{k}-\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}\rvert\partial_{x_{k^{\prime}}}\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})\big\rvert

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+\frac{\lVert g^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|u^{(K)}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}}{2^{K}}.

Remembering (
3.11
) and (
3.13
), noticing that
\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert=2^{K+1}
and using the mean value theorem once again shows that

\displaystyle\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert\big(G^{(K)}u^{(K)}\big)_{k}

\displaystyle=\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert\sum_{\ell=0}^{2^{K^{\prime}-K}-1}\partial_{x_{k+\frac{\ell}{2^{K^{\prime}}}}}\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})-\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert w^{(K)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{2^{K^{\prime}-K}-1}\sum_{k^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}}g\Big(k+\frac{\ell}{2^{K^{\prime}}}\cdot k^{\prime\prime}\Big)\alpha_{k^{\prime\prime}}^{(K^{\prime})}+\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert\sum_{\ell=0}^{2^{K^{\prime}-K}-1}\beta^{(K^{\prime})}_{k+\frac{\ell}{2^{K^{\prime}}}}-\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert w_{k}^{(K)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{2^{K^{\prime}-K}-1}\sum_{k^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}}g(k^{\prime}k^{\prime\prime})\alpha_{k^{\prime\prime}}^{(K^{\prime})}+\BigO_{1}\bigg(\frac{\lVert g^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\alpha^{(K^{\prime})}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}}{2^{K}}+\frac{3}{2^{K/2}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}\rvert\big(G^{(K^{\prime})}\alpha^{(K^{\prime})}\big)_{k^{\prime}}+\BigO_{1}\bigg(\frac{4}{2^{K/2}}\bigg)=\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}\rvert\partial_{x_{k^{\prime}}}\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})+\BigO_{1}\bigg(\frac{5}{2^{K/2}}\bigg),

where we have written
X=Y+\BigO_{1}(Z)
to mean that
\lvert X-Y\rvert\leq Z
. In the third equality we used that
\smash{{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\beta^{(K^{\prime})}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|w^{(K)}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq 3\cdot 2^{-K/2}}
, and in the fourth equality we used that
\smash{{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\alpha^{(K^{\prime})}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq a}
and increased
K
if necessary. It follows that

{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|G^{(K^{\prime})}u^{(K^{\prime})}-\nabla\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq\frac{5}{2^{K/2}}+\frac{a\lVert g^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}}{2^{K}}\leq\frac{6}{2^{K/2}},

where we have used that
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|u^{(K)}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq a
and increased
K
if necessary. Combining this bound with the Lipschitz continuity of
\mathsf{H}_{K^{\prime}}
established in
Proposition

2.3
and (
3.12
) reveals that

\partial_{t}\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})-\mathsf{H}_{K^{\prime}}\big(\nabla\phi_{K^{\prime}}(t^{*},x^{*})\big)\leq\mathcal{E}_{K}

(3.14)

for the error term

\mathcal{E}_{K}=\frac{56RM}{2^{K/2}m^{2}}.

In particular, the function
\smash{(t,x)\mapsto f^{(K,K^{\prime})}(t,x)-\mathcal{E}_{K}t}
is a viscosity subsolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
2.5
) satisfied by
\smash{f^{(K^{\prime})}}
. An identical argument shows that
\smash{(t,x)\mapsto f^{(K,K^{\prime})}(t,x)+\mathcal{E}_{K}t}
is a viscosity supersolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
2.5
) satisfied by
\smash{f^{(K^{\prime})}}
.

Step 2: comparison principle.

Using (
3.1
) and (
1.36
), it is readily verified that
\smash{f^{(K,K^{\prime})}}
and
\smash{f^{(K^{\prime})}}
are uniformly Lipschitz continuous in the
x
variable relative to the normalized-
\smash{\ell^{1}}
norm with Lipschitz constant at most
\smash{L=\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}}
. Indeed, for any
t>0
and all
\smash{x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}}}
,

\lvert f^{(K,K^{\prime})}(t,x)-f^{(K,K^{\prime})}(t,x^{\prime})\rvert\leq\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|P^{(K,K^{\prime})}x-P^{(K,K^{\prime})}x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}.

If
V={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\mathsf{H}_{K}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1,*}
, then the comparison principle in
Corollary

A.12
implies that for any
R^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
, the map

(t^{\prime},x^{\prime})\mapsto f^{(K,K^{\prime})}(t^{\prime},x^{\prime})-f^{(K^{\prime})}(t^{\prime},x^{\prime})-(2L+1)\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}+Vt^{\prime}-R^{\prime}\big)_{+}-\mathcal{E}_{K}t^{\prime}

(3.15)

achieves its supremum on
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}}
. We now choose
\smash{R^{\prime}={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x^{(K^{\prime})}(\mu)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}+Vt}
and distinguish two cases. On the one hand, if
t^{\prime}=0
and
\smash{{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\geq(2L+1)R^{\prime}}
, then (
3.15
) is bounded by

2L{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}-(2L+1)({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}-R^{\prime})=R^{\prime}-{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq 0,

(3.16)

where we have used the fact that
f^{(K,K^{\prime})}(0,0)=f^{(K^{\prime})}(0,0)
. On the other hand, if
t^{\prime}=0
and
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq(2L+1)R^{\prime}
, then (
H4
) implies that (
3.15
) is bounded by

\big\lvert\psi^{(K^{\prime})}(x^{\prime})-\psi^{(K)}(P^{(K,K^{\prime})}x^{\prime})\big\rvert\leq\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},W}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}W\big(\bar{\mu}^{(K^{\prime})}_{x^{\prime}},\bar{\mu}^{(K)}_{P^{(K,K^{\prime})}x^{\prime}}\big).

(3.17)

To estimate this Wasserstein distance, fix a Lipschitz function
h:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
with
\lVert h\rVert_{\text{Lip}}\leq 1
and observe that

\displaystyle\Big\lvert\int_{-1}^{1}h(y)\,\mathrm{d}\big(\mu^{(K^{\prime})}_{x^{\prime}}-\mu^{(K)}_{P^{(K,K^{\prime})}x^{\prime}}\big)(y)\Big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}\rvert}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{2^{K^{\prime}-K-1}}x^{\prime}_{k+\frac{\ell}{2^{K^{\prime}}}}\Big\lvert h\Big(k+\frac{\ell}{2^{K^{\prime}}}\Big)-h(k)\Big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K^{\prime}}\rvert}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{2^{K^{\prime}-K-1}}x^{\prime}_{k+\frac{\ell}{2^{K^{\prime}}}}\frac{\ell}{2^{K^{\prime}}}\leq\frac{{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}}{2^{K}}.

Taking the supremum over all such
h
and recalling (
3.17
) shows that (
3.15
) is bounded by

\frac{\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},W}(2L+1)R^{\prime}}{2^{K}}

(3.18)

whenever
t^{\prime}=0
and
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq(2L+1)R^{\prime}
. Combining this with (
3.16
) reveals that the map (
3.15
) is uniformly bounded by (
3.18
). Choosing
t^{\prime}=t
and
x^{\prime}=x^{(K^{\prime})}(\mu)
, and recalling the choice of
R^{\prime}
yields

f^{(K,K^{\prime})}\big(t,x^{(K^{\prime})}(\mu)\big)-f^{(K^{\prime})}\big(t,x^{(K^{\prime})}(\mu)\big)\leq\frac{\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},W}(2L+1)}{2^{K}}\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x^{(K^{\prime})}(\mu)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}+Vt\big)+\mathcal{E}_{K}t.

Together with (
3.9
) and an identical argument with the roles of
f^{(K,K^{\prime})}
and
f^{(K^{\prime})}
reversed, this implies that

\big\lvert f^{(K^{\prime})}\big(t,x^{(K^{\prime})}(\mu)\big)-f^{(K)}\big(t,x^{(K)}(\mu)\big)\big\rvert\leq\frac{\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},W}(2L+1)}{2^{K}}\big(\mu[-1,1]+Vt\big)+\mathcal{E}_{K}t.

Since
V={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\mathsf{H}_{K}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1,*}
is independent of
K
by
Proposition

2.3
and
\mathcal{E}_{K}
tends to zero as
K
tends to infinity, the sequence
\smash{(f^{(K)}(t,x^{(K)}(\mu)))_{K}}
is Cauchy. This establishes the existence of the limit (
3.2
) for each fixed
\smash{R>\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}}
. All that remains is to show that this limit is independent of
R
.

Step 3: independence on
R
.

To show that the limit (
3.2
) is independent of
R
, fix
\smash{R^{\prime}>R>\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}}
as well as
K\geq 1
large enough. The idea will be to show that, up to an error vanishing with
K
, the function
\smash{f_{R}^{(K)}}
satisfies the Hamilton-Jacobi equation defining
\smash{f_{R^{\prime}}^{(K)}}
. The equality of the limit (
3.2
) associated with
R
and
R^{\prime}
will then follow from the comparison principle in
Corollary

A.12
. Consider
\smash{\phi\in C^{\infty}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\big)}
with the property that
\smash{f_{R}^{(K)}-\phi}
achieves a local maximum at the point
\smash{(t^{*},x^{*})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
. Since
\smash{f_{R}^{(K)}}
is a viscosity subsolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
2.5
) associated with the non-linearity
\mathsf{H}_{K,R}
,

\big(\partial_{t}\phi-\mathsf{H}_{K,R}(\nabla\phi)\big)(t^{*},x^{*})\leq 0.

The fact that
\smash{f_{R}^{(K)}}
has its gradient in the set
\smash{\mathcal{K}_{a,K}^{\prime}}
together with (
3.1
),
Lemma

3.1
and
Lemma

3.2
implies that

\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})\in\mathcal{K}_{a,K}^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}.

It is therefore possible to find
\smash{u\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
and
\smash{w\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
with

\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})=G^{(K)}u+w,\quad{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|u|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq a\quad\text{and}\quad{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|w|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq\frac{1}{2^{K/2}}.

Observe that

{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|G^{(K)}u|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|w|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}+\frac{1}{2^{K/2}},

so increasing
K
if necessary, it is possible to ensure that
\smash{G^{(K)}u\in\mathcal{C}_{K}\cap B_{R}\subset\mathcal{C}_{K}\cap B_{R^{\prime}}}
. It follows by the Lipschitz continuity of
\mathsf{H}_{K,R}
established in
Proposition

2.3
that

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}\phi-\mathsf{H}_{K,R^{\prime}}(\nabla\phi)\big)(t^{*},x^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\partial_{t}\phi(t^{*},x^{*})-\mathsf{C}_{K}\big(G^{(K)}u\big)+\frac{8R^{\prime}M}{2^{K/2}m^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\big(\partial_{t}\phi-\mathsf{H}_{K,R}(\nabla\phi)\big)(t^{*},x^{*})+\frac{8(R^{\prime}+R)M}{2^{K/2}m^{2}}\leq\mathcal{E}_{K}

for the error term

\mathcal{E}_{K}=\frac{8(R^{\prime}+R)M}{2^{K/2}m^{2}}.

In particular, the function
\smash{(t,x)\mapsto f_{R}^{K}(t,x)-\mathcal{E}_{K}t}
is a viscosity subsolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
2.5
) defining
\smash{f_{R^{\prime}}^{K}}
. An identical argument shows that
\smash{(t,x)\mapsto f_{R}^{K}(t,x)+\mathcal{E}_{K}t}
is a viscosity supersolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
2.5
) defining
\smash{f_{R^{\prime}}^{K}}
. It follows by the comparison principle in
Corollary

A.12
that for every
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
and
t\geq 0
,

\big\lvert f_{R}^{(K)}\big(t,x^{(K)}(\mu)\big)-f_{R^{\prime}}^{(K)}\big(t,x^{(K)}(\mu)\big)\big\rvert\leq\mathcal{E}_{K}t.

Letting
K
tend to infinity completes the proof.
∎

4
Approximate Hopf-Lax formula in finite dimensions

In this section, we revisit the Hamilton-Jacobi equation studied in
Section

2
, and under the additional assumption that the matrix
\smash{G\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}}
in (
2.1
) is non-negative definite, we establish an approximate Hopf-Lax formula for the unique solution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
2.5
). By an approximate Hopf-Lax formula we mean that the error between the Hopf-Lax function we will define and the solution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation constructed in
Proposition

2.7
tends to zero when the dimension
d
tends to infinity. This will be used in the next section to establish an exact Hopf-Lax formula for the solution to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.10
). It will be convenient to introduce the bilinear form

(x,y)_{G}=Gx\cdot y

(4.1)

associated with the non-negative definite matrix
G
, as well as its induced semi-norm

\lVert x\rVert_{G}=\sqrt{(x,x)_{G}}.

(4.2)

In this notation, the non-linearity (
2.4
) may be written as

\mathsf{C}(Gx)=\frac{1}{2}Gx\cdot x=\frac{1}{2}\lVert x\rVert_{G}^{2}.

(4.3)

In particular, the non-linearity (
2.4
) is a convex function. This convexity property will allow us to establish an approximate Hopf-Lax formula for the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
2.5
). We define the Hopf-Lax function
\smash{f_{\mathrm{HL}}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}}
by

f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t,x)=\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\bigg\{\psi(x+y)-\frac{\|y\|_{G}^{2}}{2t}\bigg\}.

(4.4)

The main result of this section is the following.

Proposition 4.1

(Hopf-Lax)
.

Fix an initial condition
\smash{\psi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}}
satisfying (
2.6
) with its gradient in the set
\smash{\mathcal{K}_{a}^{\prime}}
, let
\smash{R>{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}}
, and denote by
\smash{f:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}}
the unique solution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
2.5
) constructed in
Proposition

2.7
. If
\smash{G\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}}
is non-negative definite, then for all
\smash{(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
,

\lvert f(t,x)-f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t,x)\rvert\leq\frac{t}{\sqrt{d}}\bigg(R+a+\frac{8RM}{m^{2}}\bigg).

(4.5)

To prove this result, we first verify that the convex dual of the mapping
y\mapsto\|y\|_{G}^{2}/2
is the non-linearity
\mathsf{C}
. We next show that the function (
4.4
) satisfies the right initial condition, and that the supremum in its definition is attained. We next argue that this function satisfies a semigroup property, and deduce that it belongs to
\smash{\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{unif}}}
. Finally we show that, in a sense to be made precise, it is an approximate solution to the the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
2.5
). The estimate (
4.5
) will then be a consequence of the comparison principle in
Corollary

A.12
. It will be convenient to note that for every
z\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
, we have

\|z\|_{G}^{2}\geq\frac{m}{d^{2}}\sum_{k,k^{\prime}=1}^{d}z_{k}\,z_{k^{\prime}}=m\,{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|z|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}^{2}.

(4.6)

Lemma 4.2
.

If
\smash{G\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}}
is non-negative definite, then for every
z\in\mathcal{C}
,

\mathsf{C}(z)=\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\bigg\{y\cdot z-\frac{\|y\|_{G}^{2}}{2}\bigg\}.

(4.7)

Moreover, the supremum is attained at any point
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
with
z=Gx
.

Proof.

We can represent each
z\in\mathcal{C}
in the form of
Gx
for some
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
. Using that
G
is non-negative definite, we can appeal to the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to assert that

y\cdot Gx=(x,y)_{G}\leq\|x\|_{G}\,\|y\|_{G}\leq\frac{1}{2}\|x\|_{G}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|y\|_{G}^{2}.

We thus obtain that

\mathsf{C}(Gx)=\frac{1}{2}\|x\|_{G}^{2}\geq\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\left(y\cdot Gx-\frac{\|y\|_{G}^{2}}{2}\right).

For the converse inequality, we simply test the supremum with
y=x
.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

If
\smash{G\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}}
is non-negative definite and
\psi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is a Lipschitz continuous initial condition with
\smash{\nabla\psi\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d};\mathcal{K}_{a}^{\prime})}
, then for every
\smash{x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
,

f_{\mathrm{HL}}(0,x)=\psi(x).

(4.8)

Proof.

For
t=0
, we interpret the definition of
f
as

f_{\mathrm{HL}}(0,x)=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\\
\|y\|_{G}=0\end{subarray}}\psi(x+y).

(4.9)

Recalling (
4.6
), we see that the only
y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
with
\|y\|_{G}=0
is
y=0
. Together with (
4.9
), this completes the proof.
∎

It will slightly simplify our arguments below to notice that the supremum in (
4.4
) is achieved.

Lemma 4.4
.

Fix an initial condition
\smash{\psi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}}
satisfying (
2.6
). If
\smash{G\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}}
is non-negative definite, then for any point
\smash{(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
, there exists
\smash{y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
with

f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t,x)=\psi(x+y)-\frac{\|y\|_{G}^{2}}{2t}.

(4.10)

Proof.

Combining (
4.6
) with the Lipschitz continuity of
\psi
reveals that

\psi(x+y)-\frac{\|y\|_{G}^{2}}{2t}\leq\psi(x)+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\Big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}-\frac{m}{2t}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\Big).

We can thus restrict the supremum in (
4.4
) to those
y
’s in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
that satisfy
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq\frac{2t}{m}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}
. Since we are now optimizing a continuous function over a compact set, it is clear that the supremum is achieved.
∎

Lemma 4.5

(Semigroup property)
.

Fix an initial condition
\smash{\psi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}}
satisfying (
2.6
). If
\smash{G\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}}
is non-negative definite, then for every pair
t>s>0
and
\smash{x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
,

f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t,x)=\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\bigg\{f_{\mathrm{HL}}(s,x+y)-\frac{\|y\|_{G}^{2}}{2(t-s)}\bigg\}.

(4.11)

Proof.

Fix
y,z\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
. Since
\|\cdot\|_{G}^{2}
is a convex mapping, we have

\left\|\frac{y+z}{t}\right\|_{G}^{2}\leq\frac{s}{t}\left\|\frac{y}{s}\right\|_{G}^{2}+\frac{t-s}{t}\left\|\frac{z}{t-s}\right\|_{G}^{2}.

Substituting this bound into (
4.4
) yields

f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t,x)\geq\psi(x+y+z)-\frac{t}{2}\left\|\frac{y+z}{t}\right\|_{G}^{2}\geq\psi(x+y+z)-\frac{s}{2}\left\|\frac{y}{s}\right\|_{G}^{2}-\frac{t-s}{2}\left\|\frac{z}{t-s}\right\|_{G}^{2}.

Taking the supremum over all
y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
gives

f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t,x)\geq f_{\mathrm{HL}}(s,x+z)-\frac{\|z\|_{G}^{2}}{2(t-s)},

and taking the supremum over all
z\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
establishes the lower bound

f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t,x)\geq\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\bigg\{f_{\mathrm{HL}}(s,x+y)-\frac{\|y\|_{G}^{2}}{2(t-s)}\bigg\}.

To obtain the matching upper bound, we invoke
Lemma

4.4
to find
y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
such that

f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t,x)=\psi(x+y)-\frac{\|y\|_{G}^{2}}{2t}.

Defining
z=\frac{t-s}{t}y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
, we observe that

\frac{z}{t-s}=\frac{y}{t}=\frac{y-z}{s}.

(4.12)

In particular, testing the supremum in (
4.4
) with
y-z=\frac{s}{t}y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
gives

f_{\mathrm{HL}}(s,x+z)\geq\psi(x+z+y-z)-\frac{\|y-z\|_{G}^{2}}{2s}=\psi(x+y)-\frac{\|y-z\|_{G}^{2}}{2s},

and thus, using also (
4.12
), we obtain

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{HL}}(s,x+z)-\frac{\|z\|_{G}^{2}}{2(t-s)}

\displaystyle\geq\psi(x+y)-\frac{\|y-z\|_{G}^{2}}{2s}-\frac{\|z\|_{G}^{2}}{2(t-s)}

\displaystyle=\psi(x+y)-\frac{\|y\|_{G}^{2}}{2t}.

Taking the supremum over
z\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
and then over
y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
completes the proof.
∎

We next prove some regularity properties of the function
f_{\mathrm{HL}}
in (
4.4
).

Lemma 4.6
.

Fix an initial condition
\smash{\psi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}}
satisfying (
2.6
). If
\smash{G\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}}
is non-negative definite, then
f_{\mathrm{HL}}\in\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{unif}}
with

\sup_{t>0}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t,\cdot)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}\leq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}\quad\text{and}\quad[f_{\mathrm{HL}}]_{0}\leq\frac{{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}^{2}}{2m}.

(4.13)

Proof.

Fix
(t,x,x^{\prime})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
and invoke
Lemma

4.4
to find
y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
with

f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t,x)=\psi(x+y)-\frac{\|y\|_{G}^{2}}{2t}.

Taking this
y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
in (
4.4
) gives the lower bound

f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t,x^{\prime})\geq\psi(x^{\prime}+y)-\frac{\|y\|_{G}^{2}}{2t},

and thus

f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t,x)-f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t,x^{\prime})\leq\psi(x+y)-\psi(x^{\prime}+y)\leq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}.

Reversing the roles of
x
and
x^{\prime}
gives
y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
with

f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t,x^{\prime})-f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t,x)\leq\psi(x^{\prime}+y^{\prime})-\psi(x+y^{\prime})\leq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}.

Combining these two bounds yields the first inequality in (
4.13
). To establish Lipschitz continuity in time, fix
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
as well as
t>s\geq 0
. The semigroup property in
Lemma

4.5
with
y=0
implies that

f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t,x)\geq f_{\mathrm{HL}}(s,x).

(4.14)

Using
Lemma

4.5
in combination with the first inequality in (
4.13
) and (
4.6
) gives

f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t,x)\leq f_{\mathrm{HL}}(s,x)+\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\bigg\{{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}-\frac{m{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}^{2}}{2(t-s)}\bigg\}\leq f_{\mathrm{HL}}(s,x)+\frac{{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}^{2}}{2m}(t-s),

where we have used the fact that
r\mapsto r-\frac{1}{2}ar^{2}
achieves its maximum at
r=1/a
. Combining this with (
4.14
) completes the proof.
∎

We are finally in a position to prove
Proposition

4.1
.

Proof of
Proposition

4.1
.

Denote by
\mathcal{E}_{d}
an error term that will be defined in the course of the proof. We will proceed in three steps; first, we will show that the function
\smash{f_{+}(t,x)=f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t,x)+\mathcal{E}_{d}t}
is a viscosity supersolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
2.5
), then we will show that the function
\smash{f_{-}(t,x)=f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t,x)-\mathcal{E}_{d}t}
is a viscosity subsolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
2.5
), and finally we will conclude using the comparison principle in
Corollary

A.12
.

Step 1:
f_{+}
viscosity supersolution.

Consider a smooth function
\smash{\phi\in C^{\infty}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}\big)}
with the property that
\smash{f_{+}-\phi}
has a local minimum at
\smash{(t^{*},x^{*})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}}
. Using
Proposition

B.2
, it is readily verified that
f_{\mathrm{HL}}
has its gradient in
\smash{\mathcal{K}_{a}^{\prime}}
as
\psi
does. It follows by
Lemma

3.2
that
\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})\in\mathcal{K}_{a}^{\prime}
. It is therefore possible to find
\smash{u\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
and
\smash{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
with

\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})=Gu+w,\quad{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|u|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq a\quad\text{and}\quad{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|w|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}.

On the one hand, if
s>0
is sufficiently small that
t^{*}-s>0
, then

f_{+}(t^{*}-s,x^{*}+su)-\phi(t^{*}-s,x^{*}+su)\geq f_{+}(t^{*},x^{*})-\phi(t^{*},x^{*}).

On the other hand, taking
su\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
in
Lemma

4.5
reveals that

f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t^{*},x^{*})\geq f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t^{*}-s,x^{*}+su)-s\frac{\|u\|_{G}^{2}}{2}.

It follows that

\phi(t^{*},x^{*})-\phi(t^{*}-s,x^{*}+su)+s\frac{\|u\|_{G}^{2}}{2}-\mathcal{E}_{d}s\geq 0.

Dividing by
0<s<t^{*}
and letting
s\to 0
yields

\partial_{t}\phi(t^{*},x^{*})-u\cdot\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})+\frac{\|u\|_{G}^{2}}{2}-\mathcal{E}_{d}\geq 0.

Recalling that
\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})=Gu+w
and using
Lemma

4.2
, we obtain

\partial_{t}\phi(t^{*},x^{*})-\mathsf{C}(Gu)-u\cdot w-\mathcal{E}_{d}\geq 0.

By
Lemma

3.1
and
Lemma

4.6
, we have

{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|Gu|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\leq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\leq R,

so the Lipschitz continuity of the non-linearity
\mathsf{H}_{R}
established in
Proposition

2.3
implies that

\big(\partial_{t}\phi-\mathsf{H}_{R}(\nabla\phi)\big)(t^{*},x^{*})\geq\mathcal{E}_{d}-{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|u|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|w|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}-\frac{8RM}{m^{2}\sqrt{d}}\geq\mathcal{E}_{d}-\frac{a}{\sqrt{d}}-\frac{8RM}{m^{2}\sqrt{d}}.

This shows that
f_{+}
is a supersolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
2.5
) provided that

\mathcal{E}_{d}\geq\frac{a}{\sqrt{d}}+\frac{8RM}{m^{2}\sqrt{d}}.

Step 2:
f_{-}
viscosity subsolution.

Consider a smooth function
\smash{\phi\in C^{\infty}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}\big)}
with the property that
\smash{f_{-}-\phi}
has a local maximum at
\smash{(t^{*},x^{*})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}}
. Since
f_{\mathrm{HL}}
has its gradient in
\smash{\mathcal{K}_{a}^{\prime}}
as
\psi
does, we have
\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})\in\mathcal{K}_{a}^{\prime}
by
Lemma

3.2
. It is therefore possible to find
\smash{u\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
and
\smash{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
with

\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})=Gu+w,\quad{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|u|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq a\quad\text{and}\quad{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|w|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists
\delta>0
with

\big(\partial_{t}\phi-\mathsf{H}_{R}(\nabla\phi)\big)(t^{*},x^{*})\geq\delta>0.

Arguing as in the previous step, this implies that

\partial_{t}\phi(t^{*},x^{*})-\mathsf{C}(Gu)\geq\delta-\frac{8RM}{m^{2}\sqrt{d}}.

By
Lemma

4.2
, this may be recast as the assumption that

\partial_{t}\phi(t^{*},x^{*})-y\cdot Gu+\frac{\lVert y\rVert_{G}^{2}}{2}\geq\delta-\frac{8RM}{m^{2}\sqrt{d}}

for all
y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
. By continuity, of
\partial_{t}\phi
and
\nabla\phi
, up to redefining
\delta>0
, we may in fact assume that

\partial_{t}\phi(t^{\prime},x^{\prime})-y\cdot\nabla\phi(t^{\prime},x^{\prime})+\frac{\|y\|_{G}^{2}}{2}\geq\delta-\frac{8RM}{m^{2}\sqrt{d}}-{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|w|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}

(4.15)

for all
y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
and
(t^{\prime},x^{\prime})
sufficiently close to
(t^{*},x^{*})
. Recalling
Lemma

4.5
and arguing as in the proof of
Lemma

4.4
, it is possible to find
R>0
such that, for every
s>0
sufficiently small, there exists
y_{s}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
with
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y_{s}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq Rs
and

f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t^{*},x^{*})=f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t^{*}-s,x^{*}+y_{s})-\frac{\|y_{s}\|_{G}^{2}}{2s}.

It follows by the fundamental theorem of calculus and the absurd assumption (
4.15
) used with
y=\frac{y_{s}}{s}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
that

\displaystyle\phi(t^{*},x^{*})-\phi(t^{*}-s,x^{*}+y_{s})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\phi\big(rt^{*}+(1-r)(t^{*}-s),rx^{*}+(1-r)(x^{*}+y_{s})\big)\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\big(s\partial_{t}\phi-y_{s}\cdot\nabla\phi\big)(t^{*}+(r-1)s,x^{*}+(1-r)y_{s})\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\geq s\delta-\frac{\|y_{s}\|_{G}^{2}}{2s}-s\frac{8RM}{m^{2}\sqrt{d}}-{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y_{s}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|w|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}

\displaystyle\geq f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t^{*},x^{*})-f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t^{*}-s,x^{*}+y_{s})+s\bigg(\delta-\frac{8RM}{m^{2}\sqrt{d}}-\frac{R}{\sqrt{d}}\bigg).

Rearranging shows that for
s
sufficiently small,

f_{-}(t^{*}-s,x^{*}+y_{s})-\phi(t^{*}-s,x^{*}+y_{s})\geq s\bigg(\delta-\frac{8RM}{m^{2}\sqrt{d}}-\frac{R}{\sqrt{d}}+\mathcal{E}_{d}\bigg)+f_{-}(t^{*},x^{*})-\phi(t^{*},x^{*}).

This contradicts the fact that
f-\phi
admits a local maximum at
(t^{*},x^{*})
provided that

\mathcal{E}_{d}\geq\frac{R}{\sqrt{d}}+\frac{8RM}{m^{2}\sqrt{d}}.

Step 3: comparison principle.

Combining step 1 and step 2 shows that, if we define

\mathcal{E}_{d}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\bigg(R+a+\frac{8RM}{m^{2}}\bigg),

then
\smash{f_{+}}
is a viscosity supersolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
2.5
) while
\smash{f_{-}}
is a viscosity subsolution to this equation. Together with
Lemma

4.3
,
Lemma

4.6
and the comparison principle in
Corollary

A.12
, this implies that for every
{(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
,

\lvert f_{\mathrm{HL}}(t,x)-f(t,x)\rvert\leq\mathcal{E}_{d}t

as required.
∎

5
Hopf-Lax formula for the infinite-dimensional equation

In this section, we apply
Proposition

4.1
to the projected Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.37
) and let
K
tend to infinity in the resulting variational formula to establish
Theorem

1.3
. In addition to the assumptions (
1.32
)-(
H4
), we will suppose that the kernel
g:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
is non-negative definite in the sense that it satisfies (
H5
). This assumption is equivalent to the non-negative definiteness of each of the matrices (
1.18
), and therefore to the convexity of each of the projected non-linearities (
1.21
). In particular,
Proposition

4.1
implies that the unique solution
\smash{f^{(K)}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\to\mathbb{R}}
to the projected Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.37
) in
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{unif}}
subject to the initial condition
\smash{\psi^{(K)}}
satisfies

f^{(K)}\big(t,x^{(K)}(\mu)\big)=\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}\bigg\{\psi^{(K)}\big(x^{(K)}(\mu)+y\big)-\frac{\lVert y\rVert_{G}^{2}}{2t}\bigg\}+\BigO\big(t\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert^{-1/2}\big).

(5.1)

Remembering the definition of the projected initial condition (
1.17
), the projected non-linearity (
1.21
) and its relationship (
1.24
) to the non-linearity
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
in (
1.5
) shows that

f^{(K)}\big(t,x^{(K)}(\mu)\big)=\sup_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}^{(K)}}\bigg\{\psi(\mu+\nu)-\frac{\mathsf{C}_{\infty}(G_{\nu})}{t}\bigg\}+\BigO\big(t\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert^{-1/2}\big),

(5.2)

where we have made the substitution
\smash{y=x^{(K)}(\nu)}
for
\smash{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}^{(K)}}
.
Using
Theorem

1.2
and a simple continuity argument to let
K
tend to infinity in this expression gives the variational representation formula

\displaystyle f(t,\mu)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}}\bigg\{\psi(\mu+\nu)-\frac{1}{2t}\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\nu}(y)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(y)\bigg\}

(5.3)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}}\bigg\{\psi(\mu+t\nu)-\frac{t}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\nu}(y)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(y)\bigg\}.

(5.4)

The second of these expressions follows from the first by setting
\nu^{\prime}=t\nu
. To establish
Theorem

1.3
, we need to show that the supremum in (
5.4
) is achieved at some
\nu^{*}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, and that whenever the initial condition admits a Gateaux derivative at the measure
\mu+t\nu^{*}
with density
x\mapsto D_{\mu}\psi(\mu+t\nu^{*},x)
in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
,

G_{\nu^{*}}=D_{\mu}(\mu+t\nu^{*},\cdot).

(5.5)

If we ignore the constraint that the optimizers in (
5.4
) must be non-negative measures, then this latter property is clear from the first order conditions on a maximizer. To prove this rigorously, we first show that a maximizer exists, and we then establish a Cauchy-Schwarz inequality for the non-negative definite kernel
\smash{\tilde{g}(x,y)=g(xy)}
.

Lemma 5.1
.

For every
t\geq 0
and
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, there exists
\nu^{*}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
with

f(t,\mu)=\psi(\mu+t\nu^{*})-\frac{t}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\nu^{*}}(y)\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{*}(y).

(5.6)

Proof.

Fix a probability measure
\nu\in\Pr[-1,1]
and a positive constant
\lambda>0
. The Lipschitz continuity (
H2
) of the initial condition implies that

\psi(\mu+\lambda t\nu)\leq\psi(\mu)+\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}\mathrm{TV}(0,\lambda t\nu)\leq\psi(\mu)+2\lambda t\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}.

On the other hand,

\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\lambda\nu}(y)\,\mathrm{d}(\lambda\nu)(y)=\lambda^{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}g(xy)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(x)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(y)\geq\lambda^{2}m.

Combining these two bounds reveals that

\psi(\mu+t\nu)-\frac{t}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\nu}(y)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(y)\leq\psi(\mu)+2\lambda t\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}-\frac{\lambda^{2}tm}{2}.

The supremum in (
5.4
) can therefore be restricted to measures in
\mathcal{M}_{+}
with bounded total mass. The existence of a maximizer is now an immediate consequence of Prokhorov’s theorem. Indeed, if
\smash{(\nu_{n})\subset\mathcal{M}_{+}}
denotes a maximizing sequence, we may assume without loss of generality that each measure in this sequence has total mass bounded by the same constant. It follows by Prokhorov’s theorem that this sequence is pre-compact, and therefore admits a subsequential limit with respect to the weak convergence of measures. By continuity of the functional being maximized in (
5.4
), this weak limit must be a maximizer. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.2
.

If
g
satisfies (
H5
) and
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}
are signed measures, then

\bigg(\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\nu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\bigg)^{2}\leq\bigg(\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\mu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\bigg)\bigg(\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\nu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(x)\bigg).

(5.7)

Proof.

This is the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality for the non-negative definite kernel
\smash{\tilde{g}(x,y)=g(xy)}
, and can be proved in a standard way. Indeed, for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
, let

\displaystyle P(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}g(xy)\,\mathrm{d}(\mu+t\nu)(x)\,\mathrm{d}(\mu+t\nu)(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\mu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+2t\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\nu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+t^{2}\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\nu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(x).

This polynomial is non-negative by (
H5
). In particular, its discriminant cannot be positive. This means that

2^{2}\bigg(\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\nu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\bigg)^{2}-4\bigg(\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\mu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\bigg)\bigg(\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\nu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(x)\bigg)\leq 0.

Rearranging completes the proof.
∎

We are finally in a position to prove
Theorem

1.3
.

Proof of
Theorem

1.3
.

Fix
t>0
and
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
. Combining
Theorem

1.2
with (
5.4
) shows that the unique solution to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.10
) admits the Hopf-Lax variational representation (
1.44
). Moreover,
Lemma

5.1
ensures that the supremum in (
5.4
) is achieved at some
\nu^{*}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
. To establish the final statement in
Theorem

1.3
, suppose that the initial condition
\psi
admits a Gateaux derivative at the measure
\mu+t\nu^{*}
with density
x\mapsto D_{\mu}\psi(\mu+t\nu^{*},x)
in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
. For any measure
\eta\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, the Gateaux derivative of the functional

\nu\mapsto\psi(\mu+t\nu)-\frac{t}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\nu}(y)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(y)

at the measure
\nu^{*}
in the direction of
\eta-\nu^{*}
is

D_{\mu}\psi\big(\mu+t\nu^{*};t(\eta-\nu^{*})\big)-t\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}g(xy)\,\mathrm{d}(\eta-\nu^{*})(x)\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{*}(y).

(5.8)

Moreover, for every
\epsilon\in[0,1]
, we have that
\nu^{*}+\epsilon(\eta-\nu^{*})
belongs to
\mathcal{M}_{+}
, and is thus a valid candidate for the optimization problem in (
5.4
). As a consequence, the quantity in (
5.8
) must be non-positive. Using also the definition of the Gateaux derivative density in (
1.9
), we get that

t\int_{-1}^{1}\Big(D_{\mu}\psi(\mu+t\nu^{*},x)-\int_{-1}^{1}g(xy)\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{*}(y)\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\big(\eta-\nu^{*}\big)(x)\leq 0,

(5.9)

for every
\eta\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
. The assumption that the density
x\mapsto D_{\mu}\psi(\mu+t\nu^{*},x)
belongs to the cone
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
gives a measure
\eta^{*}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
with

\smash{G_{\eta^{*}}(x)=D_{\mu}\psi(\mu+t\nu^{*},x)}
. Applying (
5.9
) to the measure
\eta=\eta^{*}
reveals that

\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}g(xy)\,\mathrm{d}(\eta^{*}-\nu^{*}\big)(y)\,\mathrm{d}(\eta^{*}-\nu^{*}\big)(x)\leq 0.

Together with (
1.43
), this implies that

\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}g(xy)\,\mathrm{d}(\eta^{*}-\nu^{*}\big)(y)\,\mathrm{d}(\eta^{*}-\nu^{*}\big)(x)=0.

Applying
Lemma

5.2
to the signed measures
\eta^{*}-\nu^{*}
and
\delta_{x}
for some
x\in[-1,1]
shows that

\int_{-1}^{1}g(xy)\,\mathrm{d}\big(\eta^{*}-\nu^{*}\big)(y)=\int_{-1}^{1}g(yz)\,\mathrm{d}\big(\eta^{*}-\nu^{*}\big)(y)\,\mathrm{d}\delta_{x}(z)=0.

Rearranging gives
G_{\nu^{*}}(x)=G_{\eta^{*}}(x)=D_{\mu}(\mu+t\nu^{*},x)
. Since
x\in[-1,1]
is arbitrary, this completes the proof.
∎

6
The infinite-dimensional equation with arbitrary kernel

In this section, we extend the main results of this paper to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.10
) associated with a kernel
g
that is not necessarily assumed to be positive. Fix
b\in\mathbb{R}
, and recall the definition of the modified kernel
\smash{\widetilde{g}_{b}}
in (
1.51
), of the modified Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.55
) and of the modified solution
\smash{f_{b}}
in (
1.57
). Introduce the symmetric matrix

\widetilde{G}^{(K)}_{b}=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert^{2}}\big(\widetilde{g}_{b}(kk^{\prime})\big)_{k,k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}\times\mathcal{D}_{K}}

(6.1)

the projected cone

\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b,K}=\Big\{\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}x^{(K)}(\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\mid\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}^{(K)}\Big\}=\Big\{\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}x\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\mid x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\Big\}

(6.2)

and the projected non-linearity
\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{b,K}:\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b,K}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{b,K}\big(\widetilde{G}^{(K)}_{b}x\big)=\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}x\cdot x=\frac{1}{2\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert^{2}}\sum_{k,k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}\widetilde{g}_{b}(kk^{\prime})x_{k}x_{k^{\prime}}.

(6.3)

Also define the closed convex set

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{=a,b,K}=\Big\{\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}x\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\mid x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\text{ and }{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}=a\Big\}

(6.4)

The first order of business will be to verify that the function
\smash{f_{b}}
is well-defined by ensuring that (
1.32
)-(
H4
) are satisfied in the context of the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.55
).

Lemma 6.1
.

Under the assumptions of
Theorem

1.4
, the kernel
\smash{\widetilde{g}_{b}}
in (
1.51
) and the initial condition
\smash{\widetilde{\psi}_{b}}
in (
1.56
) satisfy (
1.32
)-(
H4
). Moreover, each projected initial condition
\smash{\widetilde{\psi}_{b}^{(K)}}
has its gradient in
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{=a,b,K}}
.

Proof.

The kernel
\widetilde{g}_{b}
satisfies (
1.32
) by the choice of
b
, while the initial condition
\widetilde{\psi}_{b}
satisfies (
H2
) by the triangle inequality and the bound

\Big\lvert ab\int_{-1}^{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu-ab\int_{-1}^{1}\,\mathrm{d}\nu\Big\rvert\leq a\lvert b\rvert\big\lvert\mu[-1,1]-\nu[-1,1]\big\rvert\leq a\lvert b\rvert\mathrm{TV}(\mu,\nu).

An identical argument shows that the initial condition
\widetilde{\psi}_{b}
satisfies (
H4
). To verify (
1.34
), introduce the closed convex set

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{a,b,K}=\Big\{\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}x\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\mid x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\text{ and }{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq a\Big\},

and fix
c\in\mathbb{R}
and
\smash{x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
such that
(x^{\prime}-x)\cdot z\geq c
for every
\smash{z\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{a,b,K}^{\prime}}
. Now fix
\smash{y\in\mathcal{K}_{=a,K}^{\prime}}
, and represent it as
y=G^{(K)}u+w
for some
\smash{u\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
and
\smash{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
with
\smash{{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|u|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}=a}
and
\smash{{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|w|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq 2^{-K/2}}
. If
\smash{z=\widetilde{G}^{(K)}_{b}u+w}
, then

z=G^{(K)}u+b{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|u|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\iota_{K}+w=y+ab\iota_{K}

for the vector
\smash{\iota_{K}=(\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert^{-1})_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
. Since
\smash{z\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{=a,b,K}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{a,b,K}^{\prime}}
,

(x^{\prime}-x)\cdot y=(x^{\prime}-x)\cdot z-(x^{\prime}-x)\cdot ab\iota_{K}\geq c-(x^{\prime}-x)\cdot ab\iota_{K}.

The assumption (
H3’
) and
Proposition

B.2
therefore imply that

\psi^{(K)}(x^{\prime})-\psi^{(K)}(x)\geq c-(x^{\prime}-x)\cdot ab\iota_{K}.

Noticing that
x\cdot\iota_{K}={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}
and rearranging reveals that

\widetilde{\psi}^{(K)}_{b}(x^{\prime})-\widetilde{\psi}^{(K)}_{b}(x)\geq c.

Together with
Proposition

B.2
, this establishes (
1.34
). Notice that this argument only needed the assumption that
(x^{\prime}-x)\cdot z\geq c
for every
\smash{z\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{=a,b,K}}
so it also shows that
\smash{\widetilde{\psi}_{b}^{(K)}}
has its gradient in
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{=a,b,K}}
by
Proposition

B.2
. This completes the proof.
∎

Together with
Proposition

2.7
this result implies that for every
R>0
, the Hamilton-Jacobi equation

\partial_{t}\widetilde{f}^{(K)}(t,x)=\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{b,K,R}\big(\nabla\widetilde{f}^{(K)}(t,x)\big)\quad\text{on}\quad\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}

(6.5)

admits a unique viscosity solution
\smash{\widetilde{f}^{(K)}_{b,R}\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{unif}}}
subject to the initial condition
\smash{\widetilde{\psi}_{b}^{(K)}}
which satisfies the Lipschitz bound

\sup_{t\geq 0}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\widetilde{f}_{b,R}^{(K)}(t,\cdot)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\widetilde{\psi}_{b}^{(K)}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}.

(6.6)

Here
\smash{\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{b,K,R}:\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\to\mathbb{R}}
denotes the extension of the non-linearity
\smash{\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{b,K}}
provided by
Proposition

2.3
. Since the projected initial condition
\smash{\widetilde{\psi}_{b}^{(K)}}
has its gradient in the closed convex set
\smash{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{=a,b,K}}
by
Lemma

6.1
, an identical argument to that in
Proposition

2.7
shows that the solution
\smash{\widetilde{f}^{(K)}_{b,R}}
also has its gradient in
\smash{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{=a,b,K}}
. Moreover,
Theorem

1.2
allows us to define the solution to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.55
) by

\widetilde{f}_{b}(t,\mu)=\lim_{K\to\infty}\widetilde{f}^{(K)}_{b,R}\big(t,x^{(K)}(\mu)\big),

(6.7)

and guarantees that this limit is independent of
R>0
provided that
\smash{R>\lVert\widetilde{\psi}_{b}\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}}
. Using the comparison principle in
Corollary

A.12
we now show that the limit defining the function (
1.57
),

f_{b}(t,\mu)=\lim_{K\to\infty}\bigg(\widetilde{f}_{b,R}^{(K)}\big(t,x^{(K)}(\mu)\big)-ab{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x^{(K)}(\mu)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}-\frac{a^{2}bt}{2}\bigg),

(6.8)

is independent of
b
by establishing
Theorem

1.4
.

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

Let
b,b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
be such that the kernels
\widetilde{g}_{b}
and
\widetilde{g}_{b^{\prime}}
are positive on
[-1,1]
, and fix
\smash{R>\lVert\widetilde{\psi}_{b}\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}+\lVert\widetilde{\psi}_{b^{\prime}}\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}}
. The idea will be to show that the function

f^{(K)}_{b,b^{\prime}}(t,x)=\widetilde{f}_{b}^{(K)}(t,x)-a(b-b^{\prime}){|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}-\frac{a^{2}(b-b^{\prime})t}{2}

satisfies the Hamilton-Jacobi equation defining
\smash{\widetilde{f}_{b^{\prime}}^{(K)}}
up to an error vanishing with
K
. We have omitted the dependence on
R
, and will continue to do so throughout this proof, as this constant will remain fixed. The equality of
f_{b}
and
f_{b^{\prime}}
will then follow from the comparison principle in
Corollary

A.12
. Consider
\smash{\phi\in C^{\infty}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\big)}
with the property that
\smash{f_{b,b^{\prime}}^{(K)}-\phi}
achieves a local maximum at the point
\smash{(t^{*},x^{*})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
. Since
\smash{\widetilde{f}_{b}^{(K)}}
is a viscosity subsolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
6.5
),

\frac{a^{2}(b-b^{\prime})}{2}+\partial_{t}\phi(t^{*},x^{*})-\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{b,K}\big(a(b-b^{\prime})\iota_{K}+\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})\big)\leq 0

for the vector
\smash{\iota_{K}=(\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert^{-1})_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
. The fact that
\smash{\widetilde{f}_{b}^{(K)}}
has its gradient in
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{=a,b,K}^{\prime}}
together with (
3.1
),
Lemma

3.1
and
Lemma

3.2
implies that

a(b-b^{\prime})\iota_{K}+\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{=a,b,K}^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|a(b-b^{\prime})\iota_{K}+\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq\lVert\widetilde{\psi}_{b}\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}.

It is therefore possible to find
\smash{u\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
and
\smash{w\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
with

a(b-b^{\prime})\iota_{K}+\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})=\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}u+w,\quad{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|u|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}=a\quad\text{and}\quad{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|w|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq\frac{1}{2^{K/2}}.

Observe that

{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}u|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|a(b-b^{\prime})\iota_{K}+\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|w|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq\lVert\widetilde{\psi}_{b}\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}+\frac{1}{2^{K/2}},

so increasing
K
if necessary, it is possible to ensure that
\smash{\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}u\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b,K}\cap B_{R}}
. It follows by the Lipschitz continuity of
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{b,K}
established in
Proposition

2.3
that

\frac{a^{2}(b-b^{\prime})}{2}+\partial_{t}\phi(t^{*},x^{*})-\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{b,K}\big(\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}u\big)\leq\frac{8RM}{2^{K/2}m^{2}}.

Observe that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{b,K}\big(\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}u\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}u\cdot u=\frac{1}{2}G^{(K)}u\cdot u+\frac{1}{2}b{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|u|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{G}_{b^{\prime}}^{(K)}u\cdot u+\frac{1}{2}(b-b^{\prime})a^{2}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{b^{\prime},K}\big(\widetilde{G}_{b^{\prime}}^{(K)}u\big)+\frac{a^{2}(b-b^{\prime})}{2}

so in fact

\partial_{t}\phi(t^{*},x^{*})-\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{b^{\prime}}^{(K)}\big(\widetilde{G}_{b^{\prime}}^{(K)}u\big)\leq\frac{8RM}{2^{K/2}m}.

To replace
\widetilde{G}_{b^{\prime}}^{(K)}u
by
\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})
observe that

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{b^{\prime}}^{(K)}u

\displaystyle=\widetilde{G}^{(K)}_{b}u+(b^{\prime}-b)\iota_{K}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|u|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}=a(b-b^{\prime})\iota_{K}+\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})-w+a(b^{\prime}-b)\iota_{K}

\displaystyle=\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})-w,

and leverage the Lipschitz continuity of
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{b^{\prime},K}
established in
Proposition

2.3
to deduce that

\big(\partial_{t}\phi-\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{b^{\prime},K}(\nabla\phi)\big)(t^{*},x^{*})\leq\mathcal{E}_{K}

for the error term

\mathcal{E}_{K}=\frac{16RM}{2^{K/2}m}.

In particular, the function
\smash{(t,x)\mapsto f_{b,b^{\prime}}^{K}(t,x)-\mathcal{E}_{K}t}
is a viscosity subsolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation defining
\smash{\widetilde{f}_{b^{\prime}}^{K}}
. An identical argument shows that
\smash{(t,x)\mapsto f_{b,b^{\prime}}^{K}(t,x)+\mathcal{E}_{K}t}
is a viscosity supersolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation defining
\smash{\widetilde{f}_{b^{\prime}}^{K}}
. It follows by the comparison principle in
Corollary

A.12
that for every
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
,

\big\lvert f_{b}^{(K)}\big(t,x^{(K)}(\mu)\big)-f_{b^{\prime}}^{(K)}\big(t,x^{(K)}(\mu)\big)\big\rvert=\big\lvert f_{b,b^{\prime}}^{(K)}\big(t,x^{(K)}(\mu)\big)-\widetilde{f}_{b^{\prime}}^{(K)}\big(t,x^{(K)}(\mu)\big)\big\rvert\leq\mathcal{E}_{K}t.

Letting
K
tend to infinity completes the proof.
∎

Combining this well-posedness result with the Hopf-Lax representation formula in
Theorem

1.3
we now prove the Hopf-Lax variational representation for
f=f_{b}
stated in
Theorem

1.5
.

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

Fix
b>0
large enough so the kernel
\widetilde{g}_{b}
is positive on
[-1,1]
and satisfies (
H5
).
Lemma

6.1
and the Hopf-Lax representation formula in
Theorem

1.3
imply that for any
t>0
and
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
,

\widetilde{f}_{b}(t,\mu)=\sup_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}}\bigg\{\widetilde{\psi}_{b}(\mu+t\nu)-\frac{t}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\widetilde{G}_{b,\nu}(y)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(y)\bigg\}.

(6.9)

Since the Gateaux derivative density
x\mapsto D_{\mu}\psi(\mu+t\nu)
belongs to the set
\mathcal{C}_{a,\infty}
by assumption, there exists a measure
\eta\in\mathcal{M}_{a,+}
with
D_{\mu}\psi(\mu+t\nu,\cdot)=G_{\eta}
. This means that

D_{\mu}\widetilde{\psi}_{b}(\mu+t\nu,x)=D_{\mu}\psi(\mu+t\nu,x)+ab=\int_{-1}^{1}g(xy)\,\mathrm{d}\eta(y)+ab=\int_{-1}^{1}\widetilde{g}_{b}(xy)\,\mathrm{d}\eta(y),

so another application of
Theorem

1.3
implies that the supremum in (
6.9
) is achieved at some
\nu^{*}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
with

\widetilde{G}_{b,\nu^{*}}=D_{\mu}\widetilde{\psi}_{b}(\mu+t\nu^{*},\cdot)=\widetilde{G}_{b,\eta}.

Evaluating this equality at
x=0
reveals that

\widetilde{g}_{b}(0)\int_{-1}^{1}\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{*}(y)=\widetilde{g}_{b}(0)\int_{-1}^{1}\,\mathrm{d}\eta(y)=\widetilde{g}_{b}(0)a.

Since
\widetilde{g}_{b}(0)>0
by the choice of
b
, this means that
\nu^{*}\in\mathcal{M}_{a,+}
and

\widetilde{f}_{b}(t,\mu)=\sup_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{a,+}}\bigg\{\widetilde{\psi}_{b}(\mu+t\nu)-\frac{t}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\widetilde{G}_{b,\nu}(y)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(y)\bigg\}.

It follows by (
1.57
) that

\displaystyle f_{b}(t,\mu)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{a,+}}\bigg\{\widetilde{\psi}_{b}(\mu+t\nu)-\frac{t}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\widetilde{G}_{b,\nu}(y)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(y)-ab\int_{-1}^{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu-\frac{a^{2}bt}{2}\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{a,+}}\bigg\{\psi(\mu+t\nu)+abt\int_{-1}^{1}\,\mathrm{d}\nu-\frac{t}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\nu}(y)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(y)-\frac{bt}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}\,\mathrm{d}\nu\,\mathrm{d}\nu-\frac{a^{2}bt}{2}\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{a,+}}\bigg\{\psi(\mu+t\nu)-\frac{t}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\nu}(y)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(y)\bigg\}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix A
Hamilton-Jacobi equations on positive half-spaces

In this appendix we fix a non-linearity
\mathsf{H}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and an initial condition
\psi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
with the properties that

A1

the non-linearity
\mathsf{H}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is Lipschitz continuous with respect to the normalized-
\ell^{1,*}
norm,

\lvert\mathsf{H}(y)-\mathsf{H}(y^{\prime})\rvert\leq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\mathsf{H}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1,*}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y-y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}

(A.1)

for all
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
;

A2

the non-linearity is non-decreasing,

\mathsf{H}(y)\leq\mathsf{H}(y^{\prime})

(A.2)

for all
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
y\leq y^{\prime}
;

A3

the initial condition
\psi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is Lipschitz continuous with respect to the normalized-
\ell^{1}
norm,

\lvert\psi(x)-\psi(x^{\prime})\rvert\leq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}

(A.3)

for all
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
;

and we establish the well-posedness of the Hamilton-Jacobi equations

\partial_{t}f(t,x)=\mathsf{H}\big(\nabla f(t,x)\big)\quad\text{on}\quad\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}

(A.4)

and

\partial_{t}f(t,x)=\mathsf{H}\big(\nabla f(t,x)\big)\quad\text{on}\quad\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}

(A.5)

subject to the initial condition
\psi
. The appropriate notion of solution for these equations will be that of a viscosity solution as described in
Definition

2.6
. To establish the well-posedness of these equations we will closely follow
[
13
]
. We found it useful to provide full details when applying Perron’s method, although our arguments will certainly be seen as classical by experts, and we hope that the reader will also find these details helpful. To be more specific, first, we will use the assumptions (
A1
) and (
A3
) to prove a comparison principle for the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
) which will ensure the uniqueness of solutions to this equation. This will be the content of Section
A.1
. In Section
A.2
, we will combine (
A1
)-(
A3
) with the classical Perron method to obtain the existence of a solution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
). Finally, in Section
A.3
, we will leverage (
A2
) to show that solutions to (
A.4
) and (
A.5
) coincide. In the last section of this appendix we will show that the unique solution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equations (
A.4
) and (
A.5
) preserves the monotonicity of its initial condition. It will be convenient to remember the definition of the function spaces (
1.39
) and (
1.40
).

A.1
Comparison principle and Lipschitz continuity of solutions on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}

In this section, we use the arguments in Proposition 3.2 of
[
29
]
to obtain a comparison principle on
\smash{\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{unif}}}
for the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
). Together with the observation that any solution in
\smash{\mathfrak{L}}
to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
) is uniformly Lipschitz continuous with Lipschitz constant bounded by that of its initial condition, this comparison principle will imply the uniqueness of solutions in
\smash{\mathfrak{L}}
to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
). The Lipschitz continuity of the solutions to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
) will be obtained using the arguments in Proposition 3.4 of
[
29
]
. For every
r\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote the positive part of
r
by
r_{+}=\max(r,0)
.

Proposition A.1
.

Fix a non-linearity
\mathsf{H}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying (
A1
), and let
\smash{u,v\in\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{unif}}}
be a subsolution and a supersolution to (
A.5
). Write
\smash{L=\max\big(\sup_{t>0}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|u(t,\cdot)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1},\sup_{t>0}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|v(t,\cdot)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}\big)}
and
\smash{V={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\mathsf{H}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1,*}}
. For every
Q>2L
and all
R\in\mathbb{R}
, the map

(t,x)\mapsto u(t,x)-v(t,x)-Q\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}+Vt-R\big)_{+}

(A.6)

achieves its supremum on
\smash{\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
.

Proof.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists
T>0
with

\sup_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\big(u(t,x)-v(t,x)-\varphi(t,x)\big)>\sup_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\big(u(0,x)-v(0,x)-\varphi(0,x)\big),

(A.7)

where
\smash{\varphi(t,x)=Q\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}+Vt-R\big)_{+}}
. The proof proceeds in three steps: first we smoothen and perturb (
A.7
), then we use a variable doubling argument to obtain a system of inequalities, and finally we contradict this system of inequalities.

Step 1: smoothing and perturbing.

Given
\epsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
to be determined, let
\theta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be an increasing function with

(r-\epsilon_{0})_{+}\leq\theta(r)\leq r_{+}

for all
r\in\mathbb{R}
. Consider the smoothed normalized-
\ell^{1}
norm,

{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\big(x_{k}^{2}+\epsilon_{0}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}},

and introduce the function

\Phi(t,x)=Q\theta\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}+Vt-R\big)

defined on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
. The choice of
\theta
and the bound
(a+b)_{+}\leq a_{+}+b_{+}
imply that

\displaystyle\varphi(t,x)

\displaystyle\leq\Phi(t,x)+Q\epsilon_{0}\leq\varphi(t,x)+Q\epsilon_{0}^{1/2}+Q\epsilon_{0},

where we have used that
(a+b)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq a^{\frac{1}{2}}+b^{\frac{1}{2}}
for
a,b>0
.
It follows by (
A.7
) that

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\big(u(0,x)-v(0,x)-\Phi(0,x)\big)

\displaystyle<\sup_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\big(u(t,x)-v(t,x)-\Phi(t,x)\big)+Q\epsilon_{0}+Q\epsilon_{0}^{1/2},

so choosing
\epsilon_{0}>0
small enough guarantees that

\sup_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\big(u(t,x)-v(t,x)-\Phi(t,x)\big)>\sup_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\big(u(0,x)-v(0,x)-\Phi(0,x)\big).

(A.8)

This is a smoothed version of the absurd hypothesis (
A.7
). For technical reasons, it will be convenient to perturb the function
\Phi
. Given
\epsilon>0
to be determined, introduce the function

\chi(t,x)=\Phi(t,x)+\frac{\epsilon}{T-t},

defined on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
. Choosing
\epsilon>0
small enough ensures that

\sup_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\big(u(t,x)-v(t,x)-\chi(t,x)\big)>\sup_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\big(u(0,x)-v(0,x)-\chi(0,x)\big).

(A.9)

This is a smoothed and perturbed version of the absurd hypothesis (
A.7
).

Step 2: system of inequalities.

For each
\alpha\geq 1
, define the function
\Psi_{\alpha}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
by

\Psi_{\alpha}(t,x,t^{\prime},x^{\prime})=u(t,x)-v(t^{\prime},x^{\prime})-\frac{\alpha}{2}\big(\lvert t-t^{\prime}\rvert^{2}+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}\big)-\chi(t,x).

(A.10)

By doubling the variables and introducing the potential in this way, we ensure that whenever
\alpha>4(L+1)
, the function
\Psi_{\alpha}
achieves its supremum at a point
(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})
which remains bounded as
\alpha
tends infinity. Indeed, if we write
C>0
for a constant that depends on
T
,
Q
,
R
,
V
,
u(0,0)
,
[u]_{0}
,
v(0,0)
and
[v]_{0}
whose value might not be the same at each occurrence, then for any
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
with
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}>R+1
, the bound
\smash{\Phi(t,x)\geq Q({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}+Vt-R-1)_{+}}
reveals that

\displaystyle\Psi_{\alpha}(t,x,t^{\prime},x^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq u(0,x)-v(0,x^{\prime})-\frac{\alpha}{2}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}-\Phi(t,x)+C

\displaystyle\leq L\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\big)-\frac{\alpha}{2}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}-Q{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}+C

\displaystyle\leq(2L-Q){|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}+\Big(L-\frac{\alpha}{2}\Big){|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}+C

\displaystyle\leq(2L-Q){|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}+C.

Remembering that
Q>2L
and observing that the supremum of (
A.10
) is bounded from below by
\smash{\Psi_{\alpha}(0,0,0,0)}
ensures that this upper semi-continuous function achieves its supremum at some point
\smash{(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})}
which remains bounded with respect to the normalized-
\ell^{1}
norm as
\alpha
tends to infinity. In particular, the potential

\alpha\big(\lvert t_{\alpha}-t_{\alpha}^{\prime}\rvert^{2}+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{\alpha}-x_{\alpha}^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\big)\leq\alpha\big(\lvert t_{\alpha}-t_{\alpha}^{\prime}\rvert^{2}+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{\alpha}-x_{\alpha}^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}\big)

must remain bounded as
\alpha
tends to infinity. It follows that, up to the extraction of a subsequence, there exist
t_{0}\in[0,T]
and
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
such that
t_{\alpha}\to t_{0}
,
t_{\alpha}^{\prime}\to t_{0}
,
x_{\alpha}\to x_{0}
and
x_{\alpha}^{\prime}\to x_{0}
as
\alpha\to\infty
. The term
\smash{\frac{\epsilon}{T-t}}
in the definition of
\chi
guarantees that
t_{0}\in[0,T)
. On the other hand, the semi-continuity of
u
,
v
and
\chi
together with the bounds

\big(u-v-\chi\big)(t,x)\leq\Psi_{\alpha}(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})\leq u(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})-v(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})-\chi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})

imply that

(u-v-\chi)(t_{0},x_{0})=\sup_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}(u-v-\chi).

By (
A.9
) it must be the case that
t_{0}\in(0,T)
. This means that
t_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{\prime}\in(0,T)
for all
\alpha
large enough. We have therefore found a sequence of quadruples
((t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime}))_{\alpha}
with
t_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{\prime}\in(0,T)
such that
\Psi_{\alpha}
achieves its supremum at
(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})
for
\alpha
large enough. With this in mind, fix
\alpha\geq 1
large enough, and introduce the smooth functions
\smash{\phi,\phi^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}\big((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\big)}
defined by

\displaystyle\phi(t,x)

\displaystyle=v(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})+\frac{\alpha}{2}\big(\lvert t-t_{\alpha}^{\prime}\rvert^{2}+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x_{\alpha}^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}\big)+\chi(t,x),

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}(t^{\prime},x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=u(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})-\frac{\alpha}{2}\big(\lvert t^{\prime}-t_{\alpha}\rvert^{2}+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x^{\prime}-x_{\alpha}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}\big)-\chi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}).

Since
(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})
maximizes
\Psi_{\alpha}
, the function
u-\phi
achieves a local maximum at the point
\smash{(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
while
v-\phi^{\prime}
achieves a local minimum at
\smash{(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
. It follows by definition of a viscosity solution that

\big(\partial_{t}\phi-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\phi)\big)(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})\leq 0\quad\text{and}\quad\big(\partial_{t}\phi^{\prime}-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\phi^{\prime})\big)(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})\geq 0.

(A.11)

This is the system of inequalities that we now strive to contradict.

Step 3: reaching a contradiction.

A direct computation gives

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}\phi-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\phi)\big)(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\alpha(t_{\alpha}-t_{\alpha}^{\prime})+\partial_{t}\Phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})+\frac{\epsilon}{(T-t)^{2}}-\mathsf{H}\big(\nabla\phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})\big),

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}\phi^{\prime}-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\phi^{\prime})\big)(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\alpha(t_{\alpha}-t_{\alpha}^{\prime})-\mathsf{H}\big(\nabla\phi^{\prime}(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})\big),

where

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{k}}\phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{2d}\cdot\frac{\big(x_{\alpha}-x_{\alpha}^{\prime}\big)_{k}}{((x_{\alpha}-x_{\alpha}^{\prime})_{k}^{2}+\epsilon_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\partial_{x_{k}}\Phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}),

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{k}}\phi^{\prime}(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{2d}\cdot\frac{\big(x_{\alpha}-x_{\alpha}^{\prime}\big)_{k}}{((x_{\alpha}-x_{\alpha}^{\prime})_{k}^{2}+\epsilon_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

It follows by the definition of
\smash{V={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\mathsf{H}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1,*}}
that

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}\phi^{\prime}-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\phi^{\prime})\big)(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\alpha(t_{\alpha}-t_{\alpha}^{\prime})-\mathsf{H}\big(\nabla\phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})\big)+V{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\nabla\phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}))-\nabla\phi^{\prime}(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha(t_{\alpha}-t_{\alpha}^{\prime})-\mathsf{H}\big(\nabla\phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})\big)+Vd\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\lvert\partial_{x_{k}}\Phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})\rvert.

A direct computation shows that
Vd\lvert\partial_{x_{k}}\Phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})\rvert\leq\partial_{t}\Phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})
, so this can be bounded further by

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}\phi^{\prime}-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\phi^{\prime})\big)(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\alpha(t_{\alpha}-t_{\alpha}^{\prime})+\partial_{t}\Phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})-\mathsf{H}\big(\nabla\phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})\big)

\displaystyle<\big(\partial_{t}\phi-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\phi)\big)(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})\leq 0,

where the strict inequality is due to the term
\frac{\epsilon}{(T-t)^{2}}
and the final inequality leverages the first inequality in (
A.11
). This contradicts the second inequality in (
A.11
) and completes the proof.
∎

Corollary A.2
.

Fix a non-linearity
\mathsf{H}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying (
A1
). If
\smash{u,v\in\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{unif}}}
are respectively a subsolution and a supersolution to (
A.5
), then

\sup_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\big(u(t,x)-v(t,x)\big)=\sup_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\big(u(0,x)-v(0,x)\big).

(A.12)

Proof.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists
(t^{*},x^{*})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
with

u(t^{*},x^{*})-v(t^{*},x^{*})>\sup_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\big(u(0,x)-v(0,x)\big).

Applying the comparison principle in
Proposition

A.1
with
R={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x^{*}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}+Vt^{*}
yields a contradiction and completes the proof.
∎

Proposition A.3
.

Fix a non-linearity
\mathsf{H}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying (
A1
). If
\smash{f\in\mathfrak{L}}
is a viscosity solution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
), then

\sup_{t\geq 0}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|f(t,\cdot)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|f(0,\cdot)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}.

(A.13)

Proof.

Let
L={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|f(0,\cdot)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}
, and suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists
T>0
with

\displaystyle\sup_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\big(f(t,x)-f(t,x^{\prime})-L

\displaystyle{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\big)

\displaystyle>0\geq\sup_{x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\big(f(0,x)-f(0,x^{\prime})-L{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\big).

(A.14)

The proof proceeds in three steps: first we perturb (
A.1
), then we use a variable doubling argument to obtain a system of inequalities, and finally we contradict this system of inequalities.

Step 1: perturbing.

Given
\epsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
to be determined, let
\theta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be an increasing function with

(r-\epsilon_{0})_{+}\leq\theta(r)\leq r_{+}

for all
r\in\mathbb{R}
, and consider the smoothed normalized-
\ell^{1}
norm,

{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\big(x_{k}^{2}+\epsilon_{0}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

For a constant
R\in\mathbb{R}
to be chosen,
Q>2L
and
V={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\mathsf{H}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1,*}
, introduce the function

\Phi(t,x)=Q\theta\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}+Vt-R\big)

defined on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
. Given
\epsilon>0
to be determined, consider the functions

u_{\epsilon}(t,x)=f(t,x)-\Phi(t,x)-\frac{\epsilon}{T-t}\quad\text{and}\quad v(t,x)=f(t,x)+\Phi(t,x)

defined on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
. Remembering (
A.1
) and choosing
R>0
large enough and
\epsilon,\epsilon_{0}>0
small enough guarantees that

\displaystyle\sup_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\big(u_{\epsilon}(t,x)-v(t,x^{\prime})-L

\displaystyle{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}\big)

\displaystyle>\sup_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\big(u_{\epsilon}(0,x)-v(0,x^{\prime})-L{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\big).

(A.15)

This is a perturbed version of the absurd hypothesis (
A.1
). Before moving onto the variable doubling argument, observe that
u_{\epsilon}
is a viscosity subsolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
) while
v
is a viscosity supersolution to this equation. Indeed, fix
\smash{\phi\in C^{\infty}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d})}
with the property that
u_{\epsilon}-\phi
has a local maximum at
(t^{*},x^{*})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
. This means that the map

(t,x)\mapsto f(t,x)-\Big(\phi(t,x)+\Phi(t,x)+\frac{\epsilon}{T-t}\Big)

has a local maximum at
(t^{*},x^{*})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
. It follows by the viscosity subsolution criterion for
f
that

\partial_{t}\phi(t^{*},x^{*})+\partial_{t}\Phi(t^{*},x^{*})+\frac{\epsilon}{(T-t^{*})^{2}}-\mathsf{H}\big(\nabla(\phi+\Phi)\big)(t^{*},x^{*})\leq 0.

A direct computation shows that
dV\lvert\partial_{x_{k}}\Phi(t^{*},x^{*})\rvert\leq\partial_{t}\Phi(t^{*},x^{*})
, so the definition of
\smash{V={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\mathsf{H}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1,*}}
implies that

\big(\partial_{t}\phi-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\phi)\big)(t^{*},x^{*})\leq\partial_{t}\phi(t^{*},x^{*})-\mathsf{H}\big(\nabla(\phi+\Phi)\big)(t^{*},x^{*})+V{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\nabla\Phi(t^{*},x^{*})|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq 0

which means that
u_{\epsilon}
is a viscosity subsolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
). An identical argument reveals that
v
is a viscosity supersolution to this equation.

Step 2: system of inequalities.

Fix
\delta\in(0,1)
, and for each
\alpha\geq 1
define the function
\Psi_{\alpha}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
by

\Psi_{\alpha}(t,x,t^{\prime},x^{\prime})=u_{\epsilon}(t,x)-v(t^{\prime},x^{\prime})-\frac{\alpha}{2}\lvert t-t^{\prime}\rvert^{2}-(L+\delta t){|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}.

(A.16)

By doubling the variables and introducing the potential in this way, we ensure that the function
\Psi_{\alpha}
achieves its supremum at a point
(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})
which remains bounded as
\alpha
tends infinity. Indeed, if we write
C>0
for a constant that depends on
T
,
Q
,
R
,
V
,
f(0,0)
and
[f]_{0}
whose value might not be the same at each occurrence, then for any
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
with
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}>R+1
,

\Psi_{\alpha}(t,x,t^{\prime},x^{\prime})\leq L\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\big)-\Phi(t,x)-L{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}+C\leq(2L-Q){|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}+C

where we have used the bound
\smash{\Phi(t,x)\geq Q({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}+Vt-R-1)_{+}}
. An analogous bound can be obtained with
x
replaced by
x^{\prime}
. Remembering that
Q>2L
and observing that the supremum of (
A.16
) is bounded from below by
\smash{\Psi_{\alpha}(0,0,0,0)}
ensures that this function achieves its supremum at some point
\smash{(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})}
which remains bounded with respect to the normalized-
\ell^{1}
norm as
\alpha
tends to infinity. In particular, the term
\smash{\alpha\lvert t-t^{\prime}\rvert^{2}}
must remain bounded as
\alpha
tends to infinity. It follows that, up to the extraction of a subsequence, there exist
t_{0}\in[0,T]
and
x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
such that
t_{\alpha}\to t_{0}
,
t_{\alpha}^{\prime}\to t_{0}
,
x_{\alpha}\to x_{0}
and
x_{\alpha}^{\prime}\to x_{0}^{\prime}
as
\alpha\to\infty
. The term
\smash{\frac{\epsilon}{T-t}}
in the definition of
u_{\epsilon}
guarantees that
t_{0}\in[0,T)
. On the other hand, if
\smash{C_{1}>T({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{\alpha}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{\alpha}^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}})}
for all
\alpha\geq 1
, then the semi-continuity of
u
together with the bound

\Psi_{\alpha}(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})\geq-C_{1}\delta+\sup_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\big(u_{\epsilon}(t,x)-v(t,x^{\prime})-L{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}\big)

implies that

\Psi_{\alpha}(t_{0},x_{0},t_{0},x_{0}^{\prime})\geq-C_{1}\delta+\sup_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\big(u_{\epsilon}(t,x)-v(t,x^{\prime})-L{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}\big).

(A.17)

To leverage this bound, observe that

\displaystyle\Psi_{\alpha}(0,x_{0},0,x_{0}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=u_{\epsilon}(0,x_{0})-v(0,x_{0}^{\prime})-(L+\delta t){|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{0}-x_{0}^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\big(u_{\epsilon}(0,x)-v(0,x^{\prime})-L{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\big)

(A.18)

and

\displaystyle\Psi_{\alpha}(t_{0},x_{0},t_{0},x_{0})

\displaystyle=u_{\epsilon}(t_{0},x_{0})-v(t_{0},x_{0})\leq\sup_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\Big(-2\Phi(t,x)-\frac{\epsilon}{T-t}\Big).

Since
\theta
is non-decreasing this can be bounded further by
