\Psi_{\alpha}(0,x_{0},0,x_{0}^{\prime})\leq\sup_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\Big(-2\Phi(0,x)-\frac{\epsilon}{T}\Big)\leq\sup_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\big(u_{\epsilon}(0,x)-v(0,x^{\prime})-L{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\big),

(A.19)

where we have used the fact that
\smash{-2\Phi(0,x)-\frac{\epsilon}{T}=u_{\epsilon}(0,x)-v(0,x)}
. Combining (
A.17
), (
A.1
) and (
A.19
) with the absurd assumption (
A.1
) and choosing
\delta
small enough shows that
t_{0}\in(0,T)
and
x_{0}\neq x_{0}^{\prime}
. This means that, taking a subsequence if necessary, it is possible to guarantee that
t_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{\prime}\in(0,T)
and
x_{\alpha}\neq x_{\alpha}^{\prime}
for all
\alpha
large enough. We have therefore found a sequence of quadruples
((t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime}))_{\alpha}
with
t_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{\prime}\in(0,T)
and
x_{\alpha}\neq x_{\alpha}^{\prime}
such that
\Psi_{\alpha}
achieves its supremum at
(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})
for
\alpha
large enough. With this in mind, fix
\alpha\geq 1
large enough, and introduce the smooth functions
\smash{\phi,\phi^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}\big((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\big)}
defined by

\displaystyle\phi(t,x)

\displaystyle=v(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})+\frac{\alpha}{2}\lvert t-t_{\alpha}^{\prime}\rvert^{2}+(L+\delta t){|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x_{\alpha}^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}},

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}(t^{\prime},x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=u_{\epsilon}(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})-\frac{\alpha}{2}\lvert t^{\prime}-t_{\alpha}\rvert^{2}-(L+\delta t_{\alpha}){|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{\alpha}-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}.

Since
(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})
maximizes
\Psi_{\alpha}
, the function
u_{\epsilon}-\phi
achieves a local maximum at the point
\smash{(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
while
v-\phi^{\prime}
achieves a local minimum at
\smash{(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
. It follows by the observation that
u_{\epsilon}
is a viscosity subsolution while
v
is a viscosity supersolution that

\big(\partial_{t}\phi-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\phi)\big)(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})\leq 0\quad\text{and}\quad\big(\partial_{t}\phi^{\prime}-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\phi^{\prime})\big)(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})\geq 0.

(A.20)

This is the system of inequalities that we now strive to contradict.

Step 3: reaching a contradiction.

A direct computation gives

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}\phi-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\phi)\big)(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\alpha(t_{\alpha}-t_{\alpha}^{\prime})+\delta{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x_{\alpha}^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}-\mathsf{H}\big(\nabla\phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})\big),

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}\phi^{\prime}-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\phi^{\prime})\big)(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\alpha(t_{\alpha}-t_{\alpha}^{\prime})-\mathsf{H}\big(\nabla\phi^{\prime}(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})\big),

where

\partial_{x_{k}}\phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})=\frac{L+\delta t_{\alpha}}{d}\cdot\frac{\big(x_{\alpha}-x_{\alpha}^{\prime}\big)_{k}}{((x_{\alpha}-x_{\alpha}^{\prime})_{k}^{2}+\epsilon_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}}=\partial_{x_{k}}\phi^{\prime}(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime}).

It follows by the second inequality in (
A.20
) that

\big(\partial_{t}\phi-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\phi)\big)(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})=\big(\partial_{t}\phi^{\prime}-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\phi^{\prime})\big)(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})+\delta{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x_{\alpha}^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}>0.

This contradicts the first inequality in (
A.20
) and completes the proof.
∎

Corollary A.4
.

Fix a non-linearity
\mathsf{H}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and an initial condition
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying (
A1
) and (
A3
). If
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathfrak{L}
are viscosity solutions to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
) with initial condition
\psi
, then
f_{1}=f_{2}
.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of
Proposition

A.3
and
Corollary

A.2
.
∎

A.2
Existence of solutions on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}

In this section, we fix a non-linearity
\mathsf{H}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and an initial condition
\psi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying (
A1
)-(
A3
), and we use the classical Perron method to establish the existence of solutions to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
). We closely follow the arguments in Chapter 5 of
[
2
]
. It will be convenient to fix a positive constant

K>\sup\big\{\lvert\mathsf{H}(y)\rvert\mid y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ with }{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}\big\},

(A.21)

and to define the continuous functions
\underline{u},\overline{u}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\underline{u}(t,x)=\psi(x)-Kt\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{u}(t,x)=\psi(x)+Kt.

(A.22)

The importance of these functions stems from the fact that they are a viscosity subsolution and a viscosity supersolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
), respectively.

Lemma A.5
.

Fix a non-linearity
\mathsf{H}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and an initial condition
\psi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying (
A1
)-(
A3
). The functions
\underline{u}
and
\overline{u}
defined in (
A.22
) are a subsolution and a supersolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
), respectively.

Proof.

Consider a smooth function
\smash{\phi\in C^{\infty}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\big)}
with the property that
\underline{u}-\phi
has a local maximum at a point
(t^{*},x^{*})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
. For any
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
and every
\epsilon>0
,

\phi(t^{*},x^{*}+\epsilon x)-\phi(t^{*},x^{*})\geq\underline{u}(t^{*},x^{*}+\epsilon x)-\underline{u}(t^{*},x^{*})=\psi(x^{*}+\epsilon x)-\psi(x^{*}).

Dividing by
\epsilon
and letting
\epsilon
tend to zero shows that
\smash{\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})\cdot x\geq\nabla\psi(x^{*})}\cdot x
for all
\smash{x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
. It follows that
\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})\geq\nabla\psi(x^{*})
, so (
A2
) and the fact that
t^{*}>0
imply that

\big(\partial_{t}\phi-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\phi)\big)(t^{*},x^{*})\leq\partial_{t}\underline{u}(t^{*},x^{*})-\mathsf{H}\big(\nabla\psi(x^{*})\big)=-K-\mathsf{H}\big(\nabla\psi(x^{*})\big).

(A.23)

To bound this further, observe that for every
x\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}
,
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\epsilon>0
small enough,

\psi(x+\epsilon x^{\prime})-\psi(x)\leq\epsilon{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}.

Dividing by
\epsilon
and letting
\epsilon
tend to zero gives

\nabla\psi(x)\cdot x^{\prime}\leq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}.

Choosing
x^{\prime}=d\sgn(\partial_{x_{k}}\psi(x))e_{k}
shows that
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\nabla\psi(x)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}
. It follows by continuity that
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\nabla\psi(x^{*})|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}
so (
A.23
) and the definition of
K
imply that
\underline{u}
is a subsolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
). An identical argument shows that
\overline{u}
is a supersolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
). This completes the proof.
∎

The main result of this section will be that the function
f:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

f(t,x)=\sup_{u\in\mathcal{S}}u(t,x)

(A.24)

for the set

\mathcal{S}=\big\{u:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\mid\underline{u}\leq u\leq\overline{u}\text{ and }u^{\star}\text{ is a subsolution to }\eqref{eqn: SBME app HJ eqn on Rp}\big\}

(A.25)

is a viscosity solution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
). We refer to
Appendix

B
for the definitions and basic properties of lower and upper semi-continuous envelopes of a function
u
, which we denote by
u_{\star}
and
u^{\star}
respectively. The strategy will be to show that
\smash{f^{\star}}
is a viscosity subsolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
) while
\smash{f_{\star}}
is a viscosity supersolution to this equation. The comparison principle in
Corollary

A.2
will then imply that
f
is a viscosity solution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
). Throughout this subsection, we will write

B_{r}(t^{*},x^{*})=\big\{(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\mid\lvert t-t^{*}\rvert^{2}+\lVert x-x^{*}\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq r^{2}\big\}.

(A.26)

for the Euclidean ball of radius
r>0
centered at the point
(t^{*},x^{*})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
. It is readily verified that
f^{\star}
is a subsolution.

Lemma A.6
.

If
\mathsf{H}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies (
A1
), then the upper semi-continuous envelope
f^{\star}
of the function (
A.24
) is a viscosity subsolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
). In particular,
f\in\mathcal{S}
.

Proof.

Consider a smooth function
\smash{\phi\in C^{\infty}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\big)}
with the property that
f^{\star}-\phi
has a strict local maximum at the point
\smash{(t^{*},x^{*})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
. To be more precise, suppose that

\big(f^{\star}-\phi\big)(t^{*},x^{*})>\big(f^{\star}-\phi\big)(t,x)

for all
(t,x)\in B_{r}(t^{*},x^{*})\setminus\{(t^{*},x^{*})\}
. By definition of the upper semi-continuous envelope and continuity of
\phi
, it is possible to find points
(t_{n},x_{n})\in B_{r}(t^{*},x^{*})
converging to
(t^{*},x^{*})
with

(f-\phi)(t_{n},x_{n})\geq(f^{\star}-\phi)(t^{*},x^{*})-\frac{1}{n}

for every integer
n\geq 1
. Similarly, by definition of
f
, it is possible find a sequence of functions
(u_{n})\subset\mathcal{S}
with

f(t_{n},x_{n})-\frac{1}{n}\leq u_{n}(t_{n},x_{n})

for all integer
n\geq 1
. If
(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})\in B_{r}(t^{*},x^{*})
denotes the maximum of
u_{n}^{\star}-\phi
on
B_{r}(t^{*},x^{*})
, then the fact that
u_{n}^{\star}
is a subsolution to (
A.5
) implies that

\big(\partial_{t}\phi-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\phi)\big)(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})\leq 0.

(A.27)

Notice that
u_{n}^{\star}-\phi
achieves its maximum on the compact set
B_{r}(t^{*},x^{*})
as it is an upper semi-continuous function by
Proposition

B.3
. Remembering that
u_{n}^{\star}\geq u_{n}
reveals that

(f^{\star}-\phi)(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})\geq(u_{n}^{\star}-\phi)(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})\geq(u_{n}-f)(t_{n},x_{n})+(f-\phi)(t_{n},x_{n})\geq(f^{\star}-\phi)(t^{*},x^{*})-\frac{2}{n},

where we have used that
u_{n}^{\star}\leq f^{\star}
as
u_{n}\leq f
. If
(t^{\prime}_{\infty},x^{\prime}_{\infty})
denotes any subsequential limit of
(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})
, then the upper semi-continuity of
f^{\star}
established in
Proposition

B.3
gives

(f^{\star}-\phi)(t^{\prime}_{\infty},x^{\prime}_{\infty})\geq(f^{\star}-\phi)(t^{*},x^{*}).

Since
(t^{*},x^{*})
is a strict local maximum of
f^{\star}-\phi
on
B_{r}(t^{*},x^{*})
, this implies that
(t^{\prime}_{\infty},x^{\prime}_{\infty})=(t^{*},x^{*})
. Letting
n
tend to infinity in (
A.27
) shows that
f^{\star}
is viscosity subsolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
). It is clear by the definition in (
A.24
) that
\underline{u}\leq f\leq\overline{u}
so
f\in\mathcal{S}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Showing that
f_{\star}
is a viscosity supersolution requires more work, and relies upon the following modification of Lemma 2.12 in
[
2
]
.

Lemma A.7
.

Fix a non-linearity
\mathsf{H}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying (
A1
) and (
A2
). If
u\in\mathcal{S}
is such that
u_{\star}
is not a viscosity supersolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
), then there exists
v\in\mathcal{S}
with
v(t,x)>u(t,x)
for some
(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
.

Proof.

The assumption that
u_{\star}
is not a viscosity supersolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
) gives
\smash{\phi\in C^{\infty}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\big)}
and
(t^{*},x^{*})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
with the property that
u_{\star}-\phi
has a strict local minimum at
(t^{*},x^{*})
but
\smash{(\partial_{t}\phi-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\phi))(t^{*},x^{*})<0}
.
To be more precise, we will assume that

\big(u_{\star}-\phi)(t,x)>\big(u_{\star}-\phi)(t^{*},x^{*})

(A.28)

for all
(t,x)\in B_{r}(t^{*},x^{*})\setminus\{(t^{*},x^{*})\}
and

\big(\partial_{t}\phi-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\phi)\big)(t,x)<-\epsilon

(A.29)

for some
\epsilon>0
and all
(t,x)\in B_{r}(t^{*},x^{*})
. Notice that we must have
u_{\star}(t^{*},x^{*})<\overline{u}(t^{*},x^{*})
. Indeed, if this were not the case, the assumption that
u\in\mathcal{S}
would imply that
u_{\star}(t^{*},x^{*})=\overline{u}(t^{*},x^{*})
, and therefore

\big(\overline{u}-\phi\big)(t,x)\geq\big(u_{\star}-\phi\big)(t,x)>(u_{\star}-\phi)(t^{*},x^{*})=\big(\overline{u}-\phi\big)(t^{*},x^{*})

for all
(t,x)\in B_{r}(t^{*},x^{*})\setminus\{(t^{*},x^{*})\}
. In other words, the supersolution
\overline{u}
would be such that
\overline{u}-\phi
achieves a local maximum at
(t^{*},x^{*})
. This would contradict (
A.29
). Decreasing
r>0
if necessary and using the continuity of
\phi
and
\overline{u}
, it is therefore possible to find
\delta>0
with

u_{\star}(t^{*},x^{*})+\delta<\overline{u}(t^{*},x^{*})-\delta\leq\overline{u}(t,x)\quad\text{and}\quad\phi(t,x)\leq\phi(t^{*},x^{*})+\frac{\delta}{2}

(A.30)

for all
(t,x)\in B_{r}(t^{*},x^{*})
. With this in mind, given
\epsilon^{\prime}<\frac{1}{4}\min(r^{2},\delta)
, introduce the function

w(t,x)=\phi(t,x)+\epsilon^{\prime}-\lVert x-x^{*}\rVert_{2}^{2}-\lvert t-t^{*}\rvert^{2}+(u_{\star}-\phi)(t^{*},x^{*}),

and define
v:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
by

v(t,x)=\begin{cases}\max\big(u(t,x),w(t,x)\big)&\text{ if }(t,x)\in B_{r}(t^{*},x^{*}),\\
u(t,x)&\text{ if }(t,x)\notin B_{r}(t^{*},x^{*}).\end{cases}

It is clear from the assumption
u\in\mathcal{S}
that
v\geq u\geq\underline{u}
. Moreover, for
(t,x)\in B_{r}(t^{*},x^{*})
,

w(t,x)\leq\phi(t^{*},x^{*})+\frac{\delta}{2}+\frac{\delta}{2}+(u_{\star}-\phi)(t^{*},x^{*})=u_{\star}(t^{*},x^{*})+\delta\leq\overline{u}(t,x),

where we have used (
A.30
) and the fact that
\epsilon^{\prime}\leq\delta/2
. Together with the assumption
u\in\mathcal{S}
, this shows that
v\leq\overline{u}
, and therefore
\underline{u}\leq v\leq\overline{u}
. Furthermore, the definition of the lower semi-continuous envelope gives points
(t_{n},x_{n})\in B_{r}(t^{*},x^{*})
with
(t_{n},x_{n})\to(t^{*},x^{*})
and
u(t_{n},x_{n})\to u_{\star}(t^{*},x^{*})
. Since
v\geq w
on
B_{r}(t^{*},x^{*})
, it follows that

\liminf_{n\to\infty}v(t_{n},x_{n})\geq\liminf_{n\to\infty}w(t_{n},x_{n})=\phi(t^{*},x^{*})+\epsilon^{\prime}+(u_{\star}-\phi)(t^{*},x^{*})=u_{\star}(t^{*},x^{*})+\epsilon^{\prime}.

This means that for any
n
large enough

v(t_{n},x_{n})\geq u(t_{n},x_{n})+\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}}{2}>u(t_{n},x_{n}),

so there exists a point
(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
with
v(t,x)>u(t,x)
. All that remains is to verify that
v^{\star}
is a subsolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
).
Consider
\smash{\beta\in C^{\infty}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\big)}
and
(t_{0},x_{0})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
with the property that
v^{\star}-\beta
has a strict local maximum on
B_{r^{\prime}}(t_{0},x_{0})
at
(t_{0},x_{0})
. The definition of the upper semi-continuous envelope gives points
(t_{n},x_{n})\in B_{r^{\prime}}(t_{0},x_{0})
converging to
(t_{0},x_{0})
with

v(t_{n},x_{n})\geq v^{\star}(t_{0},x_{0})-\frac{1}{n}.

(A.31)

Since
v(t_{n},x_{n})
is either equal to
w(t_{n},x_{n})
or
u(t_{n},x_{n})
, passing to a subsequence, it is possible to assume that
v(t_{n},x_{n})=w(t_{n},x_{n})
for all
n\geq 1
or that
v(t_{n},x_{n})=u(t_{n},x_{n})
for all
n\geq 1
. We treat these two cases separately.

Case 1:
v(t_{n},x_{n})=w(t_{n},x_{n})
for all
n\geq 1

In this case, we must have
(t_{n},x_{n})\in B_{r}(t^{*},x^{*})
for all
n\geq 1
. Indeed, for
(t,x)\notin B_{r/2}(t^{*},x^{*})
,

(w-u)(t,x)\leq(w-u_{\star})(t,x)\leq(\phi-u_{\star})(t,x)+\epsilon^{\prime}-\frac{r^{2}}{4}+(u_{\star}-\phi)(t^{*},x^{*})<\epsilon^{\prime}-\frac{r^{2}}{4}\leq 0

where we have used (
A.28
) and the fact that
\epsilon^{\prime}<r^{2}/4
. If
(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})
denotes the maximum of
w-\beta
on
B_{r}(t^{*},x^{*})\cap B_{r^{\prime}}(t_{0},x_{0})
, arguing as in the proof of
Lemma

A.5
shows that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\beta(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}w(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})=\partial_{t}\phi(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})-2(t_{n}^{\prime}-t^{*}),

\displaystyle\nabla\beta(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geq\nabla w(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})=\nabla\phi(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})-2(x_{n}^{\prime}-x^{*}).

It follows by (
A2
), (
A1
) and (
A.29
) that

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}\beta-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\beta)\big)(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\partial_{t}\phi(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})-\mathsf{H}\big(\nabla\phi(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})\big)+2\lvert t_{n}^{\prime}-t^{*}\rvert+2{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\mathsf{H}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1,*}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{n}^{\prime}-x^{*}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}

\displaystyle\leq-\epsilon+2\lvert t_{n}^{\prime}-t^{*}\rvert+2d{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\mathsf{H}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1,*}\lVert x_{n}^{\prime}-x^{*}\rVert_{2}.

Decreasing
r
if necessary, it is therefore possible to ensure that
\smash{\big(\partial_{t}\beta-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\beta)\big)(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})\leq 0}
. To leverage this bound, observe that by (
A.31
), the continuity of
\beta
and the fact that
(t_{n},x_{n})
converges to
(t_{0},x_{0})
,

(v^{\star}-\beta)(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})\geq(w-\beta)(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})\geq(w-\beta)(t_{n},x_{n})=(v-\beta)(t_{n},x_{n})\geq(v^{\star}-\beta)(t_{0},x_{0})-\frac{2}{n}.

In particular, any subsequential limit
(t_{\infty}^{\prime},x_{\infty}^{\prime})
of
(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})
must satisfy

(v^{\star}-\beta)(t_{\infty}^{\prime},x_{\infty}^{\prime})\geq(v^{\star}-\beta)(t_{0},x_{0})\quad\text{and}\quad\big(\partial_{t}\beta-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\beta)\big)(t_{\infty}^{\prime},x_{\infty}^{\prime})\leq 0.

Since
(t_{0},x_{0})
is a strict local maximum of
v^{\star}-\beta
on
B_{r^{\prime}}(t_{0},x_{0})
, the first of these inequalities shows that
(t_{\infty}^{\prime},x_{\infty}^{\prime})=(t_{0},x_{0})
while the second implies the required subsolution criterion.

Case 2:
v(t_{n},x_{n})=u(t_{n},x_{n})
for all
n\geq 1

In this case, let
(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})
denote the maximum of
u^{\star}-\beta
on
B_{r^{\prime}}(t_{0},x_{0})
. Since
u^{\star}
is a viscosity subsolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
),

\big(\partial_{t}\beta-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\beta)\big)(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})\leq 0.

On the other hand, the inequality
v\geq u
and (
A.31
) reveal that

\displaystyle(v^{\star}-\beta)(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geq(u^{\star}-\beta)(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})\geq(u^{\star}-\beta)(t_{n},x_{n})\geq(u-\beta)(t_{n},x_{n})=(v-\beta)(t_{n},x_{n})

\displaystyle\geq(v^{\star}-\beta)(t_{0},x_{0})-\frac{1}{n},

so any subsequential limit
(t_{\infty}^{\prime},x_{\infty}^{\prime})
of
(t_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime})
must satisfy

(v^{\star}-\beta)(t_{\infty}^{\prime},x_{\infty}^{\prime})\geq(v^{\star}-\beta)(t_{0},x_{0})\quad\text{and}\quad\big(\partial_{t}\beta-\mathsf{H}(\nabla\beta)\big)(t_{\infty}^{\prime},x_{\infty}^{\prime})\leq 0.

Since
(t_{0},x_{0})
is a strict local maximum of
v^{\star}-\beta
on
B_{r^{\prime}}(t_{0},x_{0})
, the first of these inequalities shows that
(t_{\infty}^{\prime},x_{\infty}^{\prime})=(t_{0},x_{0})
while the second implies the required subsolution criterion. This completes the proof.
∎

Corollary A.8
.

If
\mathsf{H}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies (
A1
) and (
A2
), then the lower semi-continuous envelope
f_{\star}
of the function (
A.24
) is a viscosity supersolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
).

Proof.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that
f_{\star}
is not a supersolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
). Combining
Lemma

A.6
and
Lemma

A.7
gives a function
v\in\mathcal{S}
and a point
(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
with
v(t,x)>f(t,x)
. The contradiction

f(t,x)=\sup_{u\in\mathcal{S}}u(t,x)\geq v(t,x)>f(t,x)

completes the proof.
∎

Together with
Lemma

A.6
and the comparison principle in
Corollary

A.2
, this result allows us to establish the well-posedness of the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
).

Proposition A.9
.

If
\mathsf{H}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\psi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy (
A1
)-(
A3
), then the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
) admits a unique viscosity solution
f\in\mathfrak{L}
subject to the initial condition
\psi
. Moreover,

\sup_{t>0}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|f(t,\cdot)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}.

(A.32)

Proof.

Denote by
f\in\mathfrak{L}
the function defined in (
A.24
). Combining
Lemma

A.6
and
Corollary

A.8
shows that
f^{\star}
is a viscosity subsolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
) while
f_{\star}
is a viscosity supersolution to this equation. By
Proposition

B.3
and continuity of
\underline{u}
and
\overline{u}
, it is clear that
\underline{u}\leq f_{\star}\leq f\leq f^{\star}\leq\overline{u}
. Moreover, any function
h:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\underline{u}\leq h\leq\overline{u}
satisfies the bounds

\displaystyle\psi(x)

\displaystyle=\underline{u}(0,x)\leq h(0,x)\leq\overline{u}(0,x)=\psi(x),

\displaystyle h(t,x)-h(0,x)

\displaystyle\leq\overline{u}(t,x)-\psi(x)=Kt,

\displaystyle h(0,x)-h(t,x)

\displaystyle\leq\psi(x)-\underline{u}(t,x)\leq Kt.

for all
t>0
and every
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
. In particular, we have
h\in\mathfrak{L}
, and therefore
f_{\star},f,f^{\star}\in\mathfrak{L}
. It follows by the comparison principle in
Corollary

A.2
that
f^{\star}\leq f_{\star}
. Since
f_{\star}\leq f\leq f^{\star}
by
Proposition

B.3
, we must have
f=f_{\star}=f^{\star}
. In particular, the function
f\in\mathfrak{L}
is a continuous viscosity solution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
). The uniqueness of such a viscosity solution is guaranteed by
Corollary

A.4
. Recalling
Proposition

A.3
gives the Lipschitz bound and completes the proof.
∎

A.3
Equivalence of solutions on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
and
\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}

In this section, we leverage the monotonicity assumption (
A2
) of the non-linearity to show that viscosity solutions to the Hamilton-Jacobi equations (
A.4
) and (
A.5
) coincide. Combining this with
Proposition

A.9
gives a well-posedness theory for the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.4
).

To ignore the boundary of the upper half-plane, we proceed as in Proposition 2.1 of
[
13
]
which is inspired by
[
18
,
31
]
. The main difference between
[
13
]
and
[
18
,
31
]
is in the definition of a distance-like function to the boundary of the domain on which the Hamilton-Jacobi equation is defined. Since this distance-like function will reappear in the next section when we show that the solution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
) preserves the monotonicity of its initial condition, we will define it for a general closed convex cone
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Given a closed convex cone
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, denote by

\mathcal{K}^{*}=\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid x\cdot y\geq 0\text{ for all }y\in\mathcal{K}\big\}

(A.33)

its dual cone, and define the distance-like function

\udo:\mathcal{K}^{*}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
by

\udo(x)=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}{|\kern-0.3014pt|\kern-0.3014pt|y|\kern-0.3014pt|\kern-0.3014pt|}_{1,*}=1\\
y\in\mathcal{K}\end{subarray}}y\cdot x.

(A.34)

The notion of a dual cone is reviewed in
Appendix

B
. Before stating the main properties of this distance-like function, recall that the super-differential of a function
h:\mathcal{K}^{*}\to\mathbb{R}
at a point
x\in\mathrm{int}(\mathcal{K}^{*})
is the set

\partial h(x)=\big\{p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid h(x^{\prime})\leq h(x)+p\cdot(x^{\prime}-x)+o(x^{\prime}-x)\text{ as }x^{\prime}\to x\text{ in }\mathcal{K}^{*}\big\}.

(A.35)

Lemma A.10
.

The function
\udo:\mathcal{K}^{*}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
defined in (
A.34
) satisfies the following basic properties.

1.

The infimum defining
\udo(x)
is achieved for every
x\in\mathcal{K}^{*}
.

2.

\udo(x)=0
if and only if
x\in\partial\mathcal{K}^{*}
.

3.

\udo
is Lipschitz continuous with respect to the normalized-
\ell^{1}
norm. Moreover, it has Lipschitz constant at most one.

4.

\udo
is concave and
\mathcal{K}^{*}
-non-decreasing.

5.

If
x\in\mathrm{int}(\mathcal{K}^{*})
, then
\partial\udo(x)\subset\mathcal{K}
and
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|p|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq 1
for any
p\in\partial\udo(x)
.

6.

If
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is a differentiable function and
x\mapsto h(x)-\frac{1}{\udo(x)}
achieves a local maximum at a point
x_{0}\in\mathrm{int}(\mathcal{K}^{*})
, then
\smash{-\udo(x_{0})^{2}\nabla h(x_{0})\in\partial\udo(x_{0})}
.

Proof.

We treat each property separately.

1.

Consider a sequence
(y_{n})\subset\mathcal{K}
with
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y_{n}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}=1
and
y_{n}\cdot x\to\udo(x)
. Since
(y_{n})
is uniformly bounded, it admits a subsequential limit
\smash{y\in\mathcal{K}}
with
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}=1
and
y\cdot x=\udo(x)
. We have used the equivalence and continuity of norms as well as the fact that
\mathcal{K}
is closed. This shows that the infimum in the definition of
\udo(x)
is attained.

2.

If
\udo(x)=0
, then there exists
y\in\mathcal{K}
with
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}=1
and
y\cdot x=0
. This shows that
x\in\partial\mathcal{K}^{*}
. On the other hand, if
\smash{x\in\partial\mathcal{K}^{*}}
, then there exists a non-zero
\smash{z\in\mathcal{K}}
with
z\cdot x=0
. Taking
y=z/{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|z|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}
gives
\smash{y\in\mathcal{K}}
with
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}=1
and
\udo(x)\leq y\cdot x=0
. This shows that
\udo(x)=0
.

3.

Fix
x,y\in\mathcal{K}^{*}
, and let
\smash{z\in\mathcal{K}}
with
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|z|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}=1
be such that
\udo(y)=z\cdot y
. By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\udo(x)-\udo(y)\leq z\cdot x-z\cdot y=z\cdot(y-x)\leq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|z|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y-x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y-x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}.

Reversing the roles of
x
and
y
shows that
\udo
is Lipschitz continuous with Lipschitz constant at most one.

4.

Fix
x,y\in\mathcal{K}^{*}
as well as
t\in[0,1]
, and let
z\in\mathcal{K}
achieve the infimum for
\udo(tx+(1-t)y)
. It is clear that

\udo(tx+(1-t)y)=z\cdot\big(tx+(1-t)y\big)=t(z\cdot x)+(1-t)(z\cdot y)\geq t\udo(x)+(1-t)\udo(y).

This shows that
\udo
is concave. To see that
\udo
is
\smash{\mathcal{K}^{*}}
-non-decreasing, fix
\smash{x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}^{*}}
with
\smash{x^{\prime}-x\in\mathcal{K}^{*}}
, and let
y\in\mathcal{K}
attain the infimum defining
\udo(x^{\prime})
. Since
\smash{x^{\prime}-x\in\mathcal{K}^{*}}
,

\udo(x^{\prime})-\udo(x)\geq y\cdot x^{\prime}-y\cdot x=(x^{\prime}-x)\cdot y\geq 0

as required.

5.

Fix
\smash{x\in\mathrm{int}(\mathcal{K}^{*})}
,
\smash{z\in\mathcal{K}^{*}}
and
p\in\partial\udo(x)
. Notice that
\partial\udo(x)\neq\emptyset
as
\udo
is concave. Since
\smash{\epsilon z\in\mathcal{K}^{*}}
for every
\epsilon>0
, the
\mathcal{K}^{*}
-non-decreasingness of
\udo
and the definition of the super-differential imply that

0\leq\udo(x+\epsilon z)-\udo(x)\leq p\cdot\epsilon z+o(\epsilon z).

Dividing by
\epsilon
and letting
\epsilon
tend to zero shows that
p\cdot z\geq 0
for all
\smash{z\in\mathcal{K}^{*}}
. It follows by
Proposition

B.1
that
\smash{p\in\mathcal{K}^{**}=\mathcal{K}}
. To see that
\smash{{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|p|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq 1}
for
p\in\partial\udo(x)
, find
\epsilon>0
small enough so that
\smash{x+\epsilon y\in\mathcal{K}^{*}}
for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}=1
. Fix
p\in\partial\udo(x)
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}=1
. Since
\smash{x-\epsilon y\in\mathcal{K}^{*}}
, the definition of the super-differential implies that

\udo(x-\epsilon y)\leq\udo(x)-\epsilon p\cdot y+o(\epsilon y).

Rearranging and using the
1
-Lipschitz continuity of
\udo
reveals that

\epsilon p\cdot y\leq\epsilon{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}+o(\epsilon y)=\epsilon+o(\epsilon y).

Dividing by
\epsilon
and letting
\epsilon
tend to zero shows that
p\cdot y\leq 1
for every
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}=1
. Choosing
y=d\sgn(p_{k})e_{k}
gives
\smash{{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|p|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq 1}
.

6.

Fix
z\in\mathcal{K}^{*}
. Since
x_{0}\in\mathrm{int}(\mathcal{K}^{*})
is a local maximum of the map
\smash{x\mapsto h(x)-\frac{1}{\udo(x)}}
, for every
\epsilon>0
small enough,

h(x_{0})-\frac{1}{\udo(x_{0})}\geq h(x_{0}+\epsilon z)-\frac{1}{\udo(x_{0}+\epsilon z)}.

Rearranging and using the
1
-Lipschitz continuity of
\udo
as well as the differentiability of
h
reveals that

\displaystyle\udo(x_{0}+\epsilon z)

\displaystyle\leq\udo(x_{0})-\udo(x_{0})\udo(x_{0}+\epsilon z)\big(h(x_{0}+\epsilon z)-h(x_{0})\big)

\displaystyle=\udo(x_{0})-\udo(x_{0})^{2}\nabla h(x_{0})\cdot\epsilon z+o(\epsilon z).

This shows that
-\udo(x_{0})^{2}\nabla h(x_{0})\in\partial\udo(x_{0})
and completes the proof.∎

Proposition A.11
.

If
\mathsf{H}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is a continuous non-linearity satisfying (
A2
), then a continuous function
\smash{u:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}}
is a viscosity subsolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.4
) if and only if it is a viscosity subsolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
). An identical statement holds for viscosity supersolutions.

Proof.

The argument for viscosity subsolutions and viscosity supersolutions being almost identical, we focus exclusively on the case of viscosity subsolutions. To begin with, suppose that
u
is a viscosity subsolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
), and let
\smash{\phi\in C^{\infty}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}\big)}
be a function with the property that
u-\phi
has a local maximum at
\smash{(t^{*},x^{*})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}}
. After modifying
\phi
outside of a neighborhood of
(t^{*},x^{*})
so that it becomes a smooth function defined on the larger domain
(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
, we can apply the subsolution criterion for (
A.5
) and obtain the result.

Conversely, suppose that
u
is a continuous viscosity subsolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.4
), and consider a smooth function
\smash{\phi\in C^{\infty}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\big)}
with the property that
u-\phi
has a local maximum at
\smash{(t^{*},x^{*})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
. If
\smash{x^{*}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}}
there is nothing to prove, so assume that
\smash{x^{*}\in\partial\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
. Perturbing the test function
\phi
by a small quadratic function if necessary, suppose further that
(t^{*},x^{*})
is a strict local maximum of
u-\phi
. To be more precise, assume that

u(t,x)-\phi(t,x)<u(t^{*},x^{*})-\phi(t^{*},x^{*})

(A.36)

for any
(t,x)
other than
(t^{*},x^{*})
in the closure of the open neighborhood

\BigO_{r}=(t^{*}-r,t^{*}+r)\times\big(\mathrm{int}\big(B_{r}(x^{*})\big)\cap\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}\big).

Decreasing
r>0
if necessary, it is possible to ensure that
(t^{*}-r,t^{*}+r)\subset(0,\infty)
. To establish the subsolution criterion for (
A.5
), we proceed in two steps: first we show that there exists an almost maximizer of
u-\phi
in
\BigO_{r}
, and then we use a variable doubling argument to conclude.

Step 1: almost maximizer in
\BigO_{r}
.

Introduce the distance-like function
\udo:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\udo(x)=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}{|\kern-0.3014pt|\kern-0.3014pt|y|\kern-0.3014pt|\kern-0.3014pt|}_{1,*}=1\\
y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\end{subarray}}y\cdot x.

This corresponds to the function (
A.34
) for the cone
\mathcal{K}=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}=(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d})^{*}
. For each
\epsilon>0
, define the function
\smash{\psi_{\epsilon}:(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}}
by

\psi_{\epsilon}(s,y)=u(s,y)-\phi(s,y)-\frac{\epsilon}{\udo(y)}.

Since
\psi_{\epsilon}
is upper semi-continuous with values in
\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
, there exists
\smash{(s_{\epsilon},y_{\epsilon})\in\overline{\BigO}_{r}}
with

\psi_{\epsilon}(s_{\epsilon},y_{\epsilon})=\sup_{(s,y)\in\overline{\BigO}_{r}}\psi_{\epsilon}(s,y).

Decreasing
r
if necessary and combining the continuity of
u-\phi
with (
A.36
) gives
(t,x)\in\BigO_{r}
such that

u(s,y)-\phi(s,y)<u(t,x)-\phi(t,x)

for all
\smash{(s,y)\in\overline{\BigO}_{r}}
with
\smash{s\in\{t^{*}-r,t^{*}+r\}}
or
\smash{y\in\partial B_{r}(x^{*})\cap\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
. This means that for every
\epsilon>0
small enough, we must have
\psi_{\epsilon}(s,y)<\psi_{\epsilon}(t,x)
for all
\smash{(s,y)\in\overline{\BigO}_{r}}
with
\smash{s\in\{t^{*}-r,t^{*}+r\}}
or
\smash{y\in\partial B_{r}(x^{*})\cap\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
. Together with the term
\epsilon/\udo(y)
in
\psi_{\epsilon}
, this ensures that

(s_{\epsilon},y_{\epsilon})\in\BigO_{r}
.

Step 2: doubling the variables.

Fix
\epsilon,\delta>0
, and a smooth function
\zeta_{\epsilon}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,1]
with

\supp\zeta_{\epsilon}\subset\BigO_{r}\quad\text{and}\quad\zeta_{\epsilon}(s_{\epsilon},y_{\epsilon})=1.

Given
0<\epsilon_{0}<1
to be determined, consider the smoothed normalized-
\ell^{1}
norm,

{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\big(x_{k}^{2}+\epsilon_{0}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

For each
\theta>0
introduce the modulus of continuity of
u
on
\overline{\BigO}_{r}
,

\omega_{u}(\theta)=\sup\big\{\lvert u(t,x)-u(s,y)\rvert\mid(s,x),(s,y)\in\overline{\BigO}_{r}\text{ and }\lvert t-s\rvert^{2}+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq\theta^{2}\big\}.

Since
u
is continuous, and therefore uniformly continuous on
\overline{\BigO}_{r}
, it is possible to find
\theta>0
sufficiently small that
\omega_{u}(\theta)<\delta
. With this
\theta>0
at hand, define the function
\Psi_{\epsilon,\delta,\theta}:\overline{\BigO}_{r}\times\overline{\BigO}_{r}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\Psi_{\epsilon,\delta,\theta}(t,x,s,y)=u(t,x)-\phi(s,y)-\frac{\epsilon}{\udo(y)}-\frac{2M_{u}}{\theta^{2}}\lvert t-s\rvert^{2}-\frac{2M_{u}}{\theta^{2}}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}+\delta\zeta_{\epsilon}(s,y),

where
\smash{M_{u}=\sup_{(t,x)\in\overline{\BigO}_{r}}\lvert u(t,x)\rvert}
. Observe that
\smash{\Psi_{\epsilon,\delta,\theta}(s,y,s,y)=\psi_{\epsilon}(s,y)+\delta\zeta_{\epsilon}(s,y)}
. We now show that the maximizer
(t_{0},x_{0},s_{0},y_{0})
of this function belongs to the open set
\BigO_{r}\times\BigO_{r}
. Given points
\smash{(t,x),(s,y)\in\overline{\BigO}_{r}\times\overline{\BigO}_{r}}
with
\smash{\lvert t-s\rvert^{2}+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq\theta^{2}}
, the triangle inequality and the definition of the modulus of continuity reveal that

\Psi_{\epsilon,\delta,\theta}(t,x,s,y)\leq\omega_{u}(\theta)+u(s,y)-\phi(s,y)-\frac{\epsilon}{\udo(y)}+\delta\zeta_{\epsilon}(s,y)=\omega_{u}(\theta)+\psi_{\epsilon}(s,y)+\delta\zeta_{\epsilon}(s,y).

On the other hand, for
\smash{(t,x),(s,y)\in\overline{\BigO}_{r}\times\overline{\BigO}_{r}}
with
\smash{\lvert t-s\rvert^{2}+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}>\theta^{2}}
, the triangle inequality, the bound
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}\geq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}
and the definition of
M_{u}
imply that

\Psi_{\epsilon,\delta,\theta}(t,x,s,y)\leq u(s,y)-\phi(s,y)-\frac{\epsilon}{\udo(y)}+\delta\zeta_{\epsilon}(s,y)\leq\omega_{u}(\theta)+\psi_{\epsilon}(s,y)+\delta\zeta_{\epsilon}(s,y).

It follows that for any
(t,x)\in\overline{\BigO}_{r}
and every
(s,y)\in\overline{\BigO}_{r}\setminus\supp\zeta_{\epsilon}
,

\displaystyle\Psi_{\epsilon,\delta,\theta}(t,x,s,y)

\displaystyle\leq\omega_{u}(\theta)+\psi_{\epsilon}(s_{\epsilon},y_{\epsilon})+\delta\zeta_{\epsilon}(s,y)=\omega_{u}(\theta)+\Psi_{\epsilon,\delta,\theta}(s_{\epsilon},y_{\epsilon},s_{\epsilon},y_{\epsilon})-\delta

\displaystyle<\Psi_{\epsilon,\delta,\theta}(s_{\epsilon},y_{\epsilon},s_{\epsilon},y_{\epsilon}),

where we have used that
\zeta_{\epsilon}(s_{\epsilon},y_{\epsilon})=1
and
\omega_{u}(\theta)<\delta
. This means that
(s_{0},y_{0})\in\supp\zeta_{\epsilon}\subset\BigO_{r}
. To show that
(t_{0},x_{0})
also belongs to this open set, suppose that
\lvert t_{0}-s_{0}\rvert^{2}+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{0}-y_{0}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}>\theta^{2}
. The triangle inequality, the bound
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{0}-y_{0}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}\geq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{0}-y_{0}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}
and the definition of
M_{u}
imply that

\Psi_{\epsilon,\delta,\theta}(t_{0},x_{0},s_{0},y_{0})\leq u(t_{0},x_{0})-\phi(s_{0},y_{0})-\frac{\epsilon}{\udo(y_{0})}-2M_{u}+\delta\zeta_{\epsilon}(s_{0},y_{0})\leq\Psi_{\epsilon,\delta,\theta}(s_{0},y_{0},s_{0},y_{0}),

so, up to replacing
(t_{0},x_{0})
with
(s_{0},y_{0})
, we may assume without loss of generality that

\lvert t_{0}-s_{0}\rvert^{2}+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{0}-y_{0}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq\theta^{2}.

(A.37)

Decreasing
\theta
if necessary and recalling that
\BigO_{r}
is open shows that
(t_{0},x_{0},s_{0},y_{0})\in\BigO_{r}\times\BigO_{r}
. Since the function
\smash{(t,x)\mapsto\Psi_{\epsilon,\delta,\theta}(t,x,s_{0},y_{0})}
has a local maximum at
(t_{0},x_{0})
, the subsolution criterion for (
A.4
) implies that

\frac{4M_{u}}{\theta^{2}}(t_{0}-s_{0})-\mathsf{H}\bigg(\frac{2M_{u}}{\theta^{2}}z\bigg)\leq 0

(A.38)

for the vector
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
defined by

z_{k}=\frac{(x_{0}-y_{0})_{k}}{d((x_{0}-y_{0})_{k}^{2}+\epsilon_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

On the other hand, since the function
\smash{(s,y)\mapsto\Psi_{\epsilon,\delta,\theta}(t_{0},x_{0},s,y)}
achieves its maximum at an interior point
(s_{0},y_{0})\in\BigO_{r}
, a direct computation together with
Lemma

A.10
shows that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\phi(s_{0},y_{0})-\frac{4M_{u}}{\theta^{2}}(t_{0}-s_{0})-\delta\partial_{t}\zeta_{\epsilon}(s_{0},y_{0})

\displaystyle=0,

(A.39)

\displaystyle\frac{\udo(y_{0})^{2}}{\epsilon}\Big(\nabla\phi(s_{0},y_{0})+\frac{2M_{u}}{\theta^{2}}\widetilde{z}-\delta\nabla\zeta_{\epsilon}(s_{0},y_{0})\Big)

\displaystyle\in\partial\udo(y_{0}),

for the vector
\widetilde{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
defined by

\widetilde{z}_{k}=\frac{(y_{0}-x_{0})_{k}}{d((y_{0}-x_{0})_{k}^{2}+\epsilon_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}}=-z_{k}.

Remembering that
\partial\udo(y_{0})\subset(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d})^{*}=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
by
Lemma

A.10
, it is possible to find
p\geq 0
with

\frac{2M_{u}}{\theta^{2}}z=\nabla\phi(s_{0},y_{0})-\delta\nabla\zeta_{\epsilon}(s_{0},y_{0})-p.

Substituting this and (
A.39
) into (
A.38
) and using the fact that the non-linearity
\mathsf{H}
is non-decreasing reveals that

\partial_{t}\phi(s_{0},y_{0})-\delta\partial_{t}\zeta_{\epsilon}(s_{0},y_{0})-\mathsf{H}\big(\nabla\phi(s_{0},y_{0})-\delta\nabla\zeta_{\epsilon}(s_{0},y_{0})\big)\leq 0.

(A.40)

Recalling that
(s_{0},y_{0})\in\supp\zeta_{\epsilon}
depends on
\epsilon,\delta
and
\theta
, and that
\theta
was chosen small enough in terms of
\delta
, we would now like to let
\theta\to 0
and then
\delta\to 0
in this inequality. Observe that for any
(t,x)\in\overline{\BigO}_{r}
,

\displaystyle u(t_{0},x_{0})-\phi(s_{0},y_{0})-\frac{\epsilon}{\udo(y_{0})}+\delta\zeta_{\epsilon}(s_{0},y_{0})

\displaystyle\geq\Psi_{\epsilon,\delta,\theta}(t_{0},x_{0},s_{0},y_{0})

\displaystyle\geq u(t,x)-\phi(t,x)-\frac{\epsilon}{\udo(x)}+\delta\zeta_{\epsilon}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\psi_{\epsilon}(t,x)+\delta\zeta_{\epsilon}(t,x).

If we denote by
(t_{1},x_{1})\in\supp\zeta_{\epsilon}\subset\BigO_{r}
and
(t_{1}^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime})\in\supp\zeta_{\epsilon}\subset\BigO_{r}
subsequential limits of the sequences
(t_{0},x_{0})
and
(s_{0},y_{0})
as
\theta\to 0
and then
\delta\to 0
, we must have
t_{1}=t_{1}^{\prime}
and
x_{1}=x_{1}^{\prime}
by (
A.37
). Moreover, the subsequential limit
(t_{1},x_{1})
must satisfy the inequality

u(t_{1},x_{1})-\phi(t_{1},x_{1})\geq u(t_{1},x_{1})-\phi(t_{1},x_{1})-\frac{\epsilon}{\udo(x_{1})}\geq u(t,x)-\phi(t,x)-\frac{\epsilon}{\udo(x)}

for all
(t,x)\in\BigO_{r}
. Writing
(t_{2},x_{2})\in\overline{\BigO}_{r}
for a subsequential limit of the sequence
(t_{1},x_{1})
as
\epsilon\to 0
we find that

u(t_{2},x_{2})-\phi(t_{2},x_{2})\geq u(t,x)-\phi(t,x)

for all
(t,x)\in\BigO_{r}
. By continuity of
u-\phi
, this inequality extends to
\overline{\BigO}_{r}
. Since
(t^{*},x^{*})
is a strict local maximum of
u-\phi
on
\overline{\BigO}_{r}
, we must have
(t_{2},x_{2})=(t^{*},x^{*})
. It follows by letting
\theta\to 0
, then
\delta\to 0
and finally
\epsilon\to 0
in (
A.40
) that

\partial_{t}\phi(t^{*},x^{*})-\mathsf{H}\big(\nabla\phi(t^{*},x^{*})\big)\leq 0.

This completes the proof.
∎

Corollary A.12
.

If
\smash{\mathsf{H}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}}
and
\smash{\psi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}}
satisfy (
A1
)-(
A3
), then the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.4
) admits a unique viscosity solution
f\in\mathfrak{L}
subject to the initial condition
\psi
. Moreover,

\sup_{t>0}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|f(t,\cdot)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1},

(A.41)

and if
u,v\in\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{unif}}
are respectively a continuous subsolution and a continuous supersolution to (
A.4
), then

\sup_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\big(u(t,x)-v(t,x)\big)=\sup_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\big(u(0,x)-v(0,x)\big).

(A.42)

To be more specific, if
\smash{L=\max\big(\sup_{t>0}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|u(t,\cdot)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1},\sup_{t>0}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|v(t,\cdot)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}\big)}
and
\smash{V={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\mathsf{H}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1,*}}
, then for every
Q>2L
and
R\in\mathbb{R}
, the map

(t,x)\mapsto u(t,x)-v(t,x)-Q\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}+Vt-R\big)_{+}

(A.43)

achieves its supremum on
\smash{\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of
Proposition

A.9
,
Proposition

A.1
,
Corollary

A.2
and
Proposition

A.11
.
∎

A.4
Monotonicity of solutions on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}

Recall that the notion of being
\mathcal{C}^{*}
-non-decreasing is introduced at the beginning of Section
2
. In this section, we follow the arguments in Section 4 of
[
13
]
to show that the solution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
) preserves the monotonicity of its initial condition. To be more specific, we assume that

A4

the initial condition
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is
\mathcal{C}^{*}
-non-decreasing for some closed convex cone
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

and under a mild assumption on the dual cone
\mathcal{C}^{*}
, we show that the solution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
) constructed in
Proposition

A.9
is also
\mathcal{C}^{*}
-non-decreasing. This result will be used in
Section

2
when the well-posedness of the projected Hamilton-Jacobi equations (
1.37
) is established by means of
Proposition

A.9
. Indeed, it will allow us to verify the first condition in (
2.14
) and (
2.15
).

Proposition A.13
.

Fix a non-linearity
\mathsf{H}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and an initial condition
\smash{\psi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}}
satisfying (
A1
)-(
A4
). If
\smash{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\cap\mathrm{int}(\mathcal{C}^{*})\neq\emptyset}
and
f\in\mathfrak{L}
is a viscosity solution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
) subject to the initial condition
\psi
, then
f
is
\smash{\mathcal{C}^{*}}
-non-decreasing.

Proof.

Introduce the set

\Omega=\big\{(x,x^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\mid x^{\prime}-x\in\mathcal{C}^{*}\big\},

and suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists
T>0
with

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in[0,T]\\
(x,x^{\prime})\in\Omega\end{subarray}}\big(f(t,x)-f(t,x^{\prime})\big)>0\geq\sup_{(x,x^{\prime})\in\Omega}\big(f(0,x)-f(0,x^{\prime})\big).

(A.44)

The proof proceeds in three steps: first we perturb (
A.44
), then we use a variable doubling argument to obtain a system of inequalities, and finally we contradict this system of inequalities.

Step 1: perturbing.

Let
V={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\mathsf{H}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1,*}
and fix a constant
L>0
with

L>{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\psi|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}\quad\text{and}\quad\lvert f(t,x)-\psi(x)\rvert\leq Lt

for all
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
. The existence of such a constant follows from the assumption
f\in\mathfrak{L}
. Denote by
\smash{\udo:\mathcal{C}^{*}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}
the distance-like function (
A.34
) associated with the cone
\mathcal{C}
,

\udo(y)=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}{|\kern-0.3014pt|\kern-0.3014pt|y^{\prime}|\kern-0.3014pt|\kern-0.3014pt|}_{1,*}=1\\
y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}\end{subarray}}y^{\prime}\cdot y.

Fix
\smash{y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\cap\mathrm{int}(\mathcal{C}^{*})}
as well as
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
, and let
\theta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be an increasing function with
r_{+}\leq\theta(r)\leq(r+1)_{+}
for all
r\in\mathbb{R}
. Given
0<\epsilon_{0}<1
to be determined, consider the smoothed normalized-
\ell^{1}
norm,

{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\big(x_{k}^{2}+\epsilon_{0}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}},

and introduce the function

\Phi(t,x)=\theta\big({|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}+Vt-R\big)

defined on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
R>0
is chosen large enough so that
\Phi(0,x_{0})=0
. Increasing
R>0
if necessary, it is possible to perturb the inequality (
A.44
) to ensure that

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in[0,T]\\
(x,x^{\prime})\in\Omega\end{subarray}}\big(f(t,x)-f(t,x^{\prime})-\Phi(t,x)\big)>0\geq\sup_{(x,x^{\prime})\in\Omega}\big(f(0,x)-f(0,x^{\prime})-\Phi(0,x)\big).

Picking
\delta>0
small enough, it is also possible to guarantee that

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in[0,T]\\
(x,x^{\prime})\in\Omega\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\Big(f(t,x)-f(t,x^{\prime})-\delta t-\zeta(t,t)-\Phi(t,x)-\frac{\delta}{\udo(x^{\prime}-x)}-2\delta{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle>\sup_{(x,x^{\prime})\in\Omega}\Big(f(0,x)-f(0,x^{\prime})-\zeta(0,0)-\Phi(0,x)-\frac{\delta}{\udo(x^{\prime}-x)}-2\delta{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}^{2}\Big)

(A.45)

for the perturbation function

\zeta(t,t^{\prime})=\frac{\delta}{T-t}+\frac{\delta}{T-t^{\prime}}.

This is a perturbed version of the absurd hypothesis (
A.44
).

Step 2: system of inequalities.

For each
\alpha\geq 1
, define the function
\Psi_{\alpha}:[0,T]\times[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathcal{C}^{*}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
by

\displaystyle\Psi_{\alpha}(t,t^{\prime},x,x^{\prime},y)=f(t,x)-f(t^{\prime},x^{\prime})-

\displaystyle\Phi(t,x)-\psi_{\alpha}(x,x^{\prime},y)

\displaystyle-\delta t-\zeta(t,t^{\prime})-\alpha\lvert t-t^{\prime}\rvert^{2}-\delta{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}^{2},

(A.46)

where

\psi_{\alpha}(x,x^{\prime},y)=\alpha{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x^{\prime}-x-y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}^{2}+\frac{\delta}{\udo(y)}+\delta{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}^{2}.

Observe that
\Psi_{\alpha}(t,t,x,x^{\prime},x^{\prime}-x)
coincides with the function being maximized in (
A.4
). By doubling the variables in this way, we ensure that the function
\Psi_{\alpha}
achieves its supremum at a point
(t_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}^{\prime},y_{\alpha})
which remains bounded as
\alpha
tends to infinity. Indeed, if we temporarily fix
\alpha\geq 1
and let
\smash{(t_{\alpha,n},t_{\alpha,n}^{\prime},x_{\alpha,n},x_{\alpha,n}^{\prime},y_{\alpha,n})}
be a maximizing sequence for
\Psi_{\alpha}
, then the choice of
x_{0}
implies that when
n
is large enough,

\Psi_{\alpha}(t_{\alpha,n},t_{\alpha,n}^{\prime},x_{\alpha,n},x_{\alpha,n}^{\prime},y_{\alpha,n})\geq\Psi_{\alpha}(0,0,x_{0},x_{0}+y_{0},y_{0})=C_{0}

(A.47)

for the constant
\smash{C_{0}=f(0,x_{0})-f(0,x_{0}+y_{0})-\zeta(0,0)-\frac{\delta}{\udo(y_{0})}-2\delta\lVert y_{0}\rVert_{1,\epsilon_{0}}^{2}}
. We have used the fact that
\smash{y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
and that
\smash{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
is a cone. Combining this with the Lipschitz bound

\lvert f(t,x)-f(t^{\prime},x^{\prime})\rvert\leq L(t+t^{\prime})+L{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq L(t+t^{\prime})+L{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}

(A.48)

and the fact that
\Phi(t,x)\geq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}-R
reveals that

\displaystyle Lt_{\alpha,n}+Lt_{\alpha,n}^{\prime}+L{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{\alpha,n}-x_{\alpha,n}^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}+R-

\displaystyle{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{\alpha,n}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}

\displaystyle-\delta{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y_{\alpha,n}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}^{2}-\delta{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{\alpha,n}-x_{\alpha,n}^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}^{2}\geq C_{0}.

(A.49)

Noticing that
t_{\alpha,n},t_{\alpha,n}^{\prime}<T
due to the presence of
\zeta
in the function
\Psi_{\alpha}
and observing that the quadratic function
\smash{r\mapsto Lr-\delta r^{2}}
is bounded by
\smash{\frac{L^{2}}{4\delta}}
gives the uniform boundedness of
x_{\alpha,n}
and
y_{\alpha,n}
in both
n
and
\alpha
with respect to the normalized-
\smash{\ell^{1}}
norm. Rearranging the lower bound (
A.4
) also shows that

2LT+L{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{\alpha,n}-x_{\alpha,n}^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}+R-C_{0}\geq\delta\lVert x_{\alpha,n}-x_{\alpha,n}^{\prime}\rVert_{1,\epsilon_{0}}^{2}

which gives the uniform boundedness of
x_{\alpha,n}-x^{\prime}_{\alpha,n}
and hence
x_{\alpha,n}^{\prime}
in both
n
and
\alpha
with respect to the normalized-
\smash{\ell^{1}}
norm. It is therefore possible to let
n
tend to infinity along a subsequence to obtain a maximizer
(t_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}^{\prime},y_{\alpha})
of
\Psi_{\alpha}
all of whose components are bounded by some constant
C_{1}>0
that is independent of
\alpha
with respect to the normalized-
\smash{\ell^{1}}
norm. Choosing
\epsilon_{0}
small enough, these components will also be assumed to be bounded by
C_{1}>0
with respect to the smoothed version of the normalized-
\smash{\ell^{1}}
norm. We now obtain some essential bounds on the components of this maximizer. Taking the limit as
n
tends to infinity in the inequality (
A.47
) reveals that

f(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})-f(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})-\frac{\delta}{\udo(y_{\alpha})}-\delta{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y_{\alpha}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}^{2}-\alpha\lvert t_{\alpha}-t_{\alpha}^{\prime}\rvert^{2}-\delta{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{\alpha}-x_{\alpha}^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}^{2}\geq C_{0}.

Combining this with (
A.48
) gives

\displaystyle\alpha\lvert t_{\alpha}-t_{\alpha}^{\prime}\rvert^{2}+\frac{\delta}{\udo(y_{\alpha})}+\delta{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y_{\alpha}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq L(t_{\alpha}+t_{\alpha}^{\prime})+L{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{\alpha}-x_{\alpha}^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}-\delta{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{\alpha}-x_{\alpha}^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}^{2}-C_{0}

\displaystyle\leq 2LT+\frac{L^{2}}{4\delta}-C_{0},

where we again used the fact that the quadratic function
\smash{r\mapsto Lr-\delta r^{2}}
is bounded by
\smash{\frac{L^{2}}{4\delta}}
. If we introduce the constant
\smash{C_{2}=\max\big(2LT+\frac{L^{2}}{4\delta}-C_{0},1\big)}
, this upper bound implies that

\lvert t_{\alpha}-t_{\alpha}^{\prime}\rvert\leq\sqrt{\frac{C_{2}}{\alpha}},\qquad\udo(y_{\alpha})\geq\frac{\delta}{C_{2}},\qquad\lVert y_{\alpha}\rVert_{1,\epsilon_{0}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{C_{2}}{\delta}}.

(A.50)

In particular
y_{\alpha}\in\mathrm{int}(\mathcal{C}^{*})
, so
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|p|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq 1
for every
p\in\partial\udo(y_{\alpha})
by
Lemma

A.10
. To leverage this observation, notice that
y\mapsto\Psi_{\alpha}(t_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}^{\prime},y)
achieves a local maximum at
y_{\alpha}
, and therefore so does
y\mapsto-\psi_{\alpha}(x_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}^{\prime},y)
. It follows by a direct computation and
Lemma

A.10
that the vector
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
defined by

p_{k}=\frac{2\udo(y_{\alpha})^{2}}{\delta}\bigg(\alpha{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{\alpha}^{\prime}-x_{\alpha}-y_{\alpha}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}\frac{(x_{\alpha}^{\prime}-x_{\alpha}-y_{\alpha})_{k}}{((x_{\alpha}^{\prime}-x_{\alpha}-y_{\alpha})_{k}^{2}+\epsilon_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\delta{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y_{\alpha}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}\frac{(y_{\alpha})_{k}}{((y_{\alpha})_{k}^{2}+\epsilon_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\bigg)

belongs to the super-differential
\partial\udo(y_{\alpha})
. This means that

\alpha{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{\alpha}^{\prime}-x_{\alpha}-y_{\alpha}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\bigg\lvert\frac{(x_{\alpha}^{\prime}-x_{\alpha}-y_{\alpha})_{k}}{((x_{\alpha}^{\prime}-x_{\alpha}-y_{\alpha})_{k}^{2}+\epsilon_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\bigg\rvert\leq\frac{\delta}{2d\udo(y_{\alpha})^{2}}+\delta{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y_{\alpha}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}},

where we have used the bounds
{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|p|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq 1
and
\smash{\lvert(y_{\alpha})_{k}\rvert\leq((y_{\alpha})_{k}^{2}+\epsilon_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}}
. To bound this further, suppose that

{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{\alpha}^{\prime}-x_{\alpha}-y_{\alpha}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}>C

(A.51)

for some constant
C
to be determined, and let
1\leq k^{*}\leq d
be such that
\smash{((x_{\alpha}^{\prime}-x_{\alpha}-y_{\alpha})_{k^{*}}^{2}+\epsilon_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}>C}
. Observe that for any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\smash{(z_{k^{*}}^{2}+\epsilon_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}>C}
,

\frac{\lvert z_{k^{*}}\rvert}{(z_{k^{*}}^{2}+\epsilon_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}}=\frac{\lvert z_{k^{*}}\rvert+\sqrt{\epsilon_{0}}}{(z_{k^{*}}^{2}+\epsilon_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}}-\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon_{0}}}{(z_{k^{*}}^{2}+\epsilon_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\geq 1-\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon_{0}}}{C}=\frac{C-\sqrt{\epsilon_{0}}}{C},

where we have used the fact that
\lvert z_{k}\rvert+\sqrt{\epsilon_{0}}\geq(z_{k}^{2}+\epsilon_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}
. Together with (
A.50
), this implies that

\frac{\alpha(C-\sqrt{\epsilon_{0}})}{C}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{\alpha}^{\prime}-x_{\alpha}-y_{\alpha}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}\leq\frac{\delta}{2d\udo(y_{\alpha})^{2}}+\delta{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y_{\alpha}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}\leq K

for the constant
\smash{K=\frac{C_{2}^{2}}{2d\delta}+\sqrt{\delta C_{2}}}
. Rearranging, remembering (
A.51
) and choosing
\smash{C=\sqrt{\epsilon_{0}}+\frac{K}{\alpha}}
reveals that

{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{\alpha}^{\prime}-x_{\alpha}-y_{\alpha}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{\alpha}^{\prime}-x_{\alpha}-y_{\alpha}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}\leq\max\Big(C,\frac{KC}{\alpha(C-\sqrt{\epsilon_{0}})}\Big)=\frac{K}{\alpha}+\sqrt{\epsilon_{0}}.

Letting
\epsilon_{0}
tend to zero in this upper bound yields

{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{\alpha}^{\prime}-x_{\alpha}-y_{\alpha}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}\leq\frac{K}{\alpha}.

(A.52)

Combining this with the first bound in (
A.50
) and the fact that each component in the sequence of maximizers
(t_{\alpha},t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}^{\prime},y_{\alpha})
is uniformly bounded by a constant independent of
\alpha
gives the existence of a subsequential limit
(t_{\infty},t_{\infty},x_{\infty},x_{\infty}^{\prime},x_{\infty}^{\prime}-x_{\infty})
with respect to the normalized-
\ell^{1}
norm. Observe that for any
t\in[0,T)
and every
(x,x^{\prime})\in\Omega
,

\displaystyle f(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})-f(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})-\delta t_{\alpha}-\zeta(t_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{\prime})-\Phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})

\displaystyle-\frac{\delta}{\udo(y_{\alpha})}-\delta{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y_{\alpha}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}^{2}-\delta{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x_{\alpha}-x_{\alpha}^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,\epsilon_{0}}

\displaystyle\geq\Psi_{\alpha}(t_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}^{\prime},y_{\alpha})\geq\Psi_{\alpha}(t,t,x,x^{\prime},x^{\prime}-x).

Taking the supremum over
(t,x,x^{\prime})\in[0,T]\times\Omega
, recalling that
\Psi_{\alpha}(t,t,x,x^{\prime},x^{\prime}-x)
coincides with the function being maximized in (
A.4
) and letting
\alpha\to\infty
shows that
t_{\infty}>0
. At this point, we can use the fact that
f
is a viscosity solution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
A.5
) to obtain a system of inequalities. Using the second inequality in (
A.50
), the bound (
A.52
) and the observation that
t_{\infty}>0
, fix
\alpha\geq 1
large enough so that
\smash{x_{\alpha}^{\prime}-x_{\alpha}\in\mathrm{int}(\mathcal{C}^{*})}
and
\smash{t_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{\prime}>0}
. Introduce the smooth functions

\phi(t,x)=f(t,x)-\Psi_{\alpha}(t,t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x,x_{\alpha}^{\prime},y_{\alpha})\quad\text{and}\quad\phi^{\prime}(t^{\prime},x^{\prime})=f(t^{\prime},x^{\prime})+\Psi_{\alpha}(t_{\alpha},t^{\prime},x_{\alpha},x^{\prime},y_{\alpha})

defined on
(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}
. Since
(t_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}^{\prime},y_{\alpha})
maximizes
\Psi_{\alpha}
, the function
f-\phi
achieves a local maximum at
\smash{(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
while the function
f-\phi^{\prime}
achieves a local minimum at
\smash{(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
. It follows by definition of a viscosity solution that

\partial_{t}\phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})-\mathsf{H}\big(\nabla\phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})\big)\leq 0\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{t}\phi^{\prime}(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})-\mathsf{H}\big(\nabla\phi^{\prime}(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})\big)\geq 0.

(A.53)

This is the system of inequalities that we now strive to contradict.

Step 3: reaching a contradiction.

The choice
V={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\mathsf{H}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1,*}
and a direct computation reveal that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\phi^{\prime}(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})-\mathsf{H}\big(\nabla\phi^{\prime}(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})\big)

\displaystyle<\delta+\frac{\delta}{(T-t_{\alpha})^{2}}+V{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\nabla\Phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}+2\alpha(t_{\alpha}-t_{\alpha}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\mathsf{H}\big(\nabla\phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})\big).

Another direct computation shows that

\partial_{t}\phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})=\delta+\frac{\delta}{(T-t_{\alpha})^{2}}+\partial_{t}\Phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})+2\alpha(t_{\alpha}-t_{\alpha}^{\prime})

and that
dV\lvert\partial_{x_{k}}\Phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})\rvert\leq\partial_{t}\Phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})
. It follows by the first inequality in (
A.53
) that

\partial_{t}\phi^{\prime}(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})-\mathsf{H}\big(\nabla\phi^{\prime}(t_{\alpha}^{\prime},x_{\alpha}^{\prime})\big)<\partial_{t}\phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})-\mathsf{H}\big(\nabla\phi(t_{\alpha},x_{\alpha})\big)\leq 0

which contradicts the second inequality in (
A.53
) and completes the proof.
∎

Appendix B
Background material

In this appendix, we establish three elementary results in analysis. The first is a classical result in convex analysis regarding the bidual of a closed convex cone. Recall that the dual of a convex cone
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the closed convex cone

\mathcal{K}^{*}=\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid x\cdot y\geq 0\text{ for all }y\in\mathcal{K}\big\}.

(B.1)

It is clear that any convex cone
\mathcal{K}
is always a subset of its bidual
\mathcal{K}^{**}
. Since
\smash{\mathcal{K}^{**}}
is closed, a necessary condition for this containment to be an equality is that
\mathcal{K}
be closed; it turns out that this is also a sufficient condition. This is often deduced from the Hahn-Banach separation theorem
[
25
]
or the Fenchel-Moreau theorem
[
3
]
. For the reader’s convenience we prove this duality result using the Hahn-Banach separation theorem as stated in Theorem 4.1.1 of
[
25
]
.

Proposition B.1
.

If
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a non-empty closed convex cone, then
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}^{**}
.

Proof.

It is clear that
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{K}^{**}
. Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists
x\in\mathcal{K}^{**}
with
x\notin\mathcal{K}
. Since
\mathcal{K}
is a non-empty closed convex set, the Hahn-Banach separation theorem gives
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with

\alpha\cdot x>\sup\{\alpha\cdot y\mid y\in\mathcal{K}\}.

(B.2)

Given
x_{0}\in\mathcal{K}
, the assumption that
\mathcal{K}
is closed implies that
\smash{0=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}x_{0}\in\mathcal{K}}
. Together with (
B.2
), this means that
\alpha\cdot x>0
. If there were
y_{0}\in\mathcal{K}
with
\alpha\cdot y>0
, the fact that
\mathcal{K}
is a cone would imply that
\alpha\cdot x\geq\lambda\alpha\cdot y_{0}
for all
\lambda>0
, and letting
\lambda
tend to infinity would give a contradiction. It follows by (
B.2
) that

\alpha\cdot x>0=\sup\{\alpha\cdot y\mid y\in\mathcal{K}\},

where we have used that
0\in\mathcal{K}
. The lower bound implies that
-\alpha\in\mathcal{K}^{*}
while the upper bound gives
x\cdot(-\alpha)<0
. This contradicts the assumption that
\smash{x\in\mathcal{K}^{**}}
and completes the proof.
∎

The second also belongs to the realm of convex analysis, and it gives a non-differential characterization of a Lipschitz function having its gradient in a closed convex set.

Proposition B.2
.

If
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a closed convex set and
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is a Lipschitz function, then
\nabla\psi\in\mathcal{K}
if and only if the following holds. For every
c\in\mathbb{R}
and
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with the property that for every
z\in\mathcal{K}
,
(x^{\prime}-x)\cdot z\geq c
, we have
\psi(x^{\prime})-\psi(x)\geq c
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\nabla\psi\in\mathcal{K}
, and fix
c\in\mathbb{R}
and
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
(x^{\prime}-x)\cdot z\geq c
for every
z\in\mathcal{K}
. If we knew that
\psi
was almost everywhere differentiable along the line joining
x
and
x^{\prime}
, we could apply the fundamental theorem of calculus to the one-dimensional Lipschitz function
t\mapsto\psi(x+t(x^{\prime}-x))
and conclude that

\psi(x^{\prime})-\psi(x)=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla\psi\big(x+t(x^{\prime}-x)\big)\cdot(x^{\prime}-x)\,\mathrm{d}t\geq c.

Although
\psi
could fail to be differentiable almost everywhere on the line joining
x
and
x^{\prime}
, we will now fix
\epsilon>0
and show that it must be differentiable almost everywhere on some line joining some point
\smash{x_{\epsilon}\in B_{\epsilon}(x)}
and some point
\smash{x^{\prime}_{\epsilon}\in B_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime})}
. Denote by

\mathcal{H}=\big\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid y\cdot(x^{\prime}-x)=0\big\}\cong\mathbb{R}^{d-1}

the hyperplane perpendicular to the line segment joining
x
and
x^{\prime}
, and write

\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon,x}=B_{\epsilon}(x)\cap(x+\mathcal{H})

for the cross-section of
B_{\epsilon}(x)
through
x
and perpendicular to the line segment joining
x
and
x^{\prime}
. Denote by
\mathcal{\mathcal{L}}
the set of line segments between points in
\smash{\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon,x}}
and points in
\smash{\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon,x^{\prime}}}
which are parallel to the line segment joining
x
and
x^{\prime}
. For each
\smash{y\in\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon,x}}
, write
\smash{\ell_{y}\in\mathcal{L}}
for the unique line segment in
\smash{\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon,x}}
through
y
, and introduce the set

\mathcal{D}_{y}=\big\{z\in\ell_{y}\mid\psi\text{ is not differentiable at }z\big\}

of points on
\ell_{y}
at which
\psi
is not differentiable. If
\mathcal{D}_{y}
were a set of positive one-dimensional Lebesgue measure
\smash{m_{1}(\mathcal{D}_{y})>0}
for every
y\in\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon,x}
, then the
d
-dimensional Lebesgue measure of the set of points in
\cup_{y\in\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon,x}}\ell_{y}
at which
\psi
is not differentiable would have positive measure,

\int_{\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon,x}}m_{1}(\mathcal{D}_{y})\,\mathrm{d}y>0.

This would contradict Rademacher’s theorem on the almost everywhere differentiability of Lipschitz functions (see Theorem 6 in Chapter 5.8 of
[
20
]
). It is therefore possible to find
\smash{x_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon,x}}
with
\smash{m_{1}(\mathcal{D}_{x_{\epsilon}})=0}
. If we write
\smash{x_{\epsilon}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon,x^{\prime}}}
for the right endpoint of
\smash{\ell_{x_{\epsilon}}}
, then the fundamental theorem of calculus implies that

\psi(x^{\prime}_{\epsilon})-\psi(x_{\epsilon})=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla\psi\big(x_{\epsilon}+t(x^{\prime}_{\epsilon}-x_{\epsilon})\big)\cdot(x^{\prime}-x)\,\mathrm{d}t\geq c.

Letting
\epsilon
tend to zero shows that
\psi(x^{\prime})-\psi(x)\geq c
as required. Conversely, suppose that for every
c\in\mathbb{R}
and
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with the property that for every
z\in\mathcal{K}
,
(x^{\prime}-x)\cdot z\geq c
, we have
\psi(x^{\prime})-\psi(x)\geq c
. Assume for the sake of contradiction that there exists
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\nabla\psi(y)\notin\mathcal{K}
. The Hahn-Banach separation theorem gives
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\delta>0
with

v\cdot\nabla\psi(y)+\delta<\inf\{v\cdot z\mid z\in\mathcal{K}\}.

It follows that

\psi(y+\epsilon v)-\psi(y)\geq\epsilon\big(v\cdot\nabla\psi(y)+\delta\big).

Dividing by
\epsilon
and letting
\epsilon
tend to zero reveals that
\nabla\psi(y)\cdot v\geq v\cdot\nabla\psi(y)+\delta
.
This contradiction completes the proof.
∎

The third elementary result in analysis that we will prove regards the basic properties of semi-continuous envelopes. To strive for generality, fix a set
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
endowed with a norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert
. Recall that a function
u:X\to\mathbb{R}
is said to be
upper semi-continuous
at a point
x\in X
if

u(x)\geq\limsup_{y\to x}u(y):=\lim_{r\searrow 0}\sup\big\{u(y)\mid y\in X\text{ with }\lVert y-x\rVert\leq r\big\},

(B.3)

and it is said to be
lower semi-continuous
at a point
x\in X
if

u(x)\leq\liminf_{y\to x}u(y):=\lim_{r\searrow 0}\inf\big\{u(y)\mid y\in X\text{ with }\lVert y-x\rVert\leq r\big\}.

(B.4)

Moreover, the
upper semi-continuous envelope
of
u
is the function
u^{\star}:X\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

u^{\star}(x)=\limsup_{y\to x}u(y)=\lim_{r\searrow 0}\sup\big\{u(y)\mid y\in X\text{ with }\lVert y-x\rVert\leq r\big\},

(B.5)

while its
lower semi-continuous envelope
is the function
u_{\star}:X\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

u_{\star}(x)=\liminf_{y\to x}u(y)=\lim_{r\searrow 0}\inf\big\{u(y)\mid y\in X\text{ with }\lVert y-x\rVert\leq r\big\}.

(B.6)

The following proposition collects the basic properties of semi-continuous envelopes. This result is used in
Section

A.2
with
\smash{X=[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
and
\smash{\lVert(t,x)\rVert=\lvert t\rvert+{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}}
.

Proposition B.3
.

The semi-continuous envelopes of a locally bounded function
u:X\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy the following basic properties.

1.

u_{\star}(x)\leq u(x)\leq u^{\star}(x)
for all
x\in X
.

2.

u^{\star}(x)=\min\{v(x)\mid u\leq v\text{ and }v\text{ is upper semi-continuous}\}
for all
x\in X
. In particular,
u^{\star}
is upper semi-continuous.

3.

u_{\star}(x)=\max\{v(x)\mid v\leq u\text{ and }v\text{ is lower semi-continuous}\}
for all
x\in X
. In particular,
u_{\star}
is lower semi-continuous.

4.

u
is upper semi-continuous at
x\in X
if and only if
u(x)=u^{\star}(x)
.

5.

u
is lower semi-continuous at
x\in X
if and only if
u(x)=u_{\star}(x)
.

Proof.

To deduce properties of the lower semi-continuous envelope from the corresponding properties of the upper semi-continuous envelope we will leverage the observation that

\displaystyle u_{\star}(x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\searrow 0}\inf\big\{u(y)\mid y\in X\text{ with }\lVert y-x\rVert\leq r\big\}

\displaystyle=-\lim_{r\searrow 0}\sup\big\{-u(y)\mid y\in X\text{ with }\lVert y-x\rVert\leq r\big\}=-(-u)^{\star}(x).

(B.7)

1.

This is immediate from the definition of the semi-continuous envelopes in (
B.5
) and (
B.6
).

2.

If
v
is an upper semi-continuous function with
u\leq v
, taking the limsup as
y
tends to
x
on both sides of the inequality
u(y)\leq v(y)
and leveraging the upper semi-continuity of
v
reveals that

u^{\star}(x)=\limsup_{y\to x}u(y)\leq\limsup_{y\to x}v(y)\leq v(x).

This implies that

u^{\star}(x)\leq\inf\{v(x)\mid u\leq v\text{ and }v\text{ is upper semi-continuous}\}.

To show that this infimum is achieved and that this inequality is in fact an equality, it suffices to prove that
u^{\star}
is itself upper semi-continuous. Fix
x\in X
as well as
\epsilon>0
, and find
r>0
with

u^{\star}(x)+\epsilon>\sup\big\{u(y)\mid y\in X\text{ with }\lVert y-x\rVert\leq r\big\}.

The triangle inequality reveals that for any
z\in X
with
\lVert z-x\rVert<r
,

u^{\star}(x)+\epsilon\geq\sup\big\{u(y)\mid y\in X\text{ with }\lVert y-z\rVert\leq r-\lVert x-z\rVert\big\}\geq u^{\star}(z).

It follows that
\smash{\limsup_{z\to x}u^{\star}(z)\leq u^{\star}(x)}
so
u^{\star}
is upper semi-continuous at
x
. Since
x
is arbitrary, this establishes the claim.

3.

Combining the previous part with (
B
) shows that

u_{\star}(x)=-(-u)^{\star}(x)=\max\big\{-v(x)\mid-u\leq v\text{ and }v\text{ is upper semi-continuous}\big\}.

Observing that
v
is upper semi-continuous if and only
-v
is lower semi-continuous establishes the claim.

4.

If
u
is upper semi-continuous at
x
, then

u^{\star}(x)=\limsup_{y\to x}u(y)\leq u(x).

Together with the inequality
u(x)\leq u^{\star}(x)
, this shows that
u(x)=u^{\star}(x)
. On the other hand, if
u^{\star}(x)=u(x)
, then

\limsup_{y\to x}u(y)=u^{\star}(x)=u(x)\leq u(x)

so
u
is upper semi-continuous at
x
.

5.

Observe that
u
is lower semi-continuous at
x\in X
if and only if
-u
is upper semi-continuous at
x\in X
. The previous part implies that this is the case if and only if
-u(x)=(-u)^{\star}(x)
. Invoking (
B
) completes the proof. ∎
